#3920. Wait, Daisy Edgar-Jones Is A Nepo Baby, And Big Brother Is Involved?

File this under things I would've never known.

As Daisy Edgar-Jones continues her run in headlines thanks to her latest role in Sense & Sensibility, I was shocked to learn she has ties to my favorite reality TV show. Yes, it appears the actress isn't just relevant to the 2026 movie schedule, as this nepo baby comes from a famous father connected to Big Brother.

As rumors swirl about BB having an all-star season, I'm blindsided by the news that one of Hollywood's biggest rising stars is connected to one of the best reality television shows. So, like, when does she enter the game as a Houseguest?

The actress recently revealed this during an appearance on Open Book With Jenna. Her father, Philip Edgar-Jones, is credited as a co-creator of the UK's Big Brother series, which first aired back in 2000. The UK version and American version kicked off around the same time, though the former flourished while the U.S. version struggled in its first season.

Daisy Edgar-Jones revealed that before she was working on the sets of book-to-screen adaptations, she was visiting her father on the set of the UK series. She even hung out in the diary room, which is a dream I've had myself for the past decade.

She credited her father and his experience with Big Brother for helping her navigate how to deal with fame before she truly attained it. After seeing a bunch of reality stars navigate celebrity, he stressed to her that while she may not feel different, her world may change, as will how people interact with her. That's interesting to think about, especially after witnessing how many Houseguests have to navigate fans prying into their personal lives.

Typically, actors and actresses of Daisy Edgar-Jones' status aren't tapped to enter Big Brother as Houseguests. Celebrity Big Brother typically targets actors, musicians, or athletes who are past their prime, so how cool would it be to grab a noteworthy actress for a future season?

Drew And Melody May Be Out Of The Big Brother House, But They're Still Spending Every Minute Together

Granted, I doubt we'd be able to gobble her up for the American version of Celebrity BB, which hasn't had an iteration since 2022. The finale ended up being so toxic between participants that I questioned whether any celebrity would sign up to be a part of it again.

The good news is that Daisy Edgar-Jones would know what she's getting into, so presumably, she wouldn't need to be briefed on what may pop up. Even so, it's probably a long shot that she'd commit to anything like that, especially now that her performance in Sense & Sensibility is getting buzz for Oscar nominations.

We can only hope she'd join, and if not, at least we have this fun factoid about how Edgar-Jones is connected to Big Brother. Catch up on the American version of the series with a Paramount+ subscription, and keep an eye out for details on an upcoming all-star season.

#3919. 'Vorssaint' Replaced at Least 10 Utility Apps on My Mac

Plenty of Mac apps do one job, and do it quite well. For example, KeyboardCleanTool lets you disable your Mac's keyboard temporarily while you get dust, food particles, and gunk off the keyboard. I love these apps, but if you keep installing utilities, things start to feel crowded; it can be a real problem even trying to remember that you have an app installed for a specific task. This is where Vorssaint comes in: It's the Swiss Army knife of Mac apps, packing over 75 utilities into a single, free resource.

The best thing is that you can choose which of these utilities you wish to enable—if you need only five of Vorssaint's features, you can disable the rest. This prevents the app from becoming a resource hog, which is particularly valuable for those of us with older, base-model MacBooks. Even though Vorssaint has replaced multiple Mac apps on my MacBook, however, it's not always better than the apps it replaces. If you use Vorssaint to set a custom volume level for each Mac app, for example, it doesn't mean Vorssaint can do everything SoundSource does.

Vorssaint is very much a jack of all trades, master of none, and that's perfectly fine for most people. If that sounds like the kind of tool you need, then Vorssaint could be great for you. Here are 10 Vorssaint features that I've used to replace apps on my MacBook:

Unless you're addicted to appearing like you're yelling all the time, you probably don't need the Caps Lock key at all. You can always hold Shift whenever you want to capitalize something, and in my opinion, Caps Lock should be repurposed for something better. Apps such as Raycast allow you to convert Caps Lock into a "hyper key," which means that when you press that button, your computer registers it as a combination of other modifier keys. On my MacBook, pressing Caps Lock equals holding Command-Control-Shift-Alt together, and I've used it to create keyboard shortcuts that don't conflict with those in macOS or third-party apps.

To enable this feature in Vorssaint, open the app, and go to Settings > Features. Go to the "Mouse and keyboard" section, and enable Super key. Vorssaint allows you to map modifier keys to Caps Lock. It also lets you continue using the button for capitalization, switching input sources, or as an alternative way to press the Esc key (useful for keyboards that don't have this button). It works by triggering the "Super key" only when you hold Caps Lock, and toggling the other feature you chose when you press the button once.

Your Mac's Spotlight has a basic clipboard manager, but Vorssaint adds a few more features. The app lets you clean tracking data from URLs, and even adds a "Cut and Paste" feature to your Mac's Finder. The latter is great for people switching from Windows—Finder's cut/paste feature isn't as seamless as the one on Microsoft's platform.

Once you enable these features in Vorssaint, you can cut any file in Finder by pressing Command-X, and paste it elsewhere with Command-V. The URL cleaner is customizable, and it allows you to define parameters to remove. It also allows you to create filters for specific websites, which reduces the chances of breaking URLs when you try this feature. By default, Vorssaint will automatically clean a link the moment you copy one, but you can also manually clean links in the app if you wish to.

The app's clipboard manager has all the basic features you'll need, and it lets you exclude some apps too. This lets you keep Vorssaint from storing clipboard items from sensitive apps. The shortcut to view clipboard history from Vorssaint is Command-Option-Alt-V.

I use Rapidmg, a popular app installer format on the Mac, to auto-mount and install DMG files. After the app is installed, Rapidmg also gets rid of the installer file. Vorssaint has this feature built-in, and you can enable it by going to Vorssaint settings > Features > Disk image installer. It's located under the "Clipboard and files" section.

If you're looking for an app to set a custom volume level for each Mac app, then Vorssaint has a tool for the job. It's good enough to replace basic volume control apps like FineTune, but not nearly as powerful as SoundSource. Vorssaint lets you set a per-app volume level, quickly switch between output devices, and even set priority for audio devices. It doesn't support sending audio to multiple devices at once, tweaking latency levels, boosting volume above the maximum, or sending audio from certain apps only to specific output devices (e.g., Spotify always goes to headphones, VLC always sends audio to speakers, etc.). If you value power-user features, then SoundSource has to be your go-to. For most people, Vorssaint is good enough to get the job done. You can enable volume mixer in the app by going to Vorssaint settings > Features > Volume mixer. It's under the "Sound" section in the app.

There are many good reasons to stop your Mac from going to sleep, such as connecting your MacBook to an external display and using the laptop with its lid closed. Apps such as Amphetamine allow you to keep your Mac from going into sleep mode, and Vorssaint has this feature built-in as well. You can use it by enabling the "Keep awake" feature in the app, which lets you disable sleep mode on your Mac for a custom time interval that ranges from 15 minutes to indefinitely.

If you're missing your iPhone's Dynamic Island on your MacBook, you could install one of several useful notch apps on your Mac. These apps show a now playing widget, brightness or volume levels, calendars, or even have a temporary shelf to help you drag and drop files on your Mac. Vorssaint ships with a notch app, too: Dynamic Island. Once you enable it, Vorssaint's Dynamic Island can show notifications, calendar events, timers, song lyrics, playback queue, etc. Note that this feature is labeled "Experimental" in the app, which means that it's not as stable as most other features. I've also been using it to activate other Vorssaint features such as Keep awake and volume mixer, since Dynamic Island shows toggles to control them.

iStat Menus is one of my favorite Mac apps—it allows me to monitor my MacBook's current load and operating temperatures. Vorssaint also ships with the ability to monitor CPU load, GPU load, RAM usage, network usage, battery temperature, etc. It's not as elegant as iStat Menus, but it'll do the job for those who need a basic system stat monitor. In my tests, though, I observed that Vorssaint incorrectly reports CPU temperature. Two other apps (iStat Menus and TG Pro) reported my Mac's CPU temperature at around 104°F (40°C), but Vorssaint kept showing a number closer to 145°F (63°C). All other temperature readings were nearly identical in the three apps, which indicates that Vorssaint probably has a bug to fix.

Vorssaint can also be a substitute for apps that enhance your Mac's Dock. It allows you to enable features like previewing open windows by hovering over an app's icon in the Dock, quitting apps from the Dock preview, clicking the dock icon to minimize, hide, or cycle between apps' windows, etc. One useful feature allows you to click the green button to maximize any app's window, as opposed to switching to full-screen mode. I don't use it, but I can recommend it to people who switch from Windows and ask me about the maximize button.

macOS doesn't always allow your mouse to work the way you want it to. Third-party "mouse fix" apps add nice features such as smooth scrolling, customizable actions for side buttons, and so on. Vorssaint includes support for smooth scrolling, and a few other features. It lets you map extra mouse buttons to custom keyboard shortcuts, and even has a feature to prevent accidental double-clicks. The latter is good for people who have a mouse with double-click problems. You can enable these features under the "Mouse and keyboard" section in Vorssaint.

Apps like KeyboardCleanTool allow you to temporarily disable your Mac's keyboard for you to give it a good clean, or to allow your cat to sit on the keyboard without disturbing your workflow. Vorssaint supports this, too—you can enable this feature by toggling "Cleaning Mode" under the Tools section in the app. Once you lock the keyboard, you can either click the "Unlock" button or press Esc five times to start typing again.

#3918. As beloved Dishonored studio faces closure, Xbox agrees union request to extend negotiations deadline in hope of saving 217 jobs

Xbox has extended Arkane Lyon's consultation period by around a month, as the search for a new owner continues. If one isn't found, the studio will likely be shut down.

Arkane Lyon - the developer behind immersive sims including Dishonored and Deathloop - was one of several first-party studios impacted by Xbox CEO Asha Sharma's sweeping "reset", which will result in 3,200 job losses by the time the process is complete. Arkane's leadership was reportedly informed of Xbox's intention to close the studio on 16th July, but France's Florange Law meant a three-month search for a buyer must first be undertaken.

Last week, unions - Printemps Écologique and Syndicat des Travailleureuses du Jeu Vidéo - were said to be fighting to buy more time for negotiations. And now, as per a new report by Le Monde, Microsoft has agreed to extend the original 13th November deadline to December. If a buyer still isn't found, 217 jobs are at risk.

Documents published on the French government's website in August referenced a plan for "the complete and permanent cessation of the company's activities" and another for the "dismissal of all employees for economic reasons."

A Microsoft representative confirmed the deadline extension to PC Gamer, telling the outlet: "We have extended the consultation period by one additional month as we explore options, including finding a partner to support the studio through the development work still ahead." That "development work" refers to Marvel's Blade, which seems to still be under Arkane's purview even now.

"This is a defined extension, and we remain committed to keeping the Works Council informed throughout the process," Microsoft added. As for the filings referring to the closure of Arkane and the redundancy of its developers, the company spokesperson said they are a "part of the required consultation process and do not represent a final decision."

According to members of Printemps Ecologique (thanks VGC), Marvel's Blade is almost complete. The union says it would be a "monumental waste" for the game not to be released. However, a PC Gamer source also claimed Marvel's Blade - which has remained something of an enigma since its unveiling back in 2023 - still has years to go until it is finished.

Arkane's possible closure as part of Xbox's massive wave of cuts has already resulted in over 1,600 layoffs as restructuring continues. Sharma's "reset" has also included the jettisoning of beloved Psychonauts developer Double Fine, sweeping job cuts at Halo Studios as it transitions into a support studio, and job losses across various other Xbox-owned studios.

What's more, it seems Microsoft is far from done. According to a recent report, Xbox is planning three more waves of cuts:one this month, another in November, and a third in January.

#3917. WoW: Forever Just Got Another Major Racial Ability Shakeup Before Launch

World of Warcraft: Forever's release is just around the corner, but developer Blizzard is making one final pass at the game's racial abilities before launch in response to beta feedback.

In a forum post, WoW community manager Kaivax posted a list of significant changes to many of each race's unique abilities that players expressed concerns about during Forever's beta. Those changes include tweaks to tauren, humans, orcs, dwarves, and Skyborne elves.

"In taking a final pass on these, we've prioritized racial identity, which means that some races will naturally be slightly more effective than others at certain combat roles," Blizzard said. "In some places where we had replaced role or class-focused racials with generic ones, we're rolling those changes back."

The exception to that mindset is weapon-specialization abilities, Blizzard said, which existed for humans and orcs both in WoW Classic and early on in Forever's beta and made each race more effective with specific types of weapons.

"While those have a strong fantasy component to them, it is not healthy in the long run for future raid and dungeon loot tables to be evaluated through the lens of 'required' weapon types," Blizzard said. "At the same time, we're doing a tuning pass on some of the remaining passive bonuses to help ensure that they aren't skewing outcomes in a way that limits player preferences." 

Humans, widely viewed by the community as one of the most "optimal" races to pick thanks to their powerful passive abilities, have lost some of their power in combat. Ruthless now only grants 1% critical strike chance instead of 2%. In exchange, their Human Spirit passive has been buffed from 5% to 10%.

Tauren have seen some significant changes, with massive buffs to their unique Plainsrunning ability but a loss to some of their combat effectiveness. Plainsrunning will now grant 2% movement speed per stack up to a max of 60% increased movement speed, at the price of no longer increasing mounted movement speed and the loss of hit chance and crit damage from their Endurance passive. 

Orcs have seen their 1% crit chance removed and replaced with an earlier passive, Command. Now, orcs will deal 5% increased Warlock and Hunter pet damage. Dwarves have had their critical damage passive removed, instead replaced with a 1% chance to hit with attacks and spells.

Last but not least, Forever's newest race, Skyborne elves, have seen some major changes. Their haste bonus from Wind-Blessed has been reduced to 2% from 3%. They also now benefit more from using their Skysight and Read Leyline abilities at elemental convergence or leyline sites, with stronger effects that persist through death.

It's just the latest (and sounds like the last, at least for a while) batch of changes to Forever's various races. Blizzard said these changes, a little over a week before Forever's launch, were to address feedback players had during the beta about some abilities feeling "too generic," while others were "objectively stronger than others." You can read the full racial ablity changes below.

Blizzard looks to be making plenty of changes to Forever in the last stretch before release. It announced last week that it will be getting a new PvP-server option after all, and that the MMORPG's controversial layering system will see improvements in time for launch. WoW: Forever arrives November 4.

WoW: Forever Racial Ability Changes 10/26

  • Human
    • Ruthless now increases critical chance by 1% (was 2%).
    • Human Spirit now increases Spirit by 10% (was 5%).
  • Dwarf
    • Earth Fury (2% increased critical strike damage and healing) removed.
    • Added: Artisan’s Precision - Increases hit chance with spells and attacks by 1%.
  • Tauren
    • Plainsrunning now grants 2% movement speed per stack (was 1%), for a total max increase of 60% (was 30%).
    • Plainsrunning no longer increases mounted movement speed.
    • Endurance no longer provides any modifiers beyond 5% increased Health (no hit chance or critical damage modifiers).
  • Orc
    • Savage removed (1% increased critical strike chance).
    • Command re-added, increasing Warlock and Hunter pet damage by 5%.
    • Blood Fury’s cooldown reduced to 2 minutes (was 3 minutes).
  • Undead
    • Touch of the Grave internal cooldown reduced to 15 seconds (was 25 seconds).
  • Skyborne
    • Wind-Blessed Haste bonus reduced to 2% (was 3%).
    • Read Leyline now increases Mana and Health regeneration by 50% (was 100%), with a 15-second duration. This ability cannot be used while Innervate is active, and will be replaced by Innervate as a more powerful effect.
    • Using Read Leyline at a Ley Line now also grants “Attuned” for 1 hour. While Attuned, the benefit of Read Leyline is increased to 100% regeneration increase. Attuned persists through death.
    • Skysight is now a 15% increased movement speed (was 10%), with a 15-second duration.
    • Using Skysight at an elemental convergence now also grants “Charged” for 1 hour. While Charged, the benefit of Skysight is a 30% movement speed increase. Charged persists through death.
  • We’d also like to clarify that for Skyborne:
    • Charged persists through death, and is removed upon entering a raid.
    • Attuned persists through death, and is removed upon entering a raid.

It's been 22 years, 11 months, 28 days and counting since we were first graced with Call of Duty. Over those two decades the franchise has emerged as one of the most lucrative in videogame history, with Xbox CEO Asha Sharma announcing that it's passed the $40billion revenue mark.

Speaking of Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4's success, Sharma reflects: "One of the most celebrated Call of Duty games in its 24-year history, and now $40 billion in lifetime franchise revenue. An incredible milestone. Congrats to the Activision team. And to the players, thank you for being with us."

One of the most celebrated Call of Duty games in its 24-year history, and now $40B in lifetime franchise revenue. An incredible milestone. Congrats to the Activision team. And to the players, thank you for being with us. https://t.co/AlpXydEQIQOctober 26, 2026

$40 billion in lifetime revenue places Call of Duty behind goliaths like Mario and Pokémon. It also means that Call of Duty, on average, has earned $4,762,472 a day, which is particularly terrifying if you ask me.

But how it got there is an entirely different question—one that can be answered by the ravenous appetite of consumers in the free market. Call of Duty: Black Ops 3 still sits at the top spot with over 43 million copies sold, with Modern Warfare (2019) comes in a close second with over 41 million copies sold. But by and large, the popularity of Call of Duty is driven by Black Ops, namely its older titles.

The prize for most successful launch, however, has to go to Modern Warfare 2 (2022) which earned $1 billion in just 10 days alongside 200 million hours played. This followed Activision Blizzard's sale to Microsoft, which also meant that Modern Warfare 2 was the first time a Call of Duty game had been on Steam since 2017 with Call of Duty: WW2—it even made it into Steam's top 10 games by concurrent players.

The ridiculous release schedule of one Call of Duty game a year certainly seems to be working for Activision. I doubt it would've been able to pass this landmark without the tireless work of Infinity Ward, Treyarch, Sledgehammer Games, and Raven Software pumping out something new year after year. At this rate I'm pretty sure the Call of Duty franchise will outlive us all. Picture this: the dry arid landscape of an Earth pillaged and then left void of humanity, with the sole proof of our existence being the 15th Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 2 remaster (2193)—I hope it's good.

#3914. A leaked trailer for Transformers: Reactivate shows off the only lick of gameplay we'll likely ever see of the cancelled co-op action game

Back in 2022, Gears Tactics studio Splash Damage revealed that they were working on a Transformers game. It was called Transformers: Reactivate, and at the time was set to be a 1-4 player co-op action game, but come 2025 and the game gets canned all while parent company Tencent was going on a funding withdrawal spree in the games industry. Nothing substantial was ever really shown off of the game, but a trailer has now been leaked featuring what was apparently meant to be an internal look at it in action.

The trailer was shared by a Gwitter user called Nem who is devoted to "archiving and preserving Transformers: Reactivate because I need to cope somehow." Prior to the trailer itself, a message from Nem explained: "The following is an internal trailer not meant for the public, the music in it was released on October 21st 2024, placing this trailer at most three months prior to cancellation. With Tencent having pulled out in June/July 2024, this leads us to believe this trailer was made as part of an investment pitch to other publishers that ultimately failed."

Full credit for this discovery goes to @EnderGuyZV2!! TRANSFORMERS REACTIVATE GAMEPLAY TRAILER!!From around October-November 2024! This is our first glimpse at what the $70 game was hoping for, and hopefully we learn more about this version as time goes on. pic.twitter.com/52GhgTUrtz

Apparently by the time this trailer had been made, the game had shifted gears into a premium release, moving away from being a free-to-play live service game, complete with a story campaign that could be played solo or in online co-op. And the result is certainly a fairly real looking 2020s video game, at the very least. It's got some shooting, it's got some driving, it's got some shooting while driving, though it has surprisingly little actual transforming. Perhaps they were still ironing out the kinks mechanically and animationally.

This is one of those weird things where a big licensed game feels like it should have been a slam dunk, if only it had come out 10 years prior and wasn't catered towards the curse that is live service in the first place. I'm also always oddly eeked out by leaked trailers, not from some weird moral perspective, but because it's evidence of something existing, and yet is entirely impossible to engage with. What other medium has anything like this? Weird! I look forward to someone figuring out the solution to this. I'd suggest starting with dismantling capitalism, perhaps.

#3911. Six Things to Do Between Sets at the Gym

Rest is a crucial part of strength training. Depending on the exercise and your goals, you may find yourself resting 30 seconds, or one minute, or three to five minutes. So what do you actually do during that time? Let’s talk about your options, both in terms of training effectiveness and gym etiquette.

As awkward as it may feel at first, this is the best option for most people, most of the time. Actually rest. Don’t stretch, don’t work a different muscle; just allow your body to recover.

Some gyms will have stools, benches, or folding chairs near the platforms where people are lifting heavy (and thus need the longest rests). I’m mostly thinking of powerlifting and weightlifting gyms, but I’ve been to big-box gyms like Crunch that provide seating as well. And impromptu seats are common in many other places—who hasn’t sat on a plyo box or some other convenient surface?

There are plenty of little actions that you take at the gym (or should take) that are not explicitly exercise. The rest between sets is the perfect time to do these little maintenance tasks. Things like:

Change the weights to what you’ll need for your next set.

Write down what you just lifted in your notebook or lifting app.

If you took a video, watch it to give yourself feedback for the next set.

Trim that video so that you (or your coach, or your friends) don’t have to watch all 20 seconds of you fumbling with the camera and adjusting your belt before you actually lift.

Have a drink of water and, if needed, a snack.

Clean up after yourself! Rack any plates and dumbbells you aren’t using.

During the last few sets of an exercise, it’s time to start thinking about what you’ll do next. This could mean grabbing the equipment you’ll need or changing your shoes, but it's also an excellent opportunity to start warming up—especially if it’s an exercise that can benefit from a little gentle mobility work.

Let’s say you have a few sets of squats left, but after that you’ll do some overhead press. You probably want to warm up your shoulders with some mobility work so that you won’t be so tight when you hit that nice stable overhead position. Or maybe it’s the other way around, and you’re pressing, but next you’ll do squats. It’s a great time to start doing some ankle mobility work so that you’ll be able to comfortably hit a deeper squat.

When the weights are routine, I sit and chill. When they get heavy, I need to pace. (At weightlifting competitions, I not only stake out a warmup platform, I also pick out a walking path that I can pace between attempts.)

Or if you’re more of a cheerful, high-energy person, maybe you just want to dance. You’ll see this most often from people who lift at home, or at a chummy small gym where an impromptu dance party might occur between friends. Alone in my garage gym, I’ve been known to put on some good karaoke songs and sing along, with or without dancing.

The idea is not to get some extra cardio or get your steps in, but just to stay busy and loose and have fun—or, if you’re nervous about a big lift, to keep your head. At a busy commercial gym, make sure not to walk too far away, and consider leaving your water bottle or gym bag on your equipment to let people know it’s still taken.

I promise that it is actually OK to look at your phone between sets. You’re probably doing it anyway. There’s your lifting app, your videos that you took to check your squat form—and, yes, your usual texting and social media stuff.

The risk you’re taking here is that you’ll get distracted and rest too long. Too much rest, say more than 10 minutes, can make you feel both mentally and physically unprepared. Long rests can also lengthen your workout. Sticking to the minimum necessary rest times will get you in and out of the gym a lot faster.

From others’ perspective, a person who sits on a machine for 10 minutes scrolling is committing an unforgivable sin in gym etiquette. Don’t take longer rests than you need, if you’re holding up equipment. (That said, if you’re polite to others who approach, and you accept requests to “work in” (take turns) while you scroll, you’re morally in the clear.)

For your own sake, set a timer when you do anything that might distract you between sets. I like how I can program rest times into a Garmin watch workout, but Hevy also has a timer, and you can always use the regular timer function on your watch or phone.

Another tip: Be mindful about what you’re doing on your phone. Now is not the time to get into an argument on the internet, or check your work email and see what will be waiting for you back at the office. It is a good time to play little games on your phone, or read an ebook, or put together your next great workout playlist.

I’m leaving this option for last because I don’t want it to be your go-to. Just because you can do one exercise while resting for another, doesn’t mean you should.

Why not? If you really want to give squats your full energy, for example, don’t do pull-ups on the squat rack in between. You’ll fatigue yourself more than you realize, and you won’t do your best on those squats.

But supersets can be great if you’re willing to sacrifice a little weight for a lot of time. They’re best for exercises where the exact weight doesn’t matter—you can pair bicep curls with tricep extensions, or pull-ups with dips (so long as you’re a person who can bang out multiple reps of each with ease).

To do a superset, you just do a set of the first exercise, then a set of the second, then rest a short time (if needed at all), and repeat. I have a list of the best superset pairs here—usually you’ll want to do opposing muscles, like a push exercise with a pull exercise.

#3910. Control loreheads shouldn't put too much stock into what The Board says in Control Resonant - the devs say it's "basically gibberish"

One of Control and Control Resonant's most memorable stylistic choices is how The Board - an alien entity whose true nature is a mystery, and whose morals and motivations are never made clear - communicates with the player.

Clearly, developer Remedy knew exactly how The Board should sound coming out of your speakers. While there's no consensus, many have theories about what The Board is truly saying - and that's in part because of the way it sounds.

Except, it turns out what you hear shouldn't be taken very seriously.

"An important part of the design is that The Board is basically gibberish, not affiliated to any language," Control Resonant audio lead Lucas Pierrot told Gamespot. "So, when you hear a word, you tend to hear what you want to hear… and that's pretty neat in terms of audio design, as players can fulfill their fantasies."

The Board speaks an oblique language and in a very bassy voice, making it hard to make out what's being said from voice alone. The games' subtitles offer what could only be considered an interpretation of some of what's being said, but they alternate between different words for similar concepts to make it even harder for The Board's speech to have a definitive meaning.

Pierrot added, "You want to hear The Board praising you for performing an Execution? You can! Maybe you hear them saying 'Absolute cinema, Combo!' etc., but if you prefer to think they're arrogant and only feel disdain for slaying a weak enemy… you can hear that too!"

The Board's distinct way of communicating with the player was designed to run a gamut of emotions; Remedy wanted it to feel "scary, unsettling, or even funny sometimes". Pierrot also revealed a trick the audio team used to emphasise certain words, by adding a "pulse" sound and "low, soft kick" to them.

"I used this to give weight when necessary to have a nice flow. You can see it as some sort of trick to articulate sentences. It gives rhythm and punctuation," he explained.

Control Resonant has seemingly done well for Remedy, reaching 1m sales faster than any other game in studio history despite releasing in a very busy period. If you're looking to play it soon, Eurogamer's Control Resonant walkthrough is worth bookmarking to help you make good upgrade decisions and pick which activities to prioritise.

#3908. Here’s Another Mass Effect 5 Leak To Break Your Heart

If last week's mysterious Mass Effect: Universe trailer leak wasn't painful enough for you in the wake of BioWare's sudden closure, we've gotten yet another taste of The Game That Could Have Been, this time courtesy of award-winning video game composer Austin Wintory. 

Known for his beautiful and haunting soundtracks for games like Flow, Journey, ABZÛ, Aliens: Fireteam Elite, Sword of The Sea, John Wick Hex, and Hades II, Wintory was also evidently working on the Mass Effect: Universe soundtrack. From the sounds of things, EA and the now-defunct BioWare were considering having Wintory produce the game's OST. And thanks to Wintory, we at least know what the game might have sounded like.

"Mass Effect was my ultimate white whale, my all-time dream project," Wintory wrote in a post on Bluesky. "For the last couple years I'd been in discussion with BioWare & EA to take on the project, and as part of that, I wrote close to 20 minutes of music to explore where the franchise can go next. May as well share it!"

Wintory shared the music alongside an image that reads, "Mass Effect: Universe, the music that might have been."

"So here you go," Wintory added in a follow-up post linking to a YouTube upload of the test-run tracks. "Thank you again to my friends at both BioWare and EA. To even dream in your sandbox was a privilege."

The video features 20 minutes of Wintory's experimental score, featuring seven unique tracks and one remix. Text displayed within the video itself also reveals more about the nature of Wintory's talks with EA and BioWare, along with his inspiration for the soundtrack.

"In 2007, when the credits rolled on my first playthrough of Mass Effect, I remember calling a friend and saying, 'I can't believe I already know this will be among my favorite all-time games.' Usually, it takes the passage of time before we can be so sure something truly belongs to the ages, but in this case, the confidence was instantaneous," Wintory wrote. "This trilogy is my most-cherished gaming experience of my lifetime, and as a professional composer, represented my most sought-after 'White Whale.' For me, there is no greater dream project to be part of."

According to Wintory, nothing had been finalized yet, but both EA and BioWare seemed eager to get him on board. So Wintory, in all his love for the franchise, cooked up a little surprise for the Mass Effect: Universe team.

"For the past several years, I'd been in dialogue with people both at BioWare and EA to potentially jump into Mass Effect 5," Wintory explained. "As part of those conversations, I decided to surprise the team by writing/recording a giant love letter to the franchise, with my idea of where it can go next. When I wrote this (in 2025), I knew nothing of what they were planning, so this was a bit narratively speculative. The concept was a direct homage to Jack Wall and Sam Hulick's incredible work on the first game, while leaning even harder on true analog synths, and a more complex integration of the orchestra (almost akin to the hybrid nature of The Matrix)."

While it seems that Mass Effect: Universe will never see the light of day, at least we have the beautiful soundtrack-that-could-have been, for what looked to be an equally beautiful game.

#3905. UK-based Rockstar devs will be putting their union recognition to a vote during GTA 6's launch next month

The vote will also be held when closing arguments will be made for the ongoing employment tribunal

November is potentially going to be big for Rockstar Games, and not just because of that GTA 6 thing they're doing. As shared in a press release from the Independent Workers of Great Britain (IWGB), recognition of the IWGB Union at Rockstar will be voted upon by staffers at the studio, with votes being held from November 12th (a week before GTA 6's launch date) and November 26th.

"The terms of the recognition deal, agreed upon by workers and management, would give workers a say over topics including pay and bonuses, overtime, redundancies, flexible working arrangements, and generative AI policies," the press release explains. It also notes that if the vote is successful, the union will become the largest recognised union in the UK games industry.

This also comes at another pivotal time for Rockstar workers, both current and former alike. Almost exactly a year ago, Rockstar laid off 31 staffers in an alleged act of union busting. Fast forward to now, and an employment tribunal is being held between the game developer, the IWGB, and the laid off workers. Closing arguments for the tribunal will take place between November 17th and 18th, mere days ahead of the game's launch. It'll still be a while until a judge decides if Rockstar did break employment law.

In a statement, Rockstar Game Workers Union Committee Member Shanti Easton-Steel said, "For me, this vote comes down to ensuring employees at Rockstar get honesty and openness surrounding the decisions that affect them. When management choices are kept hidden, we can’t properly address issues like the gender pay gap, the uneven and ever-changing policies on overtime pay, or the concerns many co-workers hold around job security post-release."

Solidarity with the workers as always. GTA 6 will undoubtedly be the biggest game of the decade, and everyone that has worked on it deserves to be properly compensated and assuaged of their concerns.

#3904. Sofia Franklyn Writes Candidly About Her Abortion: “This Is Hard for Me to Say Even Now”

There’s no right way to talk about this. I share a lot in this book, but this is hard for me to say even now. Having an abortion isn’t like having a public scandal, nor is writing about it. I can usually reconcile the dark corners of my past, but my feelings around my abortion are, in many ways, still pending, and, frankly, it still feels very wrong to share publicly. But I hope putting myself out there will free me from the self-imposed exile and shame.

When you have an abortion, the take-home packet gives you explicit instructions on what to do if you bleed through two pads per hour. But nowhere does it include guidance on the emotional clusterfuck that’s to come.

I took the pills at home, and weeks later I couldn’t recognize myself in the mirror, physically or mentally, and I couldn’t figure out why. I felt crazy. I later learned that there is a physiological, hormonal, neurological chain reaction that occurs. No one prepares you for that part. At the time, all I had were Reddit threads and an off-handed comment my friend made to check my hormones months after. Getting some answers, no matter how disparate, made it less scary.

Daddy Issues is fundamentally a story about being a girl in the world, and I wrote this chapter because I want women to feel less insane. Less alone. I want women to know that other women are having abortions. They are just not talking about it. We aren’t supposed to.

The first episode of my new show, Sofia with an F, goes number one on all the charts October 2020, but with every following week, the episodes lose their newness, I lose my virality, and we trend down from number one, an impossible standard to maintain for everyone but Alex Cooper’s new Call Her Daddy.

Alex follows her “The Truth About Call Her Daddy” video with an episode called “The Funeral”—a fifty-minute-plus monologue on all the reasons I’m the greedy, lazy, male-centered puppet she’s made me out to be.

Any creative muscle I still have is paralyzed by the unshakable notion that everyone hates me. I’m starting to hate myself just as much. I don’t want to create; I don’t want to do anything at all. Sometimes I’m able to craft a skeleton of a segment on my Notes app, but as soon as it’s time to press record and speak it out loud, anything I thought was interesting or worthwhile now sounds dumb and empty.

Up in the literal clouds on the fifty-seventh floor in my apartment, I get a ping that a comment I made on a podcast made it to Fox News.

“I’ve asked the last three dudes I’ve dated for their bank account info on the first date . . . I think I have every fucking right to be like, ‘Hi, are we on the same level, or am I wasting my time?’ ”

This is how I sound now. Cold and bitter, yet still provocative, or at least trying to be. There is a disconnect happening. Disconnect from my life. Disconnect from my show. Disconnect from myself.

I’m so used to being misunderstood that I let it rock. But I won’t accept defeat, so I dissociate. That’s the playbook now.

I interview guests I don’t care about, say things for clickbait, flirt with A–Z list personalities to score a collab. I hire a talent booking agency (the same one Alex uses) that charges $60,000 for a six-month retainer to get “celebrities” on my podcast.

Living dissociated is like walking through the world half asleep.

I let my boyfriend Aidan baby me. He holds my hand through this half-life, a boy to keep my physical self company without making me confront my past, my direction, or really anything at all.

I take a work trip to LA to guest-star on an episode of Selling Sunset, but when I get there I’m so bloated that I, my cousin, and the celebrity stylist I hired spend all our off-camera time gua sha–ing my stomach so I can actually fit into the looks she pulled for me.

When I’m back in New York, Aidan suggests I might be pregnant, which I reassure him isn’t possible because I took the Chinatown version of Plan B.

“I’m not pregnant!” I yell out from the bathroom.

“Let me see,” he says, silly and relieved. “Wait, you are pregnant, though.” He points to the stick.

I spend the next three hours scrambling to prove the test wrong, comparing the color of the lines to the pictures on the box, comparing the box to the pamphlet, and googling false positives, which turns out are much rarer than false negatives.

I get to Planned Parenthood five minutes before they open and say bye to Aidan. No visitors allowed. After an hour in the initial waiting room, a nurse takes me to a room where she draws my blood and hands me a cup for my urine sample, which confirms the pregnancy.

“Okay . . . Can I get an abortion?” I ask, genuinely not knowing what comes next.

“Yes, we will talk about all your options and schedule it. Probably sometime next week.”

“I have to get it now,” I say. “I’m leaving for Europe in two days.”

They’re able to squeeze me in the following morning at 10:00 a.m. while my mom and brother are on a flight to New York, the first stop in an incredible, nonrefundable, family-only trip to the Amalfi Coast for my birthday.

I look six months pregnant by the time we get to Positano, which Reddit tells me is “post-procedure belly,” even though I was only pregnant for about five weeks. I know I’m not making it up when my brother says, “You look pregnant,” out of the blue.

When I finally tell my mom, once a twenty-one-year-old pregnant with me, I feel guilty for being thirty and getting rid of it within forty-eight hours. I think about the so-called life I’m protecting by not having a baby—the partying, the numbing, the running. How can I bring new life into the world when I don’t live a life worth keeping myself?

I get back to the States to the news that Alex is launching a new media company called Unwell.

I get tagged in hundreds of green-screen videos with the original sound bite—my story from my days at Morgan Stanley before ever even meeting Alex—that made “unwell” a staple CHD phrase. Does she really have no original thoughts? She’s not only going to use our playbook, she’s going to use my bit?

My manager comes over and says we have to say something about it but not directly.

I agree to taking pics outside my apartment building, sucking in as hard as I can and pulling my skirt up to tuck in my new stomach fat.

When I don’t like how the pics turn out, we opt for posting what I have from the disposable camera I brought on the Italy trip—a ten-image roundup of me in a bikini on various boats and in hotel lobbies.

We post the carousel to the grid with the caption “I’m well.”

Even with the dopamine hit of the post (some people are actually starting to come to my defense), it becomes impossibly clear to me how much Alex has eclipsed me: the riches, the Forbes cover, the refined beauty, the celebrity guests, the SKIMS campaign that keeps popping up when I’m mid–bedtime routine, the Miu Miu flats. The husband, the Pinterest-perfect wedding covered by Vogue, the breeded dogs. Her hiring Bella Hadid’s stylist irks me the most, for whatever reason.

She’s entirely surpassed our history, a mere blip on the timeline of her meteoric rise. I know I can’t compete with her anymore.

There’s no chance of getting ahead. It’s such a relief.

From DADDY ISSUES: A Memoir by Sofia Franklyn. Copyright © 2026 by Sofia Franklyn. To be published by Simon & Schuster, LLC.

#3903. Walter Koenig's Passing Makes Me So Grateful We Have One Recent Star Trek Appearance To Remember

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

I'm so glad we have this recent memory to look back on.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Star Trek's Walter Koenig, known to fans as Pavel Chekov, has passed away at 90. As fans share tributes and experiences with the actor over the years, I just feel so grateful we got a quick cameo of the actor in Picard before he left us.

Those who haven't been keeping up with the latest Trek shows with a Paramount+ subscription may not know this, but Koenig returned to the franchise back in 2023. He had a voiceover cameo in the series finale, "The Last Generation," in which he played President Anton Chekov, the son of the original Chekov and leader of the United Federation of Planets. Former showrunner Terry Matalas paid tribute to Koenig on X after hearing the news:

One of the sweetest, kindest humans I’ve ever worked with. It was an honor. I wish we could’ve done more…

Matalas isn't lying and has gone on the record many times about wanting to make a follow-up to Picard. Given how he was positioned, it makes sense that Walter Koenig could've appeared as President Chekov in a later episode, had the series ever been made.

As for why he didn't appear in person, Matalas told The 7th Rule Podcast he simply ran out of time to finish the season exactly how he wanted to. Here's what he said when asked about the cameo:

How awesome was that? I’ll tell you, from a fan perspective, to write and direct the finale of that magnitude, and then to have him be part of it… I wish it was on camera… We ran out of time… I couldn’t even dare try it with the schedule of that finale, but even audio is satisfying. Because it’s kind of like, 'Is it not? Oh my God, it is!' It’s that feeling. I’m just thrilled that he did it for us, and we were able to get some Original Series DNA at the end there. It makes it feel like Star Trek.

Terry Matalas jumped on the opportunity to include one of the original Star Trek actors, and I'm so happy he did. I've applauded other efforts to bring in the original TOS crew in new projects, such as George Takei for Lower Decks and the Khan audio drama podcast. Strange New Worlds also attempted to bring in William Shatner, but it didn't come together.

Fortunately, the Picard cameo with Walter Koenig happened, and we now have a recent performance to look back on for the celebrated Chekov actor. The character itself was considered bold at the time, as Gene Roddenberry featured a Russian character working alongside Americans at the height of the Cold War.

Koenig played Chekov across three seasons of Star Trek and appeared in the major motion pictures. That being said, he missed several episodes of the final season of TOS due to his participation in a play in Chicago. He'd go on to another acclaimed sci-fi series, Babylon 5, where he played the recurring role of Alfred Bester.

His death leaves William Shatner and George Takei as the only living actors amongst the bridge crew of the original Star Trek. Those two famously don't get along, and even Koenig got in on the latest round of comments they shot at each other. While they may not have stayed close, they all seem to have embraced the late Leonard Nimoy's Spock credo when he said: "Live long, and prosper."

CinemaBlend extends its condolences and well wishes to the family of Walter Koenig, and wishes them well as they lay their loved one to rest. Hopefully, there will be more great stories about him as fans share their interactions, and I can't wait to rewatch "The Last Generation" for his performance.

He graduated from the University of Southern Indiana with a degree in Journalism and a minor in Radio and Television. #3902. You Can Now Lock Your Private ChatGPT Conversations With a PIN

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

If you have personal conversations with ChatGPT that you don't want others to see, you can now add an extra layer of security for those specific chats. The new Locked Chats feature allows you to hide sensitive conversations behind a four-digit account PIN (or biometric authentication on mobile), meaning others with access to your device or screen can't read them.

Locked chats are retained in your chat history—unlike temporary chats, which are private conversations that aren't saved by default—so you can return to or continue them at any point. However, once locked, they are hidden from your regular chat history, pinned chats, and regular conversation search. To access them, you have to go to Projects > Locked chats and enter your PIN or device unlock method (such as Face ID or Touch ID).

To start a locked chat for the first time, open an existing conversation that you want to keep private and open the options menu. Select Lock chat > Lock chat and create or enter your account PIN. This moves the conversation to your locked chats project. You can repeat this with any existing conversation or start a locked chat from scratch under Projects > Locked Chats by entering your PIN or authentication method and typing a message to launch the chat. When you're done, select Lock now to end access to locked chats and hide them from the sidebar.

Note that if you don't already have an account PIN set up, you should be prompted to do so the first time you attempt to lock a chat. Select Create a PIN, enter your four-digit code, and enter it again to confirm. You can also enable biometric authentication in Account PIN settings. If you reset your account PIN at any point, all locked chats are permanently deleted and cannot be recovered.

Locked chats are available on ChatGPT Free, Go, Plus, Pro, Business, Enterprise, and Edu plans on both mobile and desktop apps. The feature is not available for ChatGPT for Healthcare. There are other limitations, too: For example, temporary, archived, and group chats can't be locked, nor can those with shared links, scheduled and background tasks, or meeting recordings. OpenAI says locked chats are rolling out gradually, so they may not be available to all users yet.

While locked chats do add a layer of privacy in terms of how conversations appear on your device, they are not suddenly encrypted or otherwise protected from being retained or used by OpenAI. As the support page notes, locked chats are not encrypted end to end or placed in a separate encrypted vault, and locking a chat does not affect other privacy settings, such as Improve the model for everyone (which can be turned off in Settings > Data controls) or remove information ChatGPT already knows about you.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.

#3901. Best factory layout in Moon Sweepers

The ultimate aim in Moon Sweepers is to create your own automated factory that makes you profit without your active participation. In simple terms, I am talking about automation and making full use of it. Let’s say the total space on the moon is limited, and an efficient design is going to make things easier.

For this guide, you need to have access to all the endgame features. Until then, it’s best to do everything manually. For this setup, we will be using Capsule Catchers, Pressure Chambers, and Stone Presses. However, the aim is to maximize the efficiency of Conveyor Belts since you can connect at most 12 of them.

For our optimal setup, we will use two Capsule Catchers, four Pressure Chambers, and eight Stone Presses. However, let’s first understand how the method works by breaking down the size.

First, place the Capsule Catcher. For maximum efficiency, you want to use four bots for a singe catcher. Next, there will be two sets of Conveyor Belts from the Capsule Catcher.

Place two Pressure Chambers. As I said, there are two sets of Conveyor Belts from the Capsule Catcher: One for Dust, and one for Sand.

Put a Splitter at one of the two Conveyor Belts connected to the Capsule Catcher. Draw two more belts from the Splitter and connect each to the Pressure Chambers. Now, the Capsule Catcher will send Dust to the Pressure Chambers and produce Moon Sand. We have one of our two key ingredients.

Next, place four Stone Presses. We will connect two Stone Presses with one Pressure Chamber. To do so, put a Splitter at the end of the Conveyor Belt that connects the two.

If I name my Pressure Chambers as PC1 and PC2 (suppose), Stone Press 1 and Stone Press 2 will be connected to PC1, and Stone Press 3 and 4 will be connected to PC2. Make sure that they’re connected to the correct paths (Sand must go to the terminals that accept Sand).

Now, put Splitters at the end of the Conveyor Belts that will deliver the Dust from the Capsule Catchers. Put the first Splitter to break the path into two paths, and one more on each to further split the two paths into four.

Each of these four belt paths will be connected to the Dust terminal of your Stone Presses. Now, your Stone Presses have Sand and Dust, and they can produce Moon Stone. This has the best rate of returns in terms of profits.

Finally, use Mergers to combine the output of all four Presses into a single Conveyor Belt that will be connected to your base.

For maximum efficiency, you’ll want to do this setup with two Capsule Catchers, four Pressure Chambers and eight Stone Presses. The method remains the same, but the design is just going to be more complex.

#3900. Ghost of Yotei studio Sucker Punch reportedly lays off 26 developers "after carefully observing the challenges in the market"

The PlayStation-owned developer is making cuts.

Layoffs have reportedly hit Sucker Punch, the PlayStation-owned studio behind the Sly Cooper series, Infamous and last year's Ghost of Yotei.

Aftermath reports that 26 workers have been affected by the cuts, citing a former employee. According to an email sent to staff and seen by Aftermath, studio leadership blamed layoffs on the shaky state of the industry and "the needs" of the developer's next, unannounced game.

"After carefully observing the challenges in the market and reviewing the team needs for our next project, we've decided to make some staffing reductions. Several positions across Sucker Punch will be eliminated as a result," studio higher ups wrote.

"We know this is a lot to take in, and we want to assure you that we did not make this decision lightly. We have given this a lot of attention and worked to support our affected colleagues during this transition as well as we can… There's no getting around that this is a hard day. Next week we will go into more details about the future."

Sucker Punch's last game, the gorgeous open-world sequel Ghost of Yotei, was seemingly a big hit for PlayStation. Ghost of Yotei sold 3.3m copies about a month after launch, outperforming other PS5 favourites such as Astro Bot in the same time period.

While Sony hasn't published updated financial figures since then, the console maker did say Ghost of Yotei "exceeded the sales of the previous title in the same period of time" when reporting its financials for the quarter ending on the 31st of December. Ghost of Tsushima sold upwards of 13m copies across consoles and PC, though Sony is seemingly done bringing its signature single-player games to PC. As ever in the games industry, even success can't protect talented developers from layoffs.

This summer's Ghost of Yotei: Complete Edition re-release came with an interesting roguelike mode, additional cosmetics, a story-focused bonus episode called Echoes of Sekigahara and much more.

#3899. Ghost Of Yotei Was A Hit, But Its Developer Is Reportedly Cutting Jobs

Sony-owned studio Sucker Punch Productions is losing 24 employees in another round of layoffs in the games industry. The studio is behind Ghost of Tsushima and Ghost of Yotei, two first-party PlayStation titles that have been critical and commercial hits.

An Aftermath report claims 24 Sucker Punch staff have lost their jobs. The studio is estimated to have between 190 and 230 staff, depending on the source, meaning Sucker Punch has lost at least 10% of its employees.

"After carefully observing the challenges in the market and reviewing the team needs for our next project, we've decided to make some staffing reductions," an email from studio leadership quoted by Aftermath read. "Several positions across Sucker Punch will be eliminated as a result."

"We know this is a lot to take in, and we want to assure you that we did not make this decision lightly. We have given this a lot of attention and worked to support our affected colleagues during this transition as well as we can," the email continued, adding: "Next week we will go into more details about the future."

For the past decade, Sucker Punch has worked on its two open-world samurai adventure games, both standout successes in Sony's first-party slate. As of 2022, Ghost of Tsushima had sold over 8 million lifetime copies, while Ghost of Yotei was reported to be Sony's best-selling title on the PS5 as of June 2026.

The studio's latest release was the critically acclaimed Echoes of Sekigahara expansion for Ghost of Yotei, which brought back the first game's protagonist, Jin Sakai.

Prior to Ghost of Tsushima, Sucker Punch developed the Sly Cooper and Infamous series. This isn't the first time the studio has undergone layoffs after a commercially successful release; an unspecified number of layoffs occurred in 2014 following the launch of Infamous Second Son, the same month the expansion First Light was released.

Rockstar union agreement covers "pay and bonuses, overtime, redundancies, flexible working, and generative AI policies"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 developers at Rockstar Games will begin the vote on the studio's proposed union right as the new Grand Theft Auto lets players start pre-loading the game on PS5 and Xbox Series X.

News of the impending Rockstar union vote comes from Aftermath, which shared a statement from the Independent Workers' union of Great Britain (IWGB), a labor union with widespread reach including a significant presence in games. IWGB has helped Rockstar developers organize their own studio union to bargain for benefits, rights, and protections.

The voting period will run from November 12 through November 26. GTA 6 launches Thursday, November 19, and pre-loading for the hotly anticipated game starts a week earlier. Rumors indicate GTA 6 is a 210GB download on PS5, so pre-loading ought to come in handy.

While the most-anticipated game of all time is devoured by millions players, many of the people who made it will be charting the future of their workplace. If the vote clears, Rockstar will officially be unionized, which would be a major step forward for the already humming labor push in the broader games industry. Just this month, D&D and Magic The Gathering developers unionized.

IWGB Game Workers shared Aftermath's story online. The agreement, if passed, will "grant workers a say over issues including pay and bonuses, overtime, redundancies, flexible working, and generative AI policies," the union reiterates.

"The terms of the recognition deal, agreed upon by workers and management, would give workers a say over topics including pay and bonuses, overtime, redundancies, flexible working arrangements, and generative AI policies," a press release reads (thanks, Rock Paper Shotgun).

Shanti Easton-Steel, a committee member for the Rockstar Game Workers Union, discussed what's being voted on: "When management choices are kept hidden, we can’t properly address issues like the gender pay gap, the uneven and ever-changing policies on overtime pay, or the concerns many co-workers hold around job security post-release."

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

"This is our moment to secure a window onto the decisions that shape our lives, and a seat at the table to improve them. I’m immensely proud of the games Rockstar gives its fans – I want to be equally proud of the voice Rockstar gives its staff," Easton-Steel adds.

This vote follows a messy and ongoing legal dispute over Rockstar staff, including many unionizing staff, who were fired for allegedly leaking "confidential information." Rockstar has repeatedly denied claims of union busting.

Rockstar also has a documented history of crunch, a term often used in game development to describe extended periods of heavy, often under-compensated, and quietly (or loudly) mandated overtime during production – the exact sort of thing that game unions have worked to prevent or control.

Rockstar devs allege crunch is so common at the GTA 6 studio that it's "built into" contracts and you have to "opt out" of it.

Playing music too loud on the subway? Believe it or not, straight to jail.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I've always thought that I'm a particularly good judge of character, so I was happy to put my theory to the test with Deadline: Afterlife Processing's new demo where you play as some corporate angel judging whether the souls of the recently deceased should go upstairs or downstairs (I think we all know what that means).

Deadline's demo starts like most stints in corporations do, with paperwork. I'm not entirely sure whether I signed my soul away, but I was sad to hear I won't be getting time off anytime soon. After I was done with all the bureaucracy it was time to get to work.

First thing's first, you need to clock in, every minute counts and you don't want to be late for work when your boss is either the creator of the universe or someone powerful enough to force the deceased into a suit and tie. The rest of my work day played out pretty normally: I'd grab a coffee, sift through some notes, and use this giant machine which can read the souls of humans and extrapolate key details of their life. This is what I used to decide if people had been naughty or nice.

Most of the souls that crossed my paths were pretty obvious. They'd either donated their lifesavings to charity or punched a random old lady in the street—figuring out who deserves to go to heaven or hell isn't exactly rocket science in these cases. There were, however, a couple of scenarios that had me stop and ponder the ethics of a given situation.

One soul that came to me on the second day of my job had killed a stranger on the subway. Now, this is obviously bad, you shouldn't kill people, but the stranger had been playing their music obnoxiously loud, no headphones or anything. That's not to say they had it coming, but I can't say that I don't at least understand where this person was coming from. I gave them the benefit of the doubt and sent them upstairs—hopefully the music isn't too loud up there.

A second case that had me momentarily stumped involved a grumpy old man who never attended or donated money to the neighbourhood block parties. After thinking on it for all of one second I decided to send him upwards as well. You see, I also hate block parties, so not attending them is the right decision in my book.

I stand by my decisions, every single one of them. I think my nebulous boss liked them as well, because after a few days I was offered a fulltime position. This is when everything started getting weird.

It was here that I encountered a soul orb that began glowing strangely and seemed as if it was trying to follow me out of the room. But managing the strange ethereal glow of abandoned souls isn't in my job description—I checked—so legally speaking that isn't my problem.

#3896. The Comment That Convinced Quentin Tarantino To Bring Back Brad Pitt’s Cliff Booth

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

Get the latest movie, TV and streaming news delivered to your inbox.

Angelina Jolie Is Allegedly Moving. Will It Impact Brad Pitt And His Relationship With Their Kids?

Everyone Thinks Mike Flanagan's 'Adaptation Everest' Is The Dark Tower. I Was Surprised About What It Really Is

I've Seen The Prestige A Bunch Of Times Already, But Heck Yeah, I'll See It In IMAX

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With one notable exception, Quentin Tarantino has avoided writing and directing sequels in his career. Now, Once Upon A Time in Hollywood is getting the sequel treatment, at least spiritually. Counting Kill Bill as one project, as Tarantino does, this will be the first time the legend will be part of a continuing story. The movie, The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, is written by Tarantino and directed by David Fincher. Surprisingly, it was a comment in a review that convinced the Pulp Fiction director to bring back Brad Pitt’s character from Once Upon a Time.

Over the weekend, Tarantino made an appearance at the AFI Fest in LA for a screening of OUATIH and a 17-minute preview of the new Cliff Booth movie. He talked about the upcoming sequel, which will hit the 2026 movie schedule on November 25 before being able to be streamed with a Netflix subscription on December 23, and why he decided to write it. It all came from a positive review of the first movie. Tarantino commented (via THR):

I tend to not remember good reviews of mine in quotes; I kind of remember the bad reviews in quotes, but I don’t really remember good reviews of mine in quotes. But one quote that I do remember somebody writing about Once Upon a Time in Hollywood, it was like, ‘This seems like the first world Quentin Tarantino has ever created that he would like to live in,’ and there is that aspect.

I wasn’t just audiences who really connected with Booth and Pitt as Booth. Tarantino went on to explain that he loved the character too, saying:

I loved the Cliff Booth character, I loved the way that Brad played him, and there was this aspect of him as sort of like a paperback hero. I could see like a series of paperbacks with Cliff Booth getting involved with this adventure or that adventure or something like that.

That led to The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, which Tarantino was originally reported to be directing in addition to writing, before he decided to step back from directing and bring in Fincher. Tarantino is also writing a novelization of the movie, turning Booth into a literal paperback hero. I have to wonder if there is more ahead from the character, assuming this movie ends up a hit. Tarantino wonders too, adding:

As time went on, I just thought about coming up with a different adventure, getting him in now a misadventure. I wanted to see what would happen with that. I’ve got nothing to do but just invest in this mythology. I invested in it before I did the movie, and then I invested in it in the years since I’ve done the movie, just so I know the mythology. The audience doesn’t need to know everything, but it’s kind of nice that I know everything, and you can trust me that I know it.

Given that Tarantino has expressed that he plans to direct just one more movie in his career, could there be a future where he writes a series of paperbacks about Cliff Booth? I have to admit, I’m intrigued by the idea.

#3895. So What Do We Want From a 'Glee' Reboot?

Did you hear whispers of four-part harmony rustling in the trees? Did you get the urge to do intricate choreography in a fluorescently lit room? Did you suddenly start believing, and did you even manage to hold on to that feeling? Well, that would make sense, because on Wednesday Disney+ and Hulu announced that Glee—Ryan Murphy’s zeitgeisty show-choir series—is officially being rebooted, with Variety reporting that none other than Lea Michele will reprise her star-making role as Rachel “She May Be Difficult, but, Boy, Can She Sing” Berry. Naturally, musical-loving millennials lost their collective minds at the news.

With the news that Girls is returning to HBO for a five-episode revival series, Glee is the second touchstone show from the 2010s to announce a revival in as many weeks. (Millennials: 2. Zoomers: 0.) Whether either of these things is a good idea creatively is sort of beside the point. The wheels are in motion, and they are hurtling our way faster than a slushy headed straight toward Rachel Berry’s headband-adorned face.

As someone who was voted “most likely to appear on Glee” in their 2011 high school yearbook (true story) and who occasionally listens to a “Glee Cast Version” of a song privately on Spotify (sorry, sometimes they’re better than the originals!), I do feel I have earned the right to make a few demands of what we want to see—and don’t want to see—from a Glee reboot series.

Yes, the series ended with a flash-forward to 2020 (spoilers, I guess?). Rachel Berry is a Tony winner and surrogate for Kurt and Blaine’s baby, and Sue Sylvester is vice president of the United States. (Actually, it’s a bit scary how much Sue Sylvester and JD Vance have in common—two Republican blowhards from the Midwest who ascend to our nation’s second-highest office.) According to Will & Grace reboot rules, none of that is canon. So we can disregard all of the happy endings from the series finale and throw the Gleeks back into the mess where they belong…

Chris Colfer, Lea Michele, Amber Riley, Cory Monteith, Jenna Ushkowitz, Glee, 2009.

Glee is about so many things: Finding your tribe. Hitting a flawless high F while nailing a double pirouette in a peplum top. Faking a pregnancy (we still talk about you, Terri Schuester). It’s also about being a kid with big dreams stuck in a small town. So for that reason we’ve gotta bring the action back to where it all began: William McKinley High School.

I am aware that Glee trades the Buckeye State for the Big Apple in season four, choosing to follow Rachel and company as they try to make it in New York City. It made sense at the time. They had to graduate eventually. But everyone knows it led to diminishing returns. Splitting time between the hustle and bustle of NYC and the glee club back at William McKinley just didn’t quite work. (Although the Sarah Jessica Parker–led mash-up “Let’s Have a Kiki / It’s Turkey Lurkey Time” remains one of the all-time craziest “they did what???” moments in the series.) Also, the Girls revival will have plenty of New York content for millennials to be nostalgic for.

Cory Monteith, Lea Michele, and Dianna Agron, Glee, 2009.

Let’s find a natural way to get them back to where it all began. Maybe some of them never left: Maybe Brittany, the secret math genius she was, is teaching at a local university. And perhaps Blaine and Kurt have settled in a nearby Midwest town and are micro-influencers for their growing antique collection.

Rachel Berry is the trickiest to crack, and as such will be the engine of the show. I think it’s fine to keep her as a Tony-winning diva, who gets run out of New York for bad behavior—maybe she’s caught poisoning her competition before a final callback for a site-specific production of Rent in the East Village—and winds up back at home, Tony in hand, tail between her legs and demanding to be the coach of New Directions. Imagine what a nightmare she’d be as a show-choir director.

Glee had so many cast members and guest stars over the course of its six-season run that it would be impossible to try to get them all back for the revival. Plus, sadly, they aren’t all still with us to return. There are some truly devastating losses from the original Glee cast (RIP Cory Monteith and Naya Rivera), and some deeply disturbing ones as well (the less said about Mark Salling the better). Figuring out who should come back is easier said than done.

Lea Michele, Chris Colfer, Amber Riley, Kevin McHale, Jenna Ushkowitz, Glee, 2009.

With Lea Michele confirmed and Brittany Pierce (Heather Morris) and Sam Evans (Chord Overstreet) teasing on social media that they’re involved, it’s clear they are going to be reaching out to some stars of the original series. Let’s throw in the other day-ones with her: Artie. Jenna. Kurt. Mercedes. Quinn. And Blaine (he basically counts at this point). As for teachers, we gotta have Jane Lynch’s Sue Sylvester, because what is Glee if Jane Lynch isn’t delivering some of the nastiest reads you’ve ever heard? Mr. Schuester is on the fence…would he really still be principal of William McKinley all these years later? (I fear in our current economy the answer is yes.) After much deliberation, Matthew Morrison’s Mr. Schue can return as well on the condition that in his spare time he keeps making Cameos that ultimately become viral TikToks.

Beyond that, I think it’s okay to be picky and choosy about who returns for a prominent role. The Glee reboot should pepper the series with some fun cameos from beloved characters, many of whom got cast in the later shakier seasons, as well as some of the most fun guest stars. Thank God Gwyneth Paltrow started acting again so she can reprise her Emmy-winning role as Holly Holliday, and NeNe Leakes is free to play bronze-medal-winning synchronized-swimming coach Roz Washington.

We also need to leave room for actors who obviously would exist and thrive in the Glee Cinematic Universe (the GCU, if you will), but never got the chance to. Ryan Murphy once teased he was writing a Glee reboot for Beanie Feldstein and Ben Platt. Congrats, you’re both teachers at McKinley High. Rachel Zegler, you’re on tap to be the second coming of Rachel Berry, as frighteningly talented and ambitious as your foremother (I mean, it’s literally in her name).

I also want to leave room for four to six of the best singers that TikTok has to offer, of any age. Ryan Murphy, it’s time to get to scrolling. Which brings us to the next point…

I don’t just want songs, I want mash-ups. The good, old-fashioned 2010s style of mixing up two completely different songs that Glee was famous for. (Matter of fact, Murphy should hire DJ Earworm right now.) We’ve lost this ridiculous art form in the modern era, and Glee can bring it back. And the music should reflect what’s happening in music today. I want to hear Mercedes belt out Chappell Roan’s “The Subway” while Kurt croons “Choosin’ Texas” by Ella Langley. Imagine Blaine singing Noah Kahan’s “Stick Season” on top of Quinn Fabray singing Stella Lefty’s “Boston” and what that might do to the internet. Hell, let’s have Rachel Berry take on a Taylor Swift–Olivia Rodrigo collab in one rousing musical number and destroy the internet once and for all. Mash-ups aside, maybe Lea Michele will be the first person to figure out if it is technically possible to “sing” a Katseye song.

The possibilities for a Glee reboot are endless, and they will scare you. Ryan Murphy, the ball is in your court. Just don’t let our fragile, millennially cringe hearts down, I beg.

#3894. This Super Fast ASUS OLED Gaming Monitor Is $100 Off Right Now

The 27-inch ASUS ROG Strix OLED XG27AQWMG gaming monitor has earned stellar reviews for its excellent visuals, refresh rate flexibility, and other impressive gaming-focused features. If you've been looking to upgrade your setup, right now is a good time: It’s $100 off, reaching its lowest price ever—$549 (originally $649).

This 27-inch monitor has a 4K UHD WOLED panel that delivers high contrast and deep blacks, making games, movies, and TV shows look more vivid. Compared to a traditional OLED panel, WOLED (White OLED) uses a white light source with filters to provide deeper blacks, faster response times, and more uniform images across a range of lighting conditions. The TrueBlack Glossy coating is better at reducing glare than a matte screen while delivering a clearer picture (though some reviews claim it’s still not bright enough to mitigate intense glare if bright light hits the screen directly).

A native 4K, 280 Hz refresh rate allows for high-detail gaming and multimedia use, while its Frame Rate Boost mode offers versatility for playing different kinds of games. For example, FHD at 330 Hz also makes it a solid choice for competitive shooting games and high-FPS play. The monitor has an overall 0.03 ms response time, which is best-in-class, and minimizes motion blur. Other notable features include NVIDIA G-SYNC and AMD FreeSync support, and reduced burn-in risk due to OLED Care Pro and the Neo Proximity Sensor.

If you’re looking for a gaming monitor with dual-mode features, nearly instantaneous response time across a range of refresh rates, low input lag, and inky blacks, the 27-inch ASUS ROG Strix OLED XG27AQWMG monitor for gamers is a great choice.
