#3893. Pokémon UNITE, the MOBA you forgot, will be shutting down in a few months

Pokémon’s attempt at a MOBA is shutting down after just five years of service, the company announced today.

Pokémon UNITE launched in July 2021, available on both mobile and Nintendo Switch, and I genuinely enjoyed playing it at first. But a miserable solo ranked experience quickly turned me away, and it appears the game has stopped being profitable several years later.

The concept of the game was simple enough to attract casual players while iterating on the MOBA genre. You chose to play as a number of different Pokémon and could level up and evolve throughout a match, scoring points at various goals throughout the arena to defeat the opposing team.

“We offer our sincerest gratitude to the many Trainers who have played since service started for the Nintendo Switch version on July 21, 2021,” the announcement read. “We would like to express our thanks to everyone by holding a special event until service ends. The Pokémon UNITE Aeos Crown 2027 Tokyo competitive tournament (an event for Pokémon UNITE only) will also be held as previously announced. We hope you continue to enjoy Pokémon UNITE until the final day of service.”

That tournament takes place just two days before the game will shut down, leaving the winner with an actual physical crown and title for a game that will soon no longer exist. A wonderful collector’s item, I suppose?

The main issues that many players had with the game over the years were its pay-to-win monetization, like consistent releases of new Pokémon that felt powerful and must-buy to keep up with the meta. The matchmaking in ranked felt like an active punishment every time I opened up the game, so I quickly moved on to other things.

【重要なお知らせ】いつも『Pokémon UNITE（ポケモンユナイト）』を遊んでいただき、誠にありがとうございます。この度、『ポケモンユナイト』は2027年3月23日（火）をもちまして、サービスを終了させていただくことになりました。…

The concept of Pokémon UNITE was great, but its execution left a lot to be desired. The announcement by the game’s Japanese account was met with massive engagement, with over 58,000 reposts on Twitter/X alone, so the brand recognition and dedicated fanbase were there. Apparently, the revenue or overall player numbers were not.

#3892. One Day Out From Early Access, Emberville Has Been Delayed To 2027

Originally set to enter early access on October 27, action-adventure RPG Emberville has been delayed by over two months to January 7, 2027. The last-minute delay gives the team time to make "essential improvements" before Emberville reaches its fans.

The upcoming game mixes Stardew Valley-like farming and community building with action-adventure RPG combat. Trapped in a magical underground dungeon, the player must uncover the truth about their cursed prison while revitalizing the ruined town of Emberville.

Emberville's demo was one of the most played during Steam Next Fest last week; however, player feedback from the demo has led to a last-minute delay.

"Some of the improvements we want to make are systems changes that can't be done quickly," the Emberville team said, acknowledging feedback from Next Fest players. "Reworking bow combat, for example, would take more time than we had prior to our Early Access release even though the team is working around the clock. After carefully considering our options, we have decided to move the release date and include some essential improvements in the Early Access launch build."

While the game is intended to grow and improve throughout its early access period, with the developer planning to add new content incrementally, the studio wants to reach a certain baseline before it launches.

"Our team has a clear vision for how Emberville will develop throughout early access," the Steam post continues. "On January 7, our goal is to deliver an experience that shows what makes the game distinctive, establishes its core gameplay, and offers a compelling glimpse of how the game will grow throughout early access."

While some players are disappointed by the eleventh-hour delay, others have said they're happy for the Emberville team to take as much time as it needs to get the game up to scratch. Some players have also speculated that Emberville is just the latest game to move its release date in order to avoid losing players to the looming release of GTA 6.

#3891. Amid sweeping layoffs at Xbox and massive AI investments, Microsoft introduces a "Voluntary Exit Offer" to 60,000 workers

Xbox isn't the only Microsoft division looking to massively reduce worker headcounts. In fact, Microsoft has just announced a "Voluntary Exit Offer" that will essentially pay staff to move in front of any potential layoffs and voluntarily leave their jobs.

Microsoft CPO Amy Coleman announced the plan earlier today. "This offer gives eligible employees who may be considering a future outside the company a new choice to make that transition now with support. It also gives participating organizations another way to adapt their workforce to changing needs," Colemon wrote in a memo sent to staff.

The voluntary buyout is only available to Microsoft employees "at level 70 and below" in Canada, India, the United Kingdom and the United States. Some corners of the company have opted out of the offer - it's currently unclear if Xbox or any of the studios under its umbrella are participating in the initiative.

"This is a time-limited, voluntary program that gives those who are eligible the opportunity to leave Microsoft with financial support and career planning resources. Some organizations are providing the Voluntary Exit Offer, and some are not. Participation varies based on each business’s priorities, customer commitments and workforce needs," Coleman added.

A Microsoft spokesperson told Business Insider that the offer will be available to about 60,000 employees, roughly 25 percent of the company's total workforce - a staggering figure. According to the outlet, Microsoft's voluntary exit offer has similar terms to the company's severance packages, and eligible workers need to decide if they'll lay themselves off by early December.

Xbox embarked on a huge "reset" earlier this year, which involved a planned 3,200 jobs cuts and a massive internal restructure for many of its first-party studios. Double Fine, Compulsion Games and Undead Labs have all gone independent since then, while the fates of Arkane Lyon and Ninja Theory are still shaky. As CNBC noted, the voluntary buyout was announced as Microsoft tries to balance its sky-high spending in generative AI.

#3890. Kim Kardashian Shares Update on Coparenting Challenges With Kanye West

Kim Kardashian shared a glimpse into her dynamic with ex-husband Kanye West in the new book Citizen Kim: The Woman Who Created the Future.

Kardashian, 46, and her inner circle were interviewed by journalist Jonathan Van Meter over the course of nearly 10 years from 2018 to 2026 for the in-depth biography, which hit shelves Tuesday, October 27.

Citizen Kim tracks Kardashian’s highs and lows from childhood to reality stardom, including her marriages to exes Damon Thomas, Kris Humphries and West, 49.

Kardashian and West, who share four children, were married from 2014 to 2022. Throughout their relationship, West battled with his mental health, initially receiving a bipolar diagnosis in 2016. Kardashian spoke openly with Van Meter about how she’s coped in the public eye.

“In big traumatic experiences, I feel like what I learned to do over and over was deal with it and move on, deal with it and move on,” she said. “Maybe dealing is not the right word. It’s more like sweeping it away, under the carpet. … And then you also feel so guilty. Or so bad, like, ‘I’m so sorry you’re going through this. And how do we make it better?’ And also kind of embarrassed, because certain things were said during psychotic breaks. Because certain things would happen, and I just wanted to protect him. I don’t want anyone to know about this.”

She added, “When it starts to get to the place where it affects your health and your safety and your mental health, then you’re kind of like, ‘OK, who am I going to save here?’ I gotta save myself. You get to that place.”

Scroll down for the biggest revelations about West from Citizen Kim:

Kardashian recalled being “fairly new into the relationship” when West wanted to give her “a fashion makeover.”

“I didn’t even know what that meant. But I was open: ‘Sure! Dress me up!'” she said. “A team of stylists came in. I was in my first house on Abington, and I thought I had the best shoes — I thought I had it all. He put me in this black Givenchy leather dress and a pair of Tom Ford snakeskin shoes and took me to a Lakers game. I came home and there were shoes piled up almost to the ceiling that had been taken out of my closet and I only had two pairs left — that Kanye’s team kept. I almost started to cry. ‘But I spent years collecting them!’ And then there were racks and racks of new clothes, designers I’d never heard of before, like Lanvin.”

Kardashian admitted she “didn’t know anything about high fashion” at the time. “There were all these fashion books piled up. It was time to get to work and learn,” she said, adding that West helped her gain “so much confidence.”

Kardashian said she met West’s late mother, Donda, only once at the 2007 MTV Video Music Awards in Las Vegas.

“I didn’t meet her as his girlfriend — just someone who came to the event. She was so nice. She was everything to him. I’m pretty sure he’ll always be lost without her,” she said. “But I also think this is his path — he needs to go on this journey without her. But I do think everything would be different if she were still here.”

Donda was 58 when she died in November 2007, soon after the VMAs. The Los Angeles County coroner’s office later revealed she died of coronary artery disease and complications from cosmetic surgery.

“I really just want him to win, I want him to shine, and I don’t like it when he doesn’t look good,” Kardashian told Van Meter while reflecting on navigating her public split from the rapper and his bipolar disorder diagnosis. “I didn’t expect this to be easy, but I didn’t expect it to be so public. I thought he was cooler than that. Like, what are you doing? And that makes me sad for him. Because I’m just like, ‘Come on, dude. Get it together.'”

Kardashian later pointed out that West’s behavior never became a concern when he was around the kids. “That was such a blessing. He would have a full episode, and the kids would never know because they would be almost a calming presence for him. So it was a fine balance of, like, ‘How do we keep them around? Or: Is it go-time?'” she said. “My whole household knows what a ‘lockdown’ means if I text it. Like, ‘We’re on lockdown.’ No TVs, just movies. No radio, just plug in a phone for music in the car. Not even, like, SiriusXM, because they talk about current events in real time. It’s like, ‘They don’t have to be a party to this.'”

“Oh, I worry about [Kanye] all day,” Kardashian admitted. “All the time. That’s the father of my kids, I’ll never not worry about him. And that’s a big burden of having kids with someone who you don’t necessarily get along with. My kids would never know that. I do everything in my power to forget about all the negative things that happened. I have to be realistic, and my therapist definitely tells me, ‘Your boundaries have to go up,’ but we manage to coparent without too much conflict.”

She continued, “It’s been pretty smooth for a while. I guess because we don’t talk that much. But when we see each other, we give each other a hug. I’m really close with his family. I welcome anyone. I tell them all, open-door policy. Anytime you’re in town, come for dinner with us, see the kids as much as you want, take them out, come over and hang out. That’s their family. So I encourage the relationship with their dad so much.”

Despite her “constant worrying,” Kardashian said, “He always seems to pick himself up … and out of something, but it does make me so sad. And I don’t think it’s going to get better. I pray it does. For my kids.”

Kardashian revealed she communicates with her ex-husband “through his wife,” Bianca Censori, or his manager.

The reality star admitted the dynamic comes with its challenges. “I’m always like, ‘Oh, Dad’s traveling,'” she said. “I still make up stories and protect [the kids] because I want them to think their dad is the greatest. But I invite him over all the time. ‘Do you want to come to dinner? I’ll leave the house. Come and have dinner with the kids in their house so that they can feel comfy. Sleep over for a few nights?’ Last Christmas, I said, ‘Sleep over on Christmas Eve. Bring your girlfriend.’ I’m easy! ‘Let’s all do Christmas together.’ And I’ll recommend it again this year. I will sweep everything under the rug, all the horrible things he has said about me, so that the kids can see their father.”

Nvidia's RTX 5090 has been a subject of much speculation recently, as it was rumoured to be discontinued last month when it vanished from the company's official marketplace, only for Nvidia to confirm it wasn't discontinued. The plot has thickened, though, as AIB partners—the companies actually designing and manufacturing RTX 50 series graphics cards—suggest even they can't get their hands on the parts required to make the card.

This is according to an Nvidia AIB partner who spoke to YouTuber Der8auer. They say, "No, Nvidia did not inform us that RTX 5090 is EOL. But currently we cannot purchase GB202 [the GPU] together with 32 GB GDDR7 as [a] complete set, and we also don't know if ordering will be possible again in the future."

The unnamed board partner clarifies that in previous shortages, they could place backorders with Nvidia, but this is "currently not possible anymore."

This is all to say that this AIB partner is unable to get the GPU and memory required to make the RTX 5090, and doesn't know when that supply issue will be fixed. Though this isn't the card being discontinued, for many partners, not getting the GPU and VRAM is as good as doing so.

Whether it's the GPU or the memory that's causing the biggest headache isn't specified, though in the current context, one can assume it's likely both to some degree.

As of right now, companies are reliant on GPU and VRAM bundles from Nvidia before they can make their own cards. There have previously been rumours that Nvidia would stop bundling its GPUs and VRAM together, but even if it did that, this would only partly solve the issue of the memory crisis for Nvidia. Those who might have been able to buy just the GPU would still be subject to a price squeeze on the VRAM when trying to source it direct from one of the major memory manufacturers.

Der8auer also spoke to an RTX 5090 cooler manufacturer who said, "Two of our partners already reduced quite much the order forecast for RTX 5090 based heatsinks in Q1 2027, when compare[d] with Q3 2026."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

"For Q2 2027, until now we still did not receive any order for RTX 5090 model heatsinks from our partners."

Nvidia still remains steadfast in saying the card has not been discontinued, though it acknowledges that limited supply chain capacity has led to a "somewhat constrained" output. Its German branch says, "Nvidia will continue to ship it, and this is very important."

The RTX 5090 (an already expensive card at its previous MSRP of $2,000) has been prohibitively expensive for months. It's so pricey, in fact, that we stopped tracking it in our graphics card price watch. It has been incredibly difficult to find any of the cards available from reputable sellers at even reasonable prices (with some listed for upwards of $8,000 right now) and therefore we could never recommend actually picking one up. Though it may not be officially discontinued, it seems like the likelihood of picking one up soon at a decent price is getting slimmer and slimmer by the day.

The Marvel Cinematic Universe is known for constantly expanding, thanks to new projects arriving in theaters and streaming with a Disney+ subscription. Fans who spent years watching the Marvel movies in order have seen a variety of post-credits scenes introduce characters and ideas we never saw again, including Daniel Destin Cretton's Shang-Chi. The filmmaker knows fans still have questions, and recently made a vow related to his MCU debut.

Cretton had his second Marvel movie with Spider-Man: Brand New Day, but there are still lingering questions about Shang-Chi's credits scenes. During an interview with The Wrap, the filmmaker brought up those scenes while discussing Brand New Day's mysterious credits sequences. In his words:

I do think it’s frustrating when these things are like left out in the open and you never see them. And Shang-Chi is one of those. It’s still hanging out there. I have every intention of answering the question.

Shang-Chi and the Legend of the Ten Rings hit theaters back in 2021, so fans have been sitting in the dark regarding its ending for years now. Luckily Daniel Destin Cretton hasn't forgotten this, and is still committed to following-up on those lingering narrative threads. Hopefully that happens sooner rather than later.

Shang-Chi crushed at the box office, and is also one of the most critically acclaimed movies in the post-Endgame phase. Unfortunately we still haven't gotten a sequel, or answers regarding the movie's intriguing credits scene. In the same interview, the director shared his perspective on why, saying:

When you really look at what happened to the industry, at least from my perspective, it was just like everything went to shit after that. I feel like if Marvel was where it was before, we would have had maybe two sequels already. Who knows?

Simu Liu has expressed similar frustrations about not getting a sequel, but luckily the actor will reprise his role as part of the Avengers: Doomsday cast. Hopefully we get answers about Shang-Chi's credits sooner rather than later.

The group of heroes discuss how the rings were seemingly acting as a beacon, with fans theorizing at the time that this was setting up Kang. As we know, Jonathan Majors' firing resulted in that character being dropped altogether.

There hasn't been any follow-ups to this cliffhanger, but maybe we'll get some references when Shang-Chi appears in Doomsday. Either that or w'ell have to keep waiting for a sequel.

Avengers: Doomsday will hit theaters on December 18th as part of the 2026 movie release list. For now, fans can re-watch Shang-Chi over on Disney+, complete with its unanswered questions.

#3887. Gone Girls: Hollywood Is Purging Its Women Execs

Way back in the days of dial-up modems, Furbies, and CDs, The New Yorker ran a 1998 story featuring seven women who were in top decision-making positions at Hollywood studios. At the time, Sherry Lansing was chairwoman and CEO of Paramount. (She previously became the first woman to lead a major studio when she became president of production at 20th Century Fox.) Amy Pascal was president of Columbia Pictures, Stacey Snider was a co-president of production at Universal Pictures (she would become chairman in 1999), and Laura Ziskin was running Fox 2000.

That era feels a million years away for many reasons, including the complete obliteration of the moviemaking landscape. With the Disney-Fox merger, the historic Big Six studios shrunk down to five, and now, with Paramount’s acquisition of Warner Bros., down to four. Meanwhile, the streamers—Netflix, Apple, Amazon—entered the game and changed all the rules.

What has also changed just as dramatically is who sits on top. Amid a flurry of cuts the past couple of years, the number of women in the highest ranks at the major studios has dwindled, and grows fewer by the day. And in just the past few weeks, it’s been a bloodbath.

“It is hard to see so many accomplished women being let go. Yet this is bigger than any one executive’s departure. If you look beyond individual headlines, there’s a larger pattern,” Women in Film CEO Kirsten Schaffer tells Vanity Fair. “We’re deeply concerned that with all of the consolidation we’re losing ground for women and people of color to attain and retain decision-making power.”

Last week, Netflix launched its largest round of job cuts since 2022, laying off about 1,000 staffers, 6% of its global workforce. The most surprising sack was chief content officer Bela Bajaria, who had been with the streaming giant since 2016. After being promoted to the role in 2023, she oversaw Netflix’s TV series, movies, and live events globally. She will be replaced by former Disney and Fox executive Peter Rice.

The Netflix layoffs included other high-profile firings, like head of film Dan Lin, though his exit had been rumored for some time. Bajaria was a far bigger shock, given her long tenure and her success expanding the streamer’s global footprint and live events business. She seemed like a company girl.

Bela Bajaria, right, with Ted Sarandos and Kerry Washington at the Oct. 1 world premiere of Animals. Two weeks later Bajaria was out at Netflix.

Netflix’s cuts come on the heels of a slew of high-level executive exits as the Paramount and Warner Bros. merger finally comes to fruition. A week before the close of the October 6 merger, Cindy Holland announced in a staff memo that she would be departing Paramount Skydance. Holland, a former Netflix exec, hadn’t been there long: She was brought in as chair of direct-to-consumer by David Ellison in August 2025 when the Paramount Skydance deal closed. Per Deadline, she stated in the note that Ellison was “optimizing for HBO stability,” which, reading between the lines, meant that HBO chief Casey Bloys would run streaming for both Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery when the merger closed.

By October 1, Pamela Abdy and Mike De Luca were reportedly out as well. The former co-chairs and CEOs of the Warner Bros. Motion Picture Group had brought the studio 30 Oscar nominations and 11 wins last year with hits like Sinners and One Battle After Another. After that huge year, their contracts were renewed that October, but 2026 has been much rockier for the pair, with The Bride! and Digger both stumbling at the box office. Paramount film heads Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein will oversee films for both movie studios after the merger, with a goal to release 30 movies each year. They hit the ground running after the merger in 2025, with a new film from Timothée Chalamet and James Mangold, a first-look deal with Wicked director Jon M. Chu, and a four-year deal with Stranger Things creators Matt and Ross Duffer. But with Warner Bros. coming into the fold, they’ll have even more oversight as they’re tasked with shaping the landscape for both studios.

Yes, there were high-profile layoffs of men too. Along with Warner Bros.’s De Luca and Netflix’s Lin, Netflix’s film VP Doug Belgrad was also let go. But with so few women in greenlight positions already, the loss of three influential leaders is a sting to both Hollywood and audiences alike. “It’s like a brutal game of musical chairs,” says Hunger Games producer Nina Jacobson, who was previously president of the Buena Vista Motion Pictures Group before launching her production company Color Force. “There have always been more men at the very top than women, and you feel that more than ever as the chairs start being taken away.”

Mergers always seem to hit high-ranking female executives hardest. After Disney acquired Fox in 2019, chairman and CEO of 20th Century Fox Stacey Snider stepped down. And after the 2022 WarnerMedia-Discovery merger, Ann Sarnoff, who in 2019 became the first woman chair and CEO of Warner Bros., was also pink-slipped. Neither of them worked at another major studio again. “Films are so hard to make that most of them are the result of at least one person on the studio side who is willing to fight for it,” says a producer. “And I have watched many of the female studio executives around town get tired of fighting.”

The ink is barely dry on the Skydance merger, and there will inevitably be many more layoffs to come as Skydance executives attempt to deliver on a promised $6 billion in cuts. One peek at the Skydance leadership website, and it’s easy to spot that Goldberg is currently the only female executive working in entertainment at the top ranks. (Of the 16 other execs listed, three others are women: chief marketing officer Rebecca Mall, CBS News editor in chief Bari Weiss, and chief communications officer Melissa Zukerman.)

Along with Goldberg, there are only a few other women left standing with that level of control in the film space now. Chairman of NBCUniversal Entertainment Donna Langley remains one of the most powerful female studio execs with a proven record of hits, including The Odyssey and The Super Mario Galaxy Movie this year. At Disney, when Josh D’Amaro became Bob Iger’s successor, Dana Walden became president and chief creative officer, a new position that included the addition of film studio operations to her already expansive purview of TV, streaming, gaming, and global marketing. And Courtenay Valenti, head of film, streaming, and theatrical at Amazon MGM, just promoted two women—Julie Rapaport and Alana Mayo—as heads of film, production, and development.

Tom Cruise, Pamela Abdy, and Michael De Luca at the Digger world premiere in London.

The consolidation of power into fewer hands (with most of those being male) is not without consequences. Individuals who have greenlight power not only decide which stories get told, but at what budget, and who gets hired. Women are still horribly underrepresented as directors. Last year, according to the USC Annenberg Inclusion Initiative, the proportion of women working as directors on the 100 top-grossing movies of the year hit a seven-year low, with only nine women out of 111 working as directors on those films. Dr. Stacy L. Smith and Dr. Katherine Pieper, the authors of the initiative, called it a “complete reversal of any progress that was achieved behind the camera over the last few years.”

Even though women are directing independent movies outside of those top 100 of the year (48% of the features in the 2026 Sundance lineup were directed by women), they aren’t directing the big-budget movies that only studios can make. Having more female executives at the top gives more opportunity to female storytellers. “[Women in Film’s] research shows that when women are in leadership positions, they hire more women,” says Schaffer. “Women executives in decision-making roles open doors for the next generation. When those seats disappear, so do the opportunities.”

Back in 2015, Lucasfilm president Kathleen Kennedy told The Guardian that “there’s nothing we’d like more than to find a female director for Star Wars.” It wasn’t until 2023 when two-time Oscar-winning documentary filmmaker Sharmeen Obaid-Chinoy was hired to helm a spin-off about Daisy Ridley’s character, Rey. But the project has languished in development. Kennedy gave up her 14-year post earlier this year, handing over the reins to Dave Filoni and Lynwen Brennan.

And having women leading studios isn’t just about giving more women a chance to work on a $200 million movie, or making female-centered stories. It’s about a more inclusive group of decision makers who can therefore make movies that reach more diverse audiences. Hollywood is supposed to be the land of endless stories and possibilities, but without a more inclusive group of people deciding which stories get told, those possibilities will keep shrinking to whatever a narrow few already believe audiences want to see. “I am reduced to counting which of the male studio heads have daughters to understand whether they’ll see value in female-driven propositions they don’t endemically understand,” says the producer of this new landscape.

Hollywood has a history of pushing women out of the executive suite, only to watch them thrive elsewhere. After Pascal was fired as co-chairman of Sony, she went on to become a top producer behind films like Little Women, Challengers, Project Hail Mary, and Greta Gerwig’s next movie, Narnia: The Magician’s Nephew. Perhaps Bajaria, Abdy, and Holland will have just as fruitful next chapters.

The question is whether the industry will build the pipeline to replace them, or if the next generation of women who might have run a studio will simply never get the chance. “In truth, I worry more for the younger folks trying to make their mark in the business than I do about those of us who have stayed in the game for decades,” says Jacobson. “The fewer jobs there are, the less people want to give up their spot, or fear it will be taken, and the fewer opportunities there are for the next generation to rise and get their chance.”

#3886. Pokemon Unite Is Shutting Down After Nearly Six Years

Nintendo has announced that Pokemon Unite, its Pokemon-themed MOBA for mobile devices and Switch consoles, will be shutting down on March 23, 2027. The game will go offline just days after the Aeos Crown 2027 tournament, and Nintendo has stopped selling the game's premium in-game currency, Aeos Gems.

"We offer our sincerest gratitude to the many Trainers who have played since service started for the Nintendo Switch version on July 21, 2021," The Pokemon Company and developer TiMi Studio Group announced in a press statement. "We hope you continue to enjoy Pokemon Unite until the final day of service."

Pokemon Unite's online matches will be a thing of the past after March, but players can download a new Library update that lets them view their career progress and stats, as well as play practice rounds. While the game had a fairly sizable audience, Pokemon Unite always seemed like a forgotten part of the Pokemon empire, and it failed to make the cut for the 2027 Pokemon World Championships. A fairly serviceable MOBA, it never seemed to catch on like other Pokemon spin-offs, and fans questioned how long it would run before Nintendo pulled the plug.

With the release of the battle-focused Pokemon Champions earlier this year, the writing may have been on the wall for Pokemon Unite, and it'll be joining several other titles in the Nintendo mobile games graveyard like Mario Kart Tour, while Animal Crossing: Pocket Camp transitioned from an online game to a microtransaction-free version that costs $10.

As the year winds down, Pokemon fans can also look forward to a new game in the series: Pokemon Winds and Waves. It's the longest wait yet between mainline entries, but judging from the teasers shown off so far, fans look content to let longtime developer Game Freak cook on this new chapter in the franchise.

You can get the Ancient Greatsword in Final Fantasy Resonance by completing the third Fledgling battle in the Colosseum. #3878. Call Of Duty Lifetime Revenue Surges Past $40 Billion, Microsoft Confirms

The Call of Duty franchise has now exceeded $40 billion in lifetime franchise revenue, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma has confirmed.

She shared this milestone following the launch of Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 on October 23. "Congrats to the team. And to the players, thank you for being with us," Sharma wrote.

Sharma did not, however, share any insights into how Modern Warfare 4 is performing, or if it was MW4 that pushed Call of Duty over $40 billion. All her statement says is that "now" Call of Duty has reached $40 billion, and that $40 billion is an "incredible milestone."

Whatever the case, $40 billion is a big number. The series debuted 23 years ago in 2003, and has seen new installments annually since. Beyond mainline entries for PC and console, the Call of Duty franchise has expanded into the mobile market and the battle royale genre with Call of Duty: Warzone.

No doubt the $40 billion figure includes sales of the main games, along with in-game content for them, as well as spinoffs and ports. Activision also surely got a fee from Paramount to make the Call of Duty movie, and it's possible this revenue is counted toward the $40 billion total as well.

Activision does not disclose development or marketing budgets for any of its games, including Call of Duty releases, so there is no way to know anything about their profitability levels. Some developers more readily share sales specifics like break-even points, but not Activision.

Activision has relied on the brand power of the Modern Warfare and Black Ops games for a long time, but a brand-new sub-series for Call of Duty is now in the works. In addition to this, Activision will develop new Halo games going forward.

Modern Warfare 4 boasts a very impressive 86 on GameSpot sister site Metacritic currently. For more, check out GameSpot’s MW4 review, which covers the campaign, DMZ, and multiplayer. If you’re just getting started, you may also wanted to check out our rundown of the best MW4 multiplayer loadouts.

#3876. EA workers complain of ‘terrible’ morale amid layoffs and ‘heavily pushed’ generative AI

"A cold dead place that's slowly decaying as leadership pilfers every last cent," one person said.

After last week’s shuttering of Mass Effect and Dragon Age studio BioWare, workers at EA are beginning to speak out, and they’re not happy.

In a new report by Chris Kerr at Vertical Slice, multiple sources from within the company sounded off about how everything feels while working at any of the company’s various studios, and to put it bluntly, the vibes are trash. And to no one’s surprise, some of it has to do with higher-ups pushing generative AI “to see how few people they can keep employed and still make a video game.”

“Morale has been terrible,” one source told Vertical Slice. “The line we kept hearing is ‘do less, better’ but it just felt like we were doing the same amount with less people. Upper management was very excited about Claude and the possibilities of [generative] AI, much to the chagrin of my team.”

“The message from every single manager I’ve heard is ‘this is the future, you have to get onboard,” said one person.

Amid EA’s announcements last week that it would “continue investing in its sports games, the online shooter Apex Legends and action-adventure projects, including the next Star Wars Jedi title and a new game starring Marvel’s Iron Man,” along with DICE and Respawn working together on a new title, likely a new Star Wars Battlefront.

“They’ve been fortunate that [EA Sports FC] sells and makes so much money that it can finance their bad bets,” another source said.

Vertical Slice said that numerous sources they spoke to all “expressed a striking sense of detachment and resentment towards their employer,” and went so far as to describe the company as “a cold dead place that’s slowly decaying as leadership pilfers every last cent, leaving the rest of us to worry about how we feed our families.”

And with more layoffs possible (if not extremely likely), one can only wonder how any of these games will get made if morale truly is this low.

#3875. This Amazon Basics Power Bank Is 67% Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Woot has dropped the price of the Amazon Basics 20,000mAh portable charger in black to $12.99 from $38.99, making it considerably cheaper than the $34.85 the same model currently costs elsewhere. You also get free standard shipping if you’re a Prime member (while everyone else pays $6), but keep in mind Woot doesn't deliver to Alaska, Hawaii, P.O. boxes, or APO addresses.

For that price, you're getting a fairly substantial backup battery that measures 4.48 by 2.83 by 1.16 inches, making it compact enough to slip into a backpack or tote, although its 0.77-pound weight might make it a little bulky for a jacket pocket. The trade-off is a fairly generous 20,000mAh capacity that should be enough to recharge a typical smartphone several times, making it particularly useful for camping trips or long weekends when access to power outlets is limited. And since you're likely to have more than just your phone with you on those trips, the charger comes with two USB-C ports and one USB-A port, allowing you to charge up to three devices at once.

When used individually, either USB-C port can deliver up to 20W, enough to fast-charge many modern smartphones, while the USB-A port tops out at 12W, making it better suited to older devices and smaller accessories. The catch is that connecting two or three devices reduces the total output to 15W shared across the ports, so charging everything together will take longer than plugging in one device at a time. Both USB-C ports can also be used to recharge the power bank itself, which takes around 4.4 hours, and the box includes a 1-foot USB-C to USB-C cable for charging (it won’t transfer data).

All said, anyone hoping to charge a laptop will need a higher-wattage model, but for occasional travel, commuting, or keeping your phone running during a long day, the capacity and price make a reasonable combination.

#3874. Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced's final major update adds long-awaited New Game Plus, new Nightmare difficulty and much more

After a period of quiet and a few small updates, Ubisoft has unveiled a massive update coming today to Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced. This is the game's last major patch and it includes some long-requested features.

Update 1.0.8 goes live on PC, PS5 and Xbox Series X/S today (27th October) at 7am PT, 10am ET, 2pm UK. It's a relatively small download on all platforms - between 2 and 4.5GB - except Xbox, where it's a whopping 15.5GB.

The new update's biggest addition is New Game Plus mode, which Ubisoft started working on in July after releasing Resynced. Much of what's earned and unlocked in your current playthrough carries over to NG+, but not everything does. Here's what carries over:

While cash and trade goods do carry over, quest-related items do not. Forts also need to be re-taken, as NG+ resets the map's fog of war.

The NG+ mode has a new exclusive currency - Escudos - earned by completing challenges and looting previously unlocked chests. The currency can be spent on NG+ exclusive outfits and items, and you'll be able to exchange Reales for Escudos at the General Store, too.

Naturally, the game difficulty increases with each NG+ run. Elite soldiers, for instance, will appear more often, and max rank ships will be a more common sight across the water. You can enter NG+ up to five times, with each further runthrough increasing the challenge.

Patch 1.0.8 also introduces Nightmare Mode, a new brutal difficulty setting. Ubisoft said enemy reaction times and the damage they deal are ramped up. Parry timings are tighter, and ranged enemies will target you more often.

What may be the biggest change, however, is the removal of the one-shot kill safety. In other words, the game won't prevent you from receiving instant deaths, such as in cases where you take high burst damage from bullets and explosives. In those cases, you're going to die, even if you have HP left. The goal was to create a harsh experience "where every duel can be your last".

Elsewhere in the patch, there are new community chests hidden around the game's world for players to uncover. There's a set of new cosmetics, new takedown animations, and, of course, a litany of bug fixes. The full change log is on the official Assassin's Creed website. It's worth reading if you're looking to attempt a playthrough on Nightmare to see what it changes.

Though that may be Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced's last big update, the game is seemingly on its way to Switch 2, and that version should include all previously-released additions, as we've come to expect.

#3870. Star Trek Enters Magic: The Gathering’s Secret Lair Next Month, Here Are The Cards

The final Magic: The Gathering expansion of 2026, Star Trek, is sending Wizards Of The Coast's card game to the final frontier. Along with the main set and pre-constructed Commander decks will be a full set of Star Trek themed Secret Lair drops, as GameSpot can now exclusively reveal the Secret Lair X Star Trek Set Phasers To Superdrop, which launches November 9 at 9 AM PT / 12 PM ET.

Six drops will be part of the Set Phaser To Superdrop offering, each one themed around the people, places, and technology of the Star Trek universe. Each drop will cost $40 in foil and $30 in non-foil, while supplies last.

Below, we have an exclusive look at five of those six drops, as well as a bonus card available to those who pick up multiple drops.

First, here's Secret Lair X Star Trek Set Phasers To Superdrop: Beam Me Up!

  • Eerie Interlude as "Beam Us Up" (Reversible)
  • Momentary Blink (Reversible)
  • Essence Flux as "Materialize" (Reversible)
  • Conjurer's Closet as "The Transporter" (Reversible)

The Beam Us Up drop features four cards themed around "blinking" permanents--or removing them from the board, then immediately bringing them back to re-use any "enters the battlefield" effects.

All four cards are reversible, with one side depicting Star Trek characters in the wilds of space, and the other showing them back in the safety of their starship, in the same pose as the first side.

The next drop is Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop: KHAAAAAAAAN!

  • Wrath of the Skies as "Best Served Cold"
  • Commandeer
  • Illusionist's Gambit as "Reliant's Betrayal"
  • Buried Alive
  • Genesis Wave
  • Fracture

The KHAAAAAAAAN Superdrop features six cards all themed around the 1982 hit film Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan. Each card highlights scenes and moments from the movie, including Ricardo Montalbán's iconic portrayal of the titular villain Khan Noonien Singh.

The third drop is Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop: Lower Decks!

  • Inti, Seneschal of the Sun as "Mariner, Breaching Protocol"
  • Kambal, Profiteering Mayor as "Boimler, Citing Protocol"
  • Silas Renn, Seeker Adept as "Rutherford, Rebuilding the Past"
  • Thrasios, Triton Hero as "Tendi, Exploring the Future"
  • Weatherlight as "U.S.S. Cerritos, California-Class"

As the name suggests, this drop features characters from Star Trek: Lower Decks, along with the show's cartoon style. This drop marks the Secret Lair debut for both Silas Renn, Seeker Adept and Thrasios, Triton Hero, which made their MTG debuts in 2016.

The fourth drop is Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop: tlhIngan maH!

  • Demand Answers (Reversible)
  • Relentless Assault (Reversible)
  • Seething Song (Reversible)
  • War's Toll (Reversible)
  • Warstorm Surge (Reversible)

This drop is all about the Klingons, with five cards showing the proud warrior race in action. Like the Beam Us Up drop, each card in the tlhIngan maH drop is reversible, with one side written in English and the other written in the iconic Klingon language.

The final drop included in today's reveal is Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop: New Worlds!

  • 2x Plains
  • 2x Island
  • 2x Swamp
  • 2x Mountain
  • 2x Forest

The New Worlds drop focuses on the many different planets Star Trek's officers have visited over the years, presented here as the five basic lands. The drop will include two of each land, which feature the artwork of longtime Magic artist Alayna Danner.

Finally, anyone who spends $200 or more on Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop will receive a bonus card: Return of the Wildspeaker as "Transporter Boondoggle!"

Secret Lair x Star Trek Set Phasers to Superdrop releases November 9 on the official MTG Secret Lair website. The sixth and final drop of the Superdrop will be "revealed later," according to the official blog post.

The full Star Trek expansion launches November 13 in local game stores and big-box retailers. Prerelease events will take place in local game stores beginning one week earlier, on November 6.

#3869. Matthew Perry Was in AA Meetings 3 Times a Day While Making Friends

In the Netflix docuseries The One About Matthew Perry, friends and family unpack the actor's legacy—and just how much he struggled with substance abuse, including at the height of his sitcom success.

Matthew Perry's former roommate remembers the one about the Friends cast making history.

"When I moved in, they were in negotiations," pal Tim Harrington, who met the actor at a recovery meeting in 2001, shares in the new Netflix docuseries The One About Matthew Perry, now streaming. "One day, I'm sitting on the bed, and he runs from his bedroom, which is directly across the hall, and he jumps on the bed and he screams, 'I just made $26 million!' or something like that."

Mind you, this wasn't two guys sharing a struggling actor's apartment.

Harrington recalls being "hopeless" when he started hanging out with the Whole Nine Yards star. They were at a California Pizza Kitchen when he told Perry he didn't think he could go on without another drink, that his roommate was using and he was considering going to live in his car.

And Perry, who died in 2023 at the age of 54 from acute effects of ketamine use, "saved my life, right there in that moment," Harrington says, by inviting him to move into his mansion in the hills.

Hence the houseguest being around when Perry, Jennifer Aniston, Courteney Cox, Lisa Kudrow, Matt LeBlanc and David Schwimmer scored their $1 million per episode deal in February 2002 after several months of negotiations.

"He was super, super excited that they had actually pulled it off," Harrington recalls.

He says he lived with Perry for about a year, during which they became near constant companions, including making time every day for multiple Alcoholics Anonymous meetings.

"He was very happy, but he was still struggling with his own stuff," Harrington says. "We'd go to three meetings a day mostly. We'd go every morning at 7:30 to West Hollywood, to the Log Cabin, a famous AA meeting."

During the drive, their go-to jam was "Drops of Jupiter" by Train, Harrington noting they'd "just play it 100 times on the way to a meeting."

"Then we went to breakfast," Harrington continues, "and then we went to the noon meeting—and then we'd discuss after that meeting what our next meeting would be."

Perry detailed how his heady rise to the peak of TV stardom was interwoven with substance abuse and trips to rehab—he first checked into treatment for a Vicodin addiction in 1997 after wrapping Friends' third season—in his raw 2022 memoir Friends, Lovers and the Big Terrible Thing, which was published just a year before he died.

As he described in the book, "Being on Friends was one of those unicorn situations where the news just kept getting better and better. But off-screen, things weren’t going so well."

Perry wrote that, after kicking his pill habit in 1997, he stayed sober for 68 days before having a drink. After which, he "drank every night until 2001."

After another stay in rehab—which is where Perry was when "The One With Monica and Chandler's Wedding" aired on May 17, 2001—he met Harrington at a meeting.

In his quest to stay sober, Perry wrote that he had spent more than $7 million, attended 6,000 AA meetings, been in rehab at least 15 times and had gone to therapy twice a week for 30 years.

"Going to meetings," Harrington explains, "it was about putting out of our minds the pain and discomfort and the struggle. And when it got quiet, that stuff would rise to the top."

Sharing a house, he and Perry were "together all the time." Then, one day, "all of a sudden," Perry told Harrington he had to move out.

He admits, "It felt like a breakup. The hanging out didn't stop, but it was just different. It raised a red flag that [Perry] may be planning for a relapse."

And "there was a resumption of use at some point," Harrington continues. "It was pretty bad. And once he was stabilized, it was wanting to necessarily not dwell on it. Like, move forward. Like, 'What's next?'"

In his book, Perry recalled spending the summer of 2002 "getting sober and playing a lot of tennis," after which season nine—which ran from September 2002 to May 2003—was "the only year I was completely sober for a Friends season."

He noted that, during the periods he was sober, "I’d think back to the recent past and wonder why I’d ever picked up pills or drugs after getting clean."

Read on for more of Perry's raw memoir revelations:

In Matthew Perry's memoir Friends, Lovers and the Big, Terrible Thing, the Friends star revealed just how close he came to dying in 2018. While staying in a sober living home in Southern California, his colon "exploded" from years of opioid overuse. The actor ended up spending two weeks in a coma and five months in a hospital. Perry also had to use a colostomy bag for nine months, which he noted broke at least 55 times. ("Dear Colostomy Bag People: make a bag that doesn't break, you f--king morons," he joked. "Did I make you laugh on Friends? If so, don't put shit all over my face.")

"I had been on opiates, and off opiates, and back on different opiates for so long that I suffered from a situation that only a subset of the population gets," Perry wrote of his organ exploding, sharing he had 14 follow-up surgeries after the initial seven-hour emergency procedure. "Opiates cause constipation. It's kind of poetic. I was so full of shit it almost killed me."

As he was in surgery, Perry revealed his family and friends who arrived at the hospital were told, "'Matthew has a two percent chance of making it through the night.' I will have to live out the rest of my days knowing that my mother and others heard those words."

Perry was originally supposed to have a role in Adam McKay's Netflix film Don't Look Up, which starred Leonardo DiCaprio, Jennifer Lawrence and Meryl Streep. But a medical scare caused him to exit the Oscar-nominated movie.During a 2020 stay at a Switzerland-based treatment center, Perry admitted he falsified symptoms to receive a prescription of hydrocodone, which proved to be near fatal when mixed with the propofol he was given for a surgery. "I was given the shot at eleven a.m.," Perry wrote. "I woke up 11 hours later in a different hospital. Apparently, the propofol had stopped my heart. For five minutes."While he didn't have a heart attack or flatline, Perry explained, "Nothing had been beating. I was told that some beefy Swiss guy really didn't want the guy from Friends dying on his table and did CPR on me for the full five minutes, beating and pounding my chest. If I hadn't been on Friends, would he have stopped at three minutes? Did Friends save my life again?"Ultimately, the life-saving tactics caused eight broken ribs and Perry was in "too much pain" to continue filming Don't Look Up, which he called "heartbreaking," and the one scene he did complete did not make the final cut of the movie.

"I have spent upward of $7 million trying to get sober," Perry revealed. "I have been to six thousand AA meetings. (Not an exaggeration, more an educated guess.) I've been  to rehab fifteen times. I've been in a mental institution, gone to therapy twice a week for thirty years, been to death's door." At the time the book was published, Perry had been sober for 18 months."Sobriety had now become the most important thing in my life," Perry wrote. "Because I learned that if you put anything in front of sobriety, you will lose that 'anything' anyway if you drink."In the book, Perry shared that he viewed alcohol as his "best friend," admitting, "I would have gone crazy without it...Learning to move forward in life without it was tantamount to asking someone to go about his or her day without breathing." And, after suffering an injury on the set of Fools Rush In, Perry developed an addiction to Vicodin and was taking 55 pills a day before checking into rehab for the first time in 1997.

While filming Friends in 2000, Perry spent 30 days in the hospital being "fed fluids through an IV" after he was told his excessive drinking was the cause of his pancreatitis.

"'F--k that,' I said," Perry recalled telling the doctor. "No. I don't drink too much.' Pancreatitis at the age of thirty was unheard of. Yay for me! Another record."

While recovering in the hospital, Perry was given Dilaudid, an opioid that changes the brain's relationship to pain. "It was my new favorite drug," he admitted, "and I would have stayed in that hospital for a hundred days if they kept administrating it."

Three years before they were cast in Friends, Perry and Jennifer Aniston met through mutual acquaintances and he was "immediately taken by" her. Believing the attraction was mutual, Perry asked Aniston out, an offer she declined. "But she said she'd love to be friends with me," Perry recalled. "And I compounded the compound by blurting, 'We can't be friends!'"

While he admitted he was "still attracted" to her when the Friends cast assembled for the first time, Perry wrote, "We were able to sail right past the past and focus on the fact  that we had both gotten the best job Hollywood had to offer." (Though he later admitted he was "crushing badly" on Aniston early on in the show's run, but her "deafening lack of interest" eventually brought it to an end.)

As for his first impressions for the rest of his co-stars, Perry described Courteney Cox as "cripplingly beautiful" and Lisa Kudrow as "just as gorgeous and hilarious and incredibly smart" as a mutual friend had said she would be. Matt LeBlanc was "nice and a cool customer," while David Schwimmer was "incredibly funny."

Perry said the six actors formed a close bond, writing, "From that first morning we were inseparable."

Perry shared that he temporarily lost a friend after he was cast on the NBC hit, having landed the part after close pal Craig Bierko choose to star in another pilot rather than play Chandler. The decision that led Bierko to not speak to Perry for years. In the plus column, though, the series saved him.

"It is not out of the realms of possibility that I may have ended up on the streets of downtown LA shooting heroin in my arm until my untimely death," he wrote, adding that it was "such a good and fun job" that it was worth remaining sober—"for awhile, at least."

"I was the second baseman for the New York Yankees. I couldn't f--k that up," he reasoned. "I would never forgive myself. When you're earning $1 million a week, you can't afford to have the seventeenth drink."

But it would be his friends on Friends that would help to once save his life a second time during the show's run, most notably Aniston, who confronted Perry about his drinking.

"'We can smell it,' she said, in a kind of weird but loving way, and the plural 'we' hit me like a sledgehammer," Perry wrote of the conversation, which he described as "devastating" and said led him to hire a sober companion at work (though he later revealed that the ninth season was the only one he was wholly sober for).

In the book, Perry revealed he was able to briefly leave his treatment center in Malibu to film Chandler and Monica's wedding in season seven."When I arrived, Jen Aniston said, 'I've been mad at you,'" Perry recalled. "'Honey,' I said, 'if you knew what I'd been through, you would notbe mad at me.'""With that we hugged, and I got the work done," he continued. "I married Monica and got driven back to the treatment center—at the height of my highest point in Friends, the highest point in my career, the iconic moment on the iconic show—in a pickup truck helmed by a sober technician."

His Shortlived Romance With Julia Roberts

Perry and Erin Brokovich star Julia Roberts were once an item in the '90s. And in the book, the actor candidly opened up about their brief romance, which began shortly after Roberts signed on to guest star in a 1996 episode of Friends. While they only dated for a few months, Perry recalled meeting Roberts' parents for the first time when he joined her family trip to New Mexico to ring in the New Year."We jumped in this big blue truck and drove up a mountain, snow swirling around," the 17 Again star wrote. "Eventually we reached a mountaintop, and for a moment the weather cleared, and we could see New Mexico and beyond, all the way back to Canada. As we sat there, she made me feel like the king of the world. A gentle snow was falling, and with that, 1996 began.But their romance only lasted three months because "dating Julia Roberts had been too much for me," Perry admitted. "I had been constantly certain that she was going to break up with me. Why would she not? I was not enough; I could never be enough; I was broken, bent, unlovable. So instead of facing the inevitable agony of losing her, I broke up with the beautiful and brilliant Julia Roberts."

Just before Friends premiered in September 1994, Perry kissed Gwyneth Paltrow in a closet while he was visiting his grandfather in Massachusetts and the Goop founder was doing a play in the area.

"At some big party we slipped off into a broom cupboard and made out," he detailed. "We were both still unknown enough that it didn't make it to the tabloids."

Paltrow wasn't the only star Perry had a secret encounter with, the actor divulging the details of a date he went on with Cameron Diaz just after she broke up with Justin Timberlake in 2007.

"The date was at a dinner party with a bunch of other people, but upon seeing me, Cameron got almost instantly stoned," Perry wrote. "It was clear that she wasn't interested in me at all."

And during a game of Pictionary, Diaz meant to playfully punch Perry in the shoulder, "but she missed," Perry recalled, "and instead, punched me smack in the side of my face. 'Are you fucking kidding me?' I said, realizing I'd just been punched in the face by Cameron Diaz."

His Memorable Encounter With Valerie Bertinelli

Perry revealed he "fell madly in love" with Hot in Cleveland actress Valerie Bertinelli when they filmed the 1990 movie Sydney and that he was having "elaborate fantasies about her leaving Eddie Van Halen and living out the rest of her days" with him instead. (Bertinelli and Van Halen were married from 1981 to 2007 and remained close until the Van Halen guitarist died in 2020.)

According to Perry, he and Bertinelli had "a long, elaborate make-out session" while her rocker husband was passed out "not 10 feet away."

"I told her I had thought about doing that for a long time," he divulged. "And she had said it right back to me." However, Perry was left "devastated" when Bertinelli never mentioned what happened again...until 2022, when she posted a TikTok in response to Perry's revelation about their kiss.

In the video, Bertinelli waves at the camera and smiles, before facepalming and shaking her head as the lyrics "It's me / Hi / I'm the problem, it's me" from Taylor Swift's "Anti-Hero" play in the background. "Anyone else misbehave in their 20's and early 30's?" she captioned the post. "Are you mortified?"

While staying at rehabilitation facility in Switzerland, Perry revealed he proposed to his then-girlfriend, whom he doesn't name, while he was "high on 1,800 milligrams of hydrocodone." "I had even asked for her family's blessing," he continued. "Then I'd proposed, high as a kite. And on one knee. And she knew it, too. And she said yes."

The two remained engaged after Perry returned to Los Angeles, though the actor admitted thinking, "'Wait...how did I get engaged? There are dogs living in my house. How did this happen?'"

Reflecting on his decision to propose, Perry connected it to his fear of abandonment. "I bought her a ring because I was desperate that she would leave me," he admitted Ultimately, Perry said he "was not ready" for marriage or any kind of commitment.

"You went down on one knee to propose, which really hurt your stomach, remember?" he wrote. "I didn't remember—needless to say, we broke up."

Several of Perry's comments about The Matrix star—including a passage where he questioned why "Keanu Reeves still walks among us" as he reflected on the deaths of "original thinkers" River Phoenix and Heath Ledger—made headlines, leading Perry to issue an apology.

"I'm actually a big fan of Keanu," the actor told People in an Oct. 26 statement. "I just chose a random name, my mistake. I apologize. I should have used my own name instead."

#3868. Playwright Jocelyn Bioh Stars in ‘School Girls; Or, the African Mean Girls Play’ on Broadway

Jocelyn Bioh is center stage on the Samuel J. Friedman Theatre in a cream ensemble—hair perfectly coiffed, ready to do business. She’s playing Eloise Amponsah, a scouter for the Miss Ghana pageant 1986, in the Broadway production of School Girls; Or, the African Mean Girls Play. In this role, Eloise has one goal: Find the girl talented and poised enough to not only win Miss Ghana but also go on to represent Ghana in the Miss Global Universe pageant. As one by one the girls try to impress Bioh’s beautifully imposing pageant scout, her character has finally had enough. The Miss Ghana pageant isn’t really about the teenage girls competing, it’s about the hunt for a worthy successor to the crown. In a Ghanaian accent with notably British affectations, Eloise lays bare what she wants, what all the girls attending the Aburi Girls’ Boarding School want. “I want for once in my life to finally be seen,” she exclaims.

It had been years since Jocelyn Bioh acted onstage—nine, to be exact. And yet, Bioh felt completely at peace when she finally treaded the boards again. “It was so strange, because when I was getting ready to go on in front of an audience on Friday night, I felt no nerves.” It may have helped that the critically acclaimed play Bioh was acting in was her very own.

For one whirlwind weekend in October, Bioh joined the glitzy cast she’d helped select, including Tony nominee Jasmine Amy Rogers, The Gilded Age star Denée Benton, and Tony winner Patina Miller.

Set entirely in Ghana in the cafeteria of the Aburi Girls’ Boarding School in 1986, School Girls follows a group of teenage girls led by their fearsome queen bee, Paulina Sarpong (Benton), as they prepare to audition for the Miss Ghana pageant. When a biracial student from Ohio, Ericka Boafo (Rogers), transfers to the school and threatens to upend Paulina’s very curated social order and chance to compete in the Miss Ghana pageant, hilarious chaos ensues.

Despite her acting bona fides, Bioh had no intention of performing in the play, but life—and Patina Miller’s schedule, which includes starring in Power Book III: Raising Kanan—had other plans. “We knew we wanted Patina,” says Bioh. But, alas, alongside her acting projects, Miller had committed to running the New York City Marathon in November. And she had to miss another weekend of performances, a mere week and a half after School Girls’ opening night. Bioh knew that losing a top-billed star for a number of performances during a pivotal time for a new Broadway play to establish itself, without an exciting replacement, could cripple the momentum of the show.

Enter Tony-nominated School Girls director, Whitney White. “Whitney was like, “Well, I have an idea…” Bioh says with a laugh. A fellow multihyphenate, White has collaborated with Bioh on a number of projects in the past, including Jaja’s African Hair Braiding, Bioh’s play set in a Harlem hair salon, which earned White a Tony nomination for best director. “She’s been trying to get me back onstage for quite a long time,” says Bioh.

In the past, Bioh always politely declined. “It just didn’t feel it was right,” she says. But something about School Girls felt different. “I don’t know why I said yes this time,” she admits. “But this play, what this play means to me, being able to do that on a Broadway stage. That just felt like that’s an offer you can’t pass up.”

School Girls is in part inspired by an actual controversy that surrounded the Miss Ghana pageant in 2011 that saw an American-born woman compete in the pageant. “It was like a scandal in the Ghanaian news media because they were like, ‘Who is this light-skinned girl who is American and doesn’t know the culture?’ It was a scandal there,” Bioh says. In Bioh’s hands, School Girls manages to blend those events with elements inspired by Tina Fey’s classic 2004 film Mean Girls about the cutthroat nature of teenage girlhood, presenting a rip-roaring play that packs in an exploration of beauty standards, colorism, colonialism, opportunity, class, friendship, the African diaspora, and beauty pageant culture in a taught 80 minutes, with humor and pathos to spare.

Bioh and Denée Benton in rehearsal for School Girls.

A graduate of Ohio State and Columbia’s MFA playwriting program, Bioh appeared in the Broadway production of The Curious Incident of the Case of the Dog in the Night-Time in 2014, as well as multiple Off Broadway productions of works written by Pulitzer Prize–and Tony Award–winning playwright Branden Jacobs Jenkins. In 2017, she was nominated for a Lucille Lortel Award for outstanding featured actress in a play for Jenkins’s Everybody. A true multihyphenate, Bioh was still writing and producing plays throughout this time, including 2015’s Nollywood Dreams and School Girls, which was originally produced Off Broadway at MCC in 2017.

“It was never my intention to step away [from acting],” says Bioh. “I just went where the water was flowing. In this case, that original production of School Girls just did so well. It did so much better than I ever imagined. My life completely changed after opening night of that play. I can very clearly mark my career from the night before and the day after opening night.”

Many theater people both act and write—see Harvey Fierstein’s Torch Song Trilogy, Heidi Schreck’s What the Constitution Means to Me, and, of course, Lin-Manuel Miranda’s Hamilton. While it wasn’t necessarily her plan, with her stint in School Girls, Bioh proves she can throw herself into that realm if she so chooses. Her acting—grounded, natural, and honest—seems to be a natural extension of her writing, which often possesses a keen yet empathetic eye for sectors of the world that are often overlooked.

Bioh, Rogers, and Heather Alicia Simms in rehearsal for School Girls.

It’s no wonder her writing career took off after School Girls, taking her to Hollywood to write for television shows like Natasha Lyonne’s Russian Doll (2019) and Kathryn Hahn’s Tiny Beautiful Things (2023). Still, her heart belonged to the theater—and she made space for it. Bioh made her Broadway debut as a playwright in 2023 with Jaja’s African Hair Braiding, which would earn a Tony nomination for best play, a win for best costume design, and a Special Tony Award for hair and wig design.

Given Bioh’s rising star, it’s no surprise that Manhattan Theatre Club would want to revive Bioh’s breakthrough play, School Girls, for a larger Broadway audience. And although she wasn’t nervous, stepping back onto the stage for the first time in nearly a decade, Bioh was confronted with a well of emotions, specifically visions of her past and all the work that led up to that moment. “Me being a server at Carmine’s, checking coats at every restaurant in New York, handing out flyers for Shrek the Musical in Times Square—fighting with the girls who hand out flyers to Chicago, who always would do high kicks,” she says. “It was just like, every sort of crazy starving-artist struggle moment all just flashed before me. I just became so overwhelmed with emotion.”

Still, reacclimating to stage performance wasn’t easy. “We were so focused on the actual production of the play, I really put the whole stepping into it out of my mind until after opening night. We opened and I had a couple of down days, and that’s when I was like, Well, let me crack open the script and make sure I know all the lines.” Bioh had just one dialect session with the cast’s vocal coach and a single rehearsal before she stepped into the show on Friday, October 9.

At that rehearsal, which I attended, Bioh was confident and at ease, but not necessarily word-perfect. It helped that at least one actor on the stage, Heather Alicia Simms, who plays Aburi’s principal, had acted with Bioh years before. “You all know, Jocelyn used to be on these stages,” Simms said after a particularly delicious line reading from Bioh. Her Eloise is arguably as ruthless as the resident Regina George of the school, Paulina. She was Miss Ghana 1966, after all, and will not let anyone forget it.

“I just love really complicated women characters,” says Bioh. “I’m just fascinated with delving in deep and humanizing what I think some would categorize as a villain. I think that’s true of Paulina. I think that’s true of Eloise. I think that’s true of all the girls in the show. They all are multifaceted and have experiences that are weirdly and strangely universal.” While Bioh set out to tell the stories of a number of girls, in a way, she was telling a part of her own story. “My mother went to the boarding school that the play is set in. Both my parents are from Ghana, and she went to Aburi,” says Bioh. “I went to Milton Hershey School in Hershey, Pennsylvania, which is a scholarship-based boarding school. I always knew I wanted to write about my boarding school experience because it was a very specific experience. Most of us who went to that school had academic promise, but didn’t necessarily have the financial means to go to a good program—the school was 70 to 80% Black and brown kids. So I had a very unique boarding school experience, where I went to school with other people who looked like me.”

Benton, Erin Morton, Bioh, Lucia Aremu, Rogers, and Nia Otchere-Sarfo in rehearsal for School Girls.

The cast is composed entirely of Black women, and each actor in the cast gets a throughline and an individual moment to shine, including supporting characters like the confident Ama (Lucia Aremu), shy Nana (Erin Morton), bookish Mercy (Jordan Rice), and the scene-stealing class clown Gifty (Nia Otchere-Sarfo). A rousing musical rendition of Whitney Houston’s “Greatest Love of All” during their audition for Miss Eloise brings the house down, showcasing each girl’s unique comedic chops as well as their vocal prowess. Paulina and Ericka, the most talented singers of the group, go head-to-head with their solos, riffing and wailing attempting to best each other. They couldn’t be more different—Paulina, the Ghanaian mean girl trying to raise her station in life via the pageant, and Ericka, the new girl in school desperately trying to find a place where she fits in. And yet, in that moment, they both want the same thing: to be chosen, and to be seen. “Every Black person has not lived the same life,” Bioh adds. “That’s why it’s so ridiculous how we’re always pushed into these weird monolithic tropes.”

Bioh credits the strength of the cast with getting her through the sometimes terrifying ordeal of acting in her own work. “It’s the best company on Broadway, and they had my back one billion percent,” she says. “They were backstage cheering for me when I got off for my first scene. Anything I needed, they were there, and they were just so supportive.” It’s something she hasn’t necessarily gotten to experience in her dual role as writer and actor. “It’s actually quite special as a playwright. You never get the vantage point of being in the play with the actors you’ve created it with.”

Bioh made it through the weekend without a hitch—almost. “What I forgot and what I was reminded of this weekend is you always got to be prepared for a set piece or a costume piece to go awry. The first night, one of my earrings slipped off. Last night, my necklace slipped off,” she says. “I was like, I’m a pro. I’ve got this. I know how to seamlessly just pick up the earring and keep going. And that’s what I did.”

If you want to see School Girls, you have until November 15. But even those not able to get to New York are in luck—the play is livestreaming its final week of performances, from November 10 to November 15, for all the world to see. Although Bioh won’t be in those performances, now that she’s successfully returned to the stage for the first time in almost a decade, there’s only one question. Is the itch to perform back? “I don’t know about acting in my own stuff. I don’t know about that,” she says. “But never say never."

#3867. Awesome, pricy book for PlayStation’s 30th anniversary shows uncharacteristic appreciation for physical media

A new book celebrating PlayStation’s 30th anniversary was just revealed, and it looks pretty awesome.

PlayStation: The First 30 Years is “400 pages of gaming history,” showcasing “the definitive visual chronicle of Sony’s world-changing videogame console, marking its 30th anniversary with unprecedented access to the company’s archive of R&D prototypes, concept sketches, and development models.”

400 pages of gaming history.PlayStation: The First 30 Years is available to order now, shipping in November: https://t.co/5xfhSpw0Ob pic.twitter.com/T4Zwn8dHWL

And it comes with a fitting price tag, too. It has a “Deluxe Edition” at $325, and just 1,994 of them are being printed, “featuring exposed binding, a clamshell presentation box, and exclusive prints” (they’re even putting DLC in books now?). The base edition is $125.

“Alongside these striking images are insights from the engineers and designers who shaped PlayStation’s identity, including Ken Kutaragi, Teiyu Goto, and Yujin Morisawa, revealing the creative and technical decisions behind some of the most iconic hardware in gaming history,” the description reads.

In years past, I would’ve entertained the thought of picking up a book like this for my coffee table. It looks gorgeous, very sleek and simple in its outward design, and would make a great collector piece for fans of the brand. But unfortunately, the sentiment around PlayStation has soured a lot this year, and the irony of this physical media being sold at high prices while the company looks to do away with physical games in a few years is not lost on me, or just about anybody else.

Since July, the comment section on anything PlayStation or even Sony-adjacent has been flooded with anger. The trend continues here as the announcement of the book on social media was met with the ire you’d expect from the angry mob still looking for some acknowledgment, and it appears this sort of thing is not going away any time soon.

But until then, if players continue to purchase digital games and PS5 consoles, then it doesn’t seem like the company will pivot away from its controversial choice until its bottom line is hit hard.

#3866. Take This Step Now to Avoid Being Locked Out of Your Spotify Family Account

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

If you are on a Spotify Family plan, you may need to verify your location to keep your account active. Like many streaming services, Spotify is cracking down on family sharing for users who don't live in the same household by requiring QR code verification for some plan members—and once prompted, you must verify within seven days or risk being locked out of Family and Duo plans for a full year.

Spotify does require users on shared plans to verify their address when they join to confirm that they live with the plan manager and to re-verify if the manager changes addresses. Users may also be prompted via to re-enter their full address if Spotify "can't confirm" their location. This obviously doesn't prevent users from sharing plans, as Spotify isn't actually tracking your location but rather cross-checking addresses.

Some users may start seeing a QR code verification requirement, which has stricter parameters and may be more difficult to get around if plan members aren't in the same location. In these cases, the plan manager must open a QR code on their phone via Profile > Your Premium > Manage, and plan members must scan it with the camera on their phone.

According to Spotify's support page, both users must be in the same location to complete the scan, and the QR code changes every few seconds, making it hard to send screenshots to anyone not present in person. However, as 9to5Google notes, some users have been able to work around these restrictions with video calls, using multiple devices to complete the scans.

If you are prompted to complete location verification either by email or QR code, make sure you do it within seven days of the notification—otherwise you'll be lose access and be downgraded to a free plan. Spotify says that you can rejoin the same Family or Duo plan again, but you'll be restricted from joining a different shared plan for a full year.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.
