#3865. Composer shares 20 minutes of music created for Mass Effect 5 following tragic closure of BioWare

Austin Wintory has shared twenty minutes of music they pitched to BioWare and EA in hopes of joining the Mass Effect 5 development team, following the tragic closure of BioWare last week.

The composer, known for his work on Journey and Sword of the Sea, uploaded the music to his personal YouTube channel. It was made without knowledge of the narrative direction of the new Mass Effect game, but was presented as a surprise to the companies in 2025 as part of years of conversation. Wintory writes in the video that it was a "direct homage" to Mass Effect 1 composers Jack Wall and Sam Hulick, leaning into analog synths and "a more complex integration of the orchestra", a hybrid approach Wintory compares to The Matrix movies scores.

This collection of music includes a track called The Legacy of Shepard, which includes "deliberate winks" to the original Mass Effect theme. The second track - Sailing the Relays - features the blend of synth and orchestra mentioned earlier quite heavily. All in all, there are eight tracks included in total, with the eighth being a remix of that first Legacy of Shepard composition.

The video's description contains a sombre message on BioWare's closure, as well as gratitude towards the music departments involved in the conversations Wintory took part it: "Receiving the news alongside everyone else that EA had decided to not only cancel the project but shutdown BioWare entirely was a deeply felt loss. It's been hard to see dear friends lose their jobs, and this beloved franchise seemingly vanish forever.

"Thank you nonetheless to everyone at BioWare and EA's music department for gifting me the chance to dream in your sandbox. It was a privilege to do even that much."

Wintory is not alone in their sadness. EA's closure of BioWare, which included the firing of 50 employees and a further 350 across other EA-owned studios, many across the industry came forward to decry the decision. The decision may even have political ramifications, as one Canadian party leader has expressed a desire to allow BioWare employees to buy back the studio with federal support. This all happened shortly after EA's $55bn private acquisition from Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner and Saudi Arabia's Savvy Games Group.

#3864. Why Was Matthew Perry's Father Missing From Netflix Doc? Absences Explained

Netflix’s documentary about Matthew Perry featured interviews with his loved ones and former costars — but there were some noticeable absences, including his father, John Bennett Perry.

“Our dad has been ill for a long time,” Perry’s half-sister Mia Perry Bowick said in The One About Matthew Perry, which premiered Tuesday, October 27.

Perry Bowick noted that her and Perry’s dad would have loved “to be able to participate in helping to share the story of his son.”

“But unfortunately, he’s just not well enough to do that,” she continued. “It really fell to me to be able to talk about the Perry side of the family.”

The One About Matthew Perry is a three-part special that explores Perry’s life. In addition to examining his rise to fame with the role of Chandler Bing on Friends, the doc addressed the late actor’s private struggles with addiction, mental health and attempts to get sober before his shocking death at age 54 in October 2023.

Perry Bowick is the only member of Perry’s family who participated in the documentary. She was joined by his lifelong friends, including Roger Castillo and David Pressman, but noticeable absences included his mother, Suzanne Marie Morrison, stepfather, Keith Morrison, and his other half-siblings.

“I haven’t heard [my dad] talk like himself in so long,” Perry Bowick said in the doc. “My dad would say, ‘[Matthew] was a fighter. He never gave up. He was so strong.’ He would just hit the positives.”

Perry Bowick continued: “My dad was extremely proud of how hard Matthew worked to get well. And he backed him up 100 percent in anything he wanted to do to get well. We were all so grateful for Matthew for doing that — for never giving up and for always trying.”

Perry was candid about his battle with substance abuse through the years. He developed an addiction to Vicodin after a Jet Ski accident led him to begin abusing alcohol. Perry was hospitalized with pancreatitis in 2000 and made multiple attempts to get sober — entering treatment on several occasions.

While Perry’s loved ones largely opted out of participating in the Netflix doc, Morrison, 79, previously reflected on the actor’s death.

“As other people have told me hundreds of times, it doesn’t go away. It’s with you every day. It’s with you all the time, and there’s some new aspect of it that assaults your brain,” the Dateline correspondent told Hoda Kotb on the “Making Space” podcast in March 2024. “It’s not easy, especially for his mom.”

Morrison was asked whether he was surprised by the loss, to which he responded, “It was the news you never want to get, but you think someday you might. Yes and no, I guess is the answer to that.”

According to Morrison, Perry seemed to be “beating” his substance abuse struggles at the time of his death. “But you never beat it, and he knew that, too,” Morrison added.

If you or someone you know is struggling with substance abuse, contact the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration (SAMHSA) National Helpline at 1-800-662-HELP (4357).

#3863. Dungeons & Dragons: World of Warcraft's class designer 'crammed as much' of the MMO into it so that even veteran tabletop players can experience something new

The upcoming book introduces dozens of new subclasses, spells, and more to D&D's ruleset.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

James Wyatt, the principal game designer and project lead designer of the upcoming Dungeons & Dragons: World of Warcraft book was already running games in Azeroth long before he took on the project. For him, the fun of bringing WoW to the table is not to ignore the flexibility of D&D but to embrace it.

"Part of the beauty of playing D&D in the world of Azeroth is that you can do things in D&D that you can't do in a digital game because the options haven't been programmed for you," he said in a recent presentation attended by PC Gamer.

On November 12, you'll be able to see how Wyatt and the other designers mixed the two RPGs by "paraphrasing" the MMO into the tabletop ruleset. But Wyatt and senior game designer Ben Petrisor gave us a peek into how their approach affects the classes you can play in D&D: WoW.

WoW's classes are thematically similar to D&D's, and that's part of what makes the collaboration so fitting. However, WoW's classes have to fit into restrictive roles, like a tank or a healer, that don't really exist in D&D, so the D&D: WoW designers decided to return to some of the class customization that was introduced with 2020's Tasha's Cauldron of Everything.

Paladins won't have to give up their usual kit when they invest in WoW's holy specialization, for example. Wyatt explained that a lot of the unique abilities and talents WoW classes have access to have been translated into options players can incorporate into their characters. A holy paladin in D&D: WoW will still smite their foes into piles of ashes but they'll also have stronger ways to heal their allies than they normally would.

The same goes for classes like wizard that can pick up the Fingers of Frost feat at level 4 to lean harder into mastering frost-based spells like a frost mage would in the MMO. Beast master rangers can see what it's like to have a pet with its own weapon mastery on top of your own, emulating the tight bond WoW hunters have with their companions. There are dozens of new subclasses, spells, and additions to the core D&D classes like these, each pulling directly from the most iconic parts of WoW.

"We really crammed as much of the impactful talents across each WoW class into this book," Petrisor said.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Not only can you play WoW's unique hero classes—death knight, demon hunter, and evoker—you can experiment with new classes that Blizzard hasn't touched: Everyone who misses the early days of WoW when shamans could stand on the frontlines as magical warriors can pick up the barbarian subclass in D&D: WoW and channel their rage into elemental storms. Even bard, a class that was only an April Fool's joke in WoW, is fully playable in D&D: WoW.

The goal, Petrisor said, was to pull the restraints off of D&D's classes so that everyone, no matter which game you're most familiar with, can try something new.

"Even if you're coming into this without a lot of familiarity to WoW-specific classes, and you kind of want to just hit the ol' reliable button you'll still have an expanded experience and new and fun stuff to play with," he said.

Petrisor explained how the limitations of publishing a single, physical book were really the biggest hurdle when it came to imagining all the ways the two worlds could collide. But just like Wyatt stressed in the first preview presentation I attended, Petrisor has plenty of other ideas that he'd love to work on if people want more.

"If there's an IP that has the space to cover another book this is definitely one of them," he said. "So, the real challenge was—for me at least—while designing things was to try to find everything that would appease the fans of the game while also being options that would be interesting to D&D players and try to cram as many of those into this book as possible."

#3862. I Didn’t See It Coming When Jenna Ortega Admitted She Didn’t Think Wednesday Would Work

Carrie's Chris Actor Picked Her Favorite Scene From The Amazon Show, And I Co-Sign Her Choice

I Didn’t Have ‘Steel Rod Dress’ On My 2026 Bingo Card, But It Was The Perfect Look For Jenna Ortega

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Given Wednesday Addams has enough confidence to take on a serial killer (or two) and has enough left over to fill three coffins, you’d expect Jenna Ortega to carry the same self-assurance that she exudes on screen. The actor has often spoken about the understanding she has in playing such an icon, so I didn’t see it coming when she admitted she originally didn’t think Wednesday would work.

Turns out Ortega lacked her braid-wearing counterpart’s confidence. The Beetlejuice actor took us all the way back to Wednesday’s first year at Nevermore on the Happy Sad Confused podcast. When Josh Horowitz asked her about her experience in those first days, I didn’t see her answer coming (though I shouldn’t be surprised how it connects to Tim Burton):

Yeah, I didn't think that it was working at all. And that was me being in my head perhaps but also Tim. I had never worked with someone like Tim before. And Tim is very, very shy. He's opened up a bunch, but when you initially start working with him, he does not speak. We were doing hair and makeup tests and he would look at you know they would try different bangs or try different lengths of braids and he would call Colleen Atwood over and Colleen would…. He would whisper in Colleen’s ear, ‘Okay, I’m thinking this…’ And Colleen would come over and speak for him.

Given the success of the first two seasons, it never occurred to me that the actors may not feel as confident as the rest of the world. However, I can understand how this would feel like a rocky start, especially when first stepping into such an iconic character. Although Ortega’s performance is easily as popular as Christina Ricci’s. The actor shares the concerns she had first working with the Beetlejuice director, and I get it:

I think that was just him maybe being sensitive now knowing him. ‘Well, she's a young girl. I don't want her to feel like you know I'm picking her apart to come from a woman.’ Now, I know Tim. But it was things like that that just felt like, ‘Okay, well, I'm not clicking with him. He's not speaking to me.’ He also was just trying to give me space as a performer to figure it out myself. But to be 17, 18 in a foreign country with this very big director, and feel like I wasn't connecting. I was very stressed out.

The series’ popularity has taken it out of Nevermore and into a new location with the new season expected to take place in the City of Love (aka Paris). This will definitely take Wednesday out of her comfort zone, and I wonder if Ortega will be channeling her feelings when Season 1 turned out to be a massive hit:

I really couldn’t believe it [was a hit]. They had sent me like an episode or two to watch out of courtesy before the show came out and I couldn't make it through the first five minutes, 10 minutes. I just was so — and I don't really like to watch myself, I don’t. So I think it actually was doing a bit of a hindrance. But just based off of not having any faith in myself, right? I was mortified. So when people started freaking out over it or I started getting recognized or there was a sudden burst and sort of explosion and everything, it really felt like some sick mind came [played a prank].... There’s no way. Still, to this day, I’m a little confused.

It surprises me how much Ortega was caught off guard by the show’s success. On the other hand, I understand how things change. As of right now, we don’t know how long she’ll continue to wear the braids as her character is now entering her senior year and the rest of the cast is quickly aging out of the high school setting. However, she has fully embraced her Wednesday persona and you can tell how much relaxed she is with the role. Personally, I’d love to see Wednesday tackle the real world as a self-proclaimed PI that hunts down notorious supernatural threats and serial killers.

The only things we know for sure is that Season 3 will continue the hunt for Enid and dive further into the mystery that is Aunt Ophelia, especially since Eva Green was cast the role. Additionally, we’ll also see a Beetlejuice Beetlejuice reunion with Winona Ryder cast in a mysterious role. (Some fans are hoping she’ll play Agnes’ mom.)

We have a long wait ahead of us until Ortega is confused yet again at the love for Wednesday. I for one can’t wait to see some drama, some horror, and some dark humor when the show rises from the grave again. Plus, I'm dying to hear what Ortega has to say about Season 3.

#3861. BioShock Movie Is Still Happening, But Director Says It’s A “Tough Nut To Crack”

A BioShock movie at Netflix remains in development, its director has confirmed, but that doesn't necessarily mean it'll ever be made. Francis Lawrence confirmed to The Direct that he hopes to make the BioShock movie, but "it's tricky on a couple fronts."

Lawrence said he is "definitely active" in working on the BioShock movie, but "it is a tough nut to crack that world and that story."

"You are the character when you play the game, and so creating that character from scratch into that world that, you know, quite honestly, is pretty batsh** in a most amazing way," he said. "It's tricky. So we're getting there, and we're getting close, which is great."

The BioShock movie was originally set to be a large-scale production, but when Netflix film chief Dan Lin came in, the project was scaled back. Lin recently left Netflix amid a major shakeup at the streaming giant, as did longtime chief content officer Bela Bajaria. Former Disney and Fox executive Peter Rice is now the new chief content officer, and Lawrence hopes the management shakeups do not affect the BioShock movie in a big way, but nothing is ever certain in Hollywood.

"I would love nothing more than to be able to make that movie," Lawrence said of BioShock. "So still working on it. And then, you have also just the Netflix of it all because Netflix right now has had regime changes and just all of that kind of nonsense that I don't like to have to deal with or have to. So we'll see. Hopefully we'll figure it out."

Lawrence's latest project is the Hunger Games movie Sunrise on the Reaping, which releases in November. Asked if BioShock would be his next project, Lawrence said, "You never know." He said working on multiple films simultaneously creates a "horse race" kind of scenario where factors like the script, talent availability, and production realities need to align for projects to go ahead.

Curiously, BioShock producer Roy Lee said the BioShock movie is planned to coincide with the release of a new BioShock game. Lee did not name any specific games outright, but Take-Two recently reiterated that the long-in-development BioShock 4 will see the light of day … some day.

#3860. Art Basel Paris Opened—Then Closed Early, Due to Protests. What Happened?

Read True Colors: Inside the global deals and colorful critiques that make the art world go round, with a weekly guide to the must-see openings, auctions, and fairs.

I was walking on the banks of the Seine, on my way to meet Jenny Holzer. When I arrived, she was staring at her own artwork, which was being projected onto the facade of the Musée d’Orsay.

“First we came and stalked it,” Holzer said of the museum, which holds some of the world’s greatest Monets, Manets, van Goghs, and Gauguins. “It’s good to walk and look and walk and look. Slow thinking. But after I cased the joint on the seventh time, I go, ‘Oh, I should use the words of the artists and the associates of the period of the museum.’”

She stared at the projections, which spread phrases like “I AM SEEKING THE IMPOSSIBLE” across the old railway station built for the 1900 World’s Fair. Her show inside the museum, “J’ai Vu,” is up through February, and features Holzer paintings installed in a room dedicated to historical works, with one of her signature LED scrolling beams installed overhead. But it’s those projected words that all of Paris will see—until the end of the day today.

As we sat on the banks of the Seine Tuesday, Art Basel Paris had just opened its exclusive Avant Première showing to select collectors at the Grand Palais. By Thursday, the fair would officially cancel its final two days due to planned protests targeting Art Basel, its collectors, and its ownership.

Holzer and I didn’t know any of that at the time, as we looked at the phrases projected on the buildings. They’re lifted from correspondence written by the artists, poets, writers, and curators at the museum.

ÉCLAIRÉ, 2026 Light projection Musée d’Orsay, Paris.

I asked Holzer to describe the messages she found in that correspondence. Her answer was warm, funny, and characteristically aphoristic: “‘War again.’ ‘I’m broke.’ ‘Everyone hates me.’ ‘I hate myself.’ ‘My work's not good enough.’ ‘I can’t rise to the occasion when things are so desperate.’ ‘Nature is greater than I, because…’ Dot, dot, dot. Ellipses galore. Ellipses abounding.”

No artist has made text-based artwork more famous than Jenny Holzer. Her truisms—PROTECT ME FROM WHAT I WANT—were bold when she started making them in the ’70s. They became eerily predictive of soundbites and meme culture, then became part of the pop cultural swirl.

The artist behind the billboards and the LED lights is not usually one for the spotlight—she gives few interviews and lives in the Northeast, far away from New York art dinner circles. But while Holzer and I talked, another passerby recognized her and approached her, slowly.

“Howdy! I’m American too,” the passerby said. “To me, this is so fucking prescient. I didn’t get to come yesterday. I wanted to come and sit here and see it.”

Holzer’s work has always been prescient. But it’s uncanny, here in Paris this week, how her charged words seem reflected in and reflexive of the protests and subsequent destruction that happened in Paris last weekend, and will continue tomorrow. She started installing the projections last Saturday, as daytime student demonstrations gave way to fires and looting. I arrived in the city that Saturday evening to texts from friends saying they were caught up in tear gas while riding the metro and walking in front of Christie’s. Dinner reservations were canceled when bonfires came perilously close to consuming the restaurants’ entryways.

I successfully made it to dinner Sunday night and was ensconced in a back room as looters burst through the capillaries of the Marais with police in pursuit. (I didn’t hear about that part till later.) On my ride home, I passed a group of people in masks standing by a pile of burning Lime bikes, throwing lighter fluid onto the pyre, grabbing trash cans to add to the flames.

Holzer told me she couldn’t go to the installation of the projection on Saturday night because she wasn’t allowed to leave her hotel. No one was allowed to leave the hotel.

“So I was like, ‘Oh, okay. I guess if they have the gates down, that means something,’” she said.

When we spoke, on Tuesday evening, the dusk was washing over the Seine. The demonstrations had subsided. Art Basel Paris had opened earlier that day. The anti-ram barriers fortifying the space worked. But according to reports, demonstrations were set to resume imminently. I asked Holzer what she made of this.

“Idealistically, and purely, and of necessity, what the students started was good,” Holzer said as we stared at her words on the Musée d’Orsay.

“I’m not sure ransacking stores is all that hot,” she added, staring ahead. “Or adding to the burning for less holy ends than education.”

Last year at Art Basel Paris, in the days before the fair began, thieves dressed as construction workers broke into the Louvre and stole €88 million in irreplaceable jewelry, forcing the world’s most famous museum to shut its doors. (Certain VIPs still got tours while it was closed to the public.) On the ground at the fair, the heist was all anyone could talk about.

This year, as the art world arrived in Paris, all they could talk about was the protests. The topic dominated every dinner table conversation on Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday. From what I heard, attendees were largely sympathetic to the demonstrators and their goals, if strongly against the destruction that followed. There were also practical concerns for the art viewer: Demonstrators shut down the Louvre Monday morning. Stan Douglas’s opening at the Jeu de Paume was postponed on Sunday evening.

The Grand Palais shut down Saturday during the worst of the trouble, and reopened Sunday. The fair fanned out beefy security guards through the halls and around the perimeter. And then…the entire facility emerged completely unscathed. Actually, everything in the Art Basel orbit did. No galleries reported damage; neither did the auction houses.

But there were more protests brewing. By Tuesday, different groups were making it clear that they planned to demonstrate the following Saturday again. This time, they would target Art Basel directly and congregate at the fair’s site at the Grand Palais.

Protesters, including students from the Ecole du Louvre, block an entrance to the Louvre Museum to express their "support" for the high school students' movement and to denounce police repression on Oct. 19.

According to the Instagram page @Documentations_art, a group calling themselves Pillage de Classe (or “Class Plunder”) published a manifesto decrying, as they framed it, tax laws that allow art collectors to write off their support for contemporary art.

“Last year, the price of the most expensive work sold at Art Basel was €23 million—meaning €14 million could be deducted from taxes if the buyer were French,” it reads. It’s not clear exactly which tax law this is referring to, but it appears that corporations and individuals in France can claim some amount of tax deductions when they purchase fine art. The work mentioned is apparently a Gerhard Richter abstract painting from 1987 that sold off the Hauser & Wirth booth in 2025 for $23 million—dollars, not euros.

The group alluded to some unsubstantiated, alleged nebulous connections—which I have not been able to uncover in any sort of tangible way—between the fair ownership, Palantir, the Pentagon, and the US Department of Defense.

“Gala dinners are no longer tolerable. We can no longer fill champagne glasses in order to buy tanks with the budgets meant for education and healthcare,” the statement from the group said. “Events such as Art Basel perpetuate imperialism and the power of the transnational bourgeoisie by fostering the political and military conditions that enable its enrichment, at the cost of genocide in Palestine.”

Another group, Cultures en Lutte (“Cultures in Struggle”), posted that the collectors at Art Basel had been gathering despite the demonstrations in the city, “gleefully [taking] advantage of tax breaks on millions of dollars that could otherwise be used to fund public services.”

On Friday, Cultures en Lutte posted that they no longer planned to protest. But there’s every indication that Pillage de Classe will march on the Grand Palais this Saturday at 4 p.m.

Sources said the Instagram algorithm was pushing these manifestos to the top of their feeds. Word of the planned Art Basel-targeting demonstrations spread through the fair. By Wednesday night, according to sources, some galleries had begun preparing to pack up the art early, cancellation or not—given the massive insurance liability of having even peaceful protests at the fair gates. By early Thursday, word trickled out that Art Basel top brass was aware of the planned demonstrations and had decided to cancel.

By the afternoon, the official word came.

In an email to the participating galleries Thursday afternoon, Art Basel fairs director Vincenzo de Bellis and Art Basel Paris director Karim Crippa said that the Paris fair would cancel the final weekend of its 2026 edition, shutting down at 7 p.m. Friday “in light of potential demonstrations across Paris this weekend, and in the interest of safeguarding our visitors.”

Inside the Art Basel Paris bubble on Monday and Tuesday, things appeared to go ahead as if nothing bad would happen. Since it moved back to the Grand Palais in 2024, this fair has been the most desirable art-buying destination for collectors—combining the allure of A-plus hardcore masterpieces, à la the flagship fair in Switzerland, with the party-happy circuit of restaurants and hotel lobbies found adjacent to the group’s fair in Miami Beach. Even in the shadow of the demonstrations, Art Basel Paris was exactly that. There didn’t appear to be a single chink in the armor. The museums around town were packed to the point where you couldn’t move.

The morning after the demonstrations, tourists dutifully lined up around the Louvre. Even after bypassing the queue, I had to wait to enter the galleries, and of course to see the Mona Lisa. It was easier to get to the museum’s small show of work by Elizabeth Peyton, made while she was in residence at the Louvre, including portraits of Jennifer Lawrence and Chopin. I had never seen François Pinault’s Bourse de Commerce this crowded before, especially in a room with Irving Penn photographs installed chockablock up the walls. After arriving at the Fondation Louis Vuitton for a show of works once in the holdings of French collector Gustave Fayet, I dutifully stood sentry as visitors took their pictures of the van Gogh self-portrait before getting a blissful minute with The Large Plane Trees, on loan from the Cleveland Museum.

The galleries saw no downturn in visitors. According to sources, the show of Lee Krasner works at Gagosian, presented with the Pollock Krasner Foundation and Olney Gleason, was among the Paris gallery’s best-attended openings ever. Gallerygoers thronged through the Marais Monday afternoon, making their way to see the astounding collection of Cy Twombly works at Karsten Greve. None were for sale—but if overheard chatter is to be believed, that wasn’t stopping a big-pocketed sovereign wealth fund from attempting to make a purchase. David Zwirner also opened a large survey of new work by Oscar Murillo there, while not one but two satellite fairs opened in the neighborhood: Post-Fair, an offshoot of the Santa Monica expo that’s held during Frieze Los Angeles, and Paris Internationale, the longstanding young gallery fair in town.

The spree of meals and parties continued unabated—most prominently a dinner celebrating Alice Diop’s “Geste de Consolation,” an exhibition commissioned by Miu Miu as part of the Art Basel Official Program. The show incorporates a new video by Diop and a wildly inventive installation that will host a series of performances throughout the week. Dinner, as any good Paris dinner should be, was held at Brasserie Lipp, long the fashion world’s preferred old-world bistro, right across from Café de Flore.

Mrs. Prada, who personally oversees all of the art commissions for Miu Miu and Prada—and who of course runs one of the world’s great private art concerns, the Prada Foundation—held court at a table in the back of Brasserie Lipp, sitting with Serpentine director Hans Ulrich Obrist, Neue Nationalgalerie director Klaus Biesenbach, LUMA founder Maja Hoffmann, curator Nancy Spector, artists Francesco Vezzoli and Arthur Jafa and Meriem Bennani; and Art Basel contingent represented by Global CEO Noah Horowitz, director of fairs Vincenzo de Bellis, and Art Basel Paris director Karim Crippa.

On Tuesday, Art Basel let a few select viewers into the fair a day early—part of a special event in its second year called Avant Première. The premise: Each gallery disburses a few passes to its primo clients. The crowd did seem especially hand-selected. I saw Bernard Arnault, LVMH’s chairman and CEO, inside the Nahmad Projects booth, checking out works by Jean Dubuffet. He was one of the few collectors present who could have easily snapped up the fair’s most expensive work: a Modigliani at the Pace Di Donna Schrader booth priced at $100 million—though as of this writing the work had not yet been sold.

Arnault’s daughter, Delphine Arnault, was on hand as well. James Murdoch, who is the majority stakeholder in the fair’s parent company MCH Group, was chatting with Iwan Wirth. I walked in behind George Economou, the shipping magnate with a wildly important private museum in Athens, and once inside, I saw the local collector Patricia Marshall walk past the Paula Cooper Gallery booth as American collector Glenn Fuhrman walked by the exit.

Were they buying the work? Evidently, someone was. Hauser & Wirth sold two different paintings, a Gerhard Richter and a Georg Baselitz, for around $10 million each on Tuesday. David Zwirner sold more than $36 million in work on Tuesday and Wednesday, including a Richter painting for $8.5 million. Gagosian sold a 1987 de Kooning for eight figures. White Cube sold two different Julie Mehretu works for $7.85 million each.

And galleries were happy to provide examples of works by the artist with the most visible work in Paris: the only artwork projected on an entire stretch of the riverbank. Spruth Magers, which first started showing Holzer in 1986, brought a rare Holzer collaborative work with the graffiti artist Lady Pink that declares on canvas, “IF YOU ARE CONSIDERED USELESS NO ONE WILL FEED YOU ANYMORE.” Hauser & Wirth sold Providing, a 2024 work in 24k gold, antique gold, champagne gold, moon gold, platinum and red gold leaf, for $400,000.

Holzer was not actually at this unprecedented brief edition of Art Basel Paris. While Avant Première unfolded Tuesday, she was on the banks of the Seine, taking in the projections, musing about her French artist heroes.

“Monet was doubting whether his work was good enough even after he was successful, and wondered whether it measured up to the times,” she said. “He was agonized. Good man. This is when he was in water lily land. He was nailing it, and he was agonized. Which is why it was that good, because he wasn’t going out for a drinky-poo.”

I watched French text from Monet’s writings scroll up the Orsay.

“Not such a shabby facade,” Holzer said. “And look…”

One of the ferries going down the Seine came into view, in front of the museum. Holzer pointed.

“When the tourist boat goes by, the phones come out. So that’s kind of reassuring, right?” she said.

ÉCLAIRÉ, 2026 Light projection at Musée d’Orsay.

The projections are only temporary. But like her other ephemeral text-light-on-building installations, they will exist in images, and remain in the minds of those who see them. Maybe this work will remain tethered to this period in French history, as students protest for better education in a city that still reveres art and culture.

Even with the fair cut short by the protests, Holzer, as you might be pleasantly surprised to hear, is optimistic.

“There’s chronic persistent idealism in a lot of this content. Maybe that’ll be contagious,” she said. “That’s a dream, anyway.”

#3859. Savannah Chrisley Announces She Is Pregnant, Engaged to Mystery Fiancé

Savannah Chrisley has shared she is expecting her first baby with her now-fiancé: “Just when I thought my heart couldn’t hold any more love, God gave us our sweetest blessing yet…we’re having a BABY!"

Savannah Chrisley is indeed grown up—and celebrating a new chapter.

The Growing Up Chrisley alum, 29, has announced she is expecting her first baby and later revealed on her Unlocked podcast that she is also engaged.

“I’ve prayed for this life. I’ve cried for this life. And there were moments I truly wondered if this life would ever be mine,” Savannah captioned an Oct. 27 video shared to Instagram featuring a montage of her and her boyfriend. “The past few years have taken me through fires I never imagined I’d have to walk through. Seasons of heartbreak, loss, and moments when I didn’t know if I would ever make it to the other side. I wondered if I would ever find my forever…if I would ever feel safe enough to truly breathe again.”

Savannah—who has kept her partner’s identity over the past year under wraps, but shared on her podcast that his name is Jason—noted, “And then God sent me J.”

“Right in the middle of the heartbreak,” she continued. “Before all the pieces were put back together. And somehow, he began healing parts of me he never broke. My protector. My fiercest fighter. My most loyal person. My lover. My safe place. A man with the patience of Job (and Lord knows he needs it). The one who makes me laugh even on my hardest days.”

The podcast host—who split from Robert Shiver in 2025—added, “For the first time in a long time, I feel safe. I feel fulfilled. I can finally exhale. I found my forever in you.”

“And just when I thought my heart couldn’t hold any more love,” she wrote, “God gave us our sweetest blessing yet… we’re having a BABY!”

The Chrisley Knows Best alum went on to note how her boyfriend has been by her side every step of the way.

“J, watching the way you already love and protect our baby before he/she has even entered this world has made me fall in love with you in an entirely new way,” she continued. “Not a single doctor’s appointment missed. Constantly caring for me, talking about our baby, and reminding me how beautiful my growing bump is. I thank God every day that this baby gets to call you Daddy.”

Savannah ended her note by sharing that she “once prayed just to make it through another day. Now I get to spend forever with the love of my life, carrying a little miracle we already love more than words.”

“From heartbreak to healing. From surviving to living. From praying for my forever to building a family with him,” she concluded. “Only God could have written this story. Here’s to forever, J… and the sweetest little blessing we can’t wait to meet.”

#3858. GTA 6 pre-load dates and file size: When can you download the game on PS5

Less than a month remains before GTA 6 goes live, and everyone is eagerly watching for breadcrumbs about what the final experience is like. While Rockstar remains tight-lipped about the final details, dataminers reportedly uncovered the game’s size and pre-load date for PlayStation 5.

None of this information is confirmed by Rockstar, but the folks behind PlayStation Game Size on Twitter/X have reportedly got the goods. Here’s what they are saying.

As of writing, the reported file size for GTA 6 on PlayStation 5 is 210 GB, but this remains unconfirmed. We also don’t yet know anything about the figure on Xbox. It’s going to be a large download either way, that’s a fair assumption.

#3857. Pokémon Unite closing down for good, but you'll still be able to use it afterwards, sort of

That's it for Pokémon Unite. Tencent's once-popular MOBA is being switched off in March 2027. The Pokémon Company announced the news today, saying: "We regret to inform you that service for Pokémon Unite will end on Tuesday, March 23, 2027, at 00:00 UTC."

By way of thanking people for playing, a "special event" runs from now until service-end, which brings a number of additions to the game. Among them is an intriguing Library feature that lets you continue using the game, to a degree, after its end.

The Library allows you to access the Practice Area of Pokémon Unite to view your earned items - Holowear, cosmetics, Pokémon, and achievements - so your efforts in the game won't entirely disappear. It'll be available starting 12th November. But note that you'll need to use the Library before the 23rd March 2027 switch-off date to get the data Library needs to show your achievements, and because that data's stored locally, you may lose the data if you change device.

"Library data generally cannot be transferred when changing devices," the Pokémon website said. "If the game or library data is deleted or otherwise lost after service ends, it may not be recoverable. Support for the library feature will not be available after service ends."

It seems like a thoughtful way to sunset a game, allowing players a record of their progress and some interactivity, instead of yanking everything away entirely.

Pokémon Unite launched in 2021, first on Switch and then on mobile devices. It's a free-to-play MOBA, essentially, where two teams of five battle it out in an arena with various routes through it and control points to secure and, as Pokémon battle, they earn experience and level up. Pokémon Unite had already been shut down in some European countries because of loot box gambling concerns.

#3856. Beware of These iPhone Duo Preorder Scams

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

iPhone Duo preorders officially launched last week to high demand, a quick sellout, and delayed delivery estimates. Naturally, scammers are finding ways to capitalize on the rush to obtain Apple's new foldable, so if you're in the market for a new device, be on the lookout for signs of compromise.

Soon after the iPhone Duo was announced, threat actors jumped into action, setting up fake preorder pages that looked like Apple's official website but were actually designed to deliver a malicious payloads to your device. As Malwarebytes outlined in late September, these pages promised a $500 voucher and AppleCare+ coverage in exchange for your contact information. But submitting the form isn't actually the scam. Rather, simply opening the page launches the attack, which uses the DarkSword exploit chain to bypass iPhone protections and gain access to cryptocurrency wallets, messages, contacts, emails, calendar entries, and location data.

This scam began several weeks ago and seemed to specifically target iPhones running iOS 18.4 to 18.6.2. Apple has said that devices running the latest versions of iOS 15 to 26 are protected from the exploit, so if your iPhone is up to date, you are likely at minimal risk for this specific scheme. That said, beware of websites and social media posts that promise access to the iPhone Duo faster or cheaper than other sources, as this is almost certainly a scam designed to steal your information or install malware on your device. Official sources for preorders include Apple itself (through the official Apple website or Apple Store app) as well as major cell carriers.

Another scam to be on the lookout for involves fraudulent charges for an iPhone Duo you didn't order. News outlets in Hong Kong have reported that shoppers are seeing transactions from Apple appear on their bank and credit card statements—even when they haven't actually tried to buy the device since preorders went live. This may be the work of bad actors who have harvested credit card details via phishing or data leaks and are using them for fraudulent purchases.

While this specific scheme doesn't appear to be widespread (yet), you should check your financial statements and accounts regularly anyway given how vulnerable your data are to theft and fraud. If you see a charge you don't recognize or duplicate charges, report it to your bank or card issuer immediately using contact information from a trusted app or client portal.

Scammers will also take the threat of fraudulent charges one step further: They may contact you directly about supposedly suspicious transactions and claim they can help resolve the issue if you provide personal information or account credentials. With the iPhone Duo specifically, bad actors may also call pretending to be Apple or a carrier and ask you to confirm information about your order, possibly to avoid having it canceled. As always, any communication that provokes fear and a sense of urgency should raise red flags. If you're not sure who you're talking to, hang up and call back directly to confirm.

#3852. The Wolf Among Us Remastered Doesn’t Change Much, And That’s A Good Thing

The Wolf Among Us 2 is finally landing in 2027, and if you want to catch up on what many consider one of Telltale Games' best exports, you can grab The Wolf Among Us Remastered on October 29. It's the original game spruced up for new hardware and enhanced with several extras, but is it a necessary purchase? Yes and no, depending on what you expect from this package.

GameSpot senior video producer Jean-Luc Seipke went hands-on with the remaster, and in the video below, you can hear their thoughts on this Telltale classic. So how much of a difference does 13 years make? With The Wolf Among Us Remastered, Telltale has focused on subtle improvements across the board. The goal was to retain the original game's look and feel while improving lighting, adding new animations where needed, making the protagonist Bigby's werewolf transformation more intimidating, and making other nuanced tweaks under the hood.

Compared to the original, the difference isn't night and day, but the extra work adds a new visual layer to a game that already had a timeless look thanks to its strong art direction. If you're unfamiliar with the source material, The Wolf Among Us adapts Fables, an award-winning comic book series that ran for 13 years and chronicled the exploits of a secret society of exiled fairy tale characters in New York. A big hit for DC's Vertigo imprint, the series ran from 2002-2015 and made a short-lived comeback in 2022.

Beyond the graphical update, The Wolf Among Us Remastered also packs plenty of extra content. Noir Mode dresses the game in a monochrome filter, adds several deleted and extended scenes, and lets you go behind the scenes with developer commentary, concept art, interviews, and other archival materials. If that content doesn't matter to you, then the remaster might not be a necessary upgrade.

That said, Telltale is launching the game at an attractive $25 price, with a launch-week discount and even deeper savings if you already own the game. So for a few bucks more, the remastered edition is the more tempting offer, and Telltale says that it won't remove the original game from storefronts either. Progress in The Wolf Among Us Remastered will also affect how the story plays out in The Wolf Among Us 2, but as of now, we haven't heard back from Telltale on whether choices made in the original game will carry over. 

Whichever version you choose, you're going to have a good time. The Wolf Among Us is an all-time classic from the Telltale library, and the remaster looks like a fun way to relive that story--or experience it for the first time--before the big bad wolf huffs and puffs down the door for his return next year.

#3849. Swipe Right on the Devil and the Real-Life Tinder Murder Behind It

It’s been almost a decade since 24-year-old Sydney Loofe disappeared after connecting with a woman named Bailey Boswell on Tinder. After a single in-person date, Loofe would later learn that she had actually been messaging with a man named Aubrey Trail, who referred to himself as a “powerful warlock,” at the head of a “coven,” which was, in reality, a coercive sex cult and criminal enterprise with Boswell as one of his “witches.” Boswell and Trail were in a romantic relationship themselves, a Bonnie and Clyde of online dating in small-town Nebraska. Within days of their first Tinder messages, Loofe was killed as a “sacrifice” by the coven’s leaders, her body dismembered and dumped in trash bags around a plot of rural land in small-town Nebraska.

In Swipe Right on the Devil, a new Ron Howard–produced, three-part true-crime series now streaming on Hulu, filmmaker Brian Biegel recounts the story of “witchcraft and its sister, black magic” in Wilber, Nebraska, as it “meets the modern-day digital dating world,” he tells Vanity Fair.

As part of the police investigation into Loofe’s death, authorities discovered that she had been recruited as the 13th member of Trail and Boswell’s “coven,” in which various women they met on dating apps were used for local thefts and sex in return for “love” and some money. “The headline is witchcraft, cult. But at its core is this more universal story of looking for belonging,” says producer Christopher St. John. “Obviously there’s the cautionary tale about tech.”

“With AI, it’s only going to get worse,” says executive producer and fellow Imagine Entertainment exec Sara Bernstein. “You really don’t know who you can trust.”

The documentary features interviews from multiple women who were scammed by Trail and Boswell, one of whom speaks in the third person to distance herself from the PTSD of her memories. “As documentarians, you want there to be a greater social value or conversation that can come,” says Bernstein, “as opposed to just retelling, frankly, what can be really graphic and gory details and having people relive their pain and suffering.”

In the documentary, Sydney’s sister Mackenzie Loofe expresses regret that she and her family didn’t offer more support regarding her sister’s sexuality. “Dating as a queer woman in middle America, there’s not a whole lot to choose from. Their way of coming out was through a dating app,” says Biegel. “So that alone made them vulnerable, because they really wanted to find their person. They wanted to feel a sense of belonging, and Sydney did. But what they couldn’t know, what was on the other side of the screen, was basically a trap waiting for them.”

The Loofe family “appreciated the opportunity to set the record straight” about Sydney, given what they felt was misleading press coverage around the case at the time of her murder, says St. John. “Sydney was misrepresented in their eyes as someone who was either involved in a crazy sex ring or involved in some sort of witchcraft, which she wasn’t,” he says. “She didn’t know these people, hadn’t met these people. It was this web of lies.”

The director spoke to Trail “on a daily basis for over a year until he agreed to give us audio and then eventually some video,” says Biegel. “Look, I watched the interviews the FBI had done with him, and he’s a game player. I also know he loves praise. So in order for me to build a rapport, I knew he was an artist, so I’m like, ‘Oh man, would you send me a painting?’ And his eyes opened up. I won him over.”

Once they had secured Trail’s participation from behind bars, they had to ensure it was used responsibly. “It’s a constant questioning, frankly, of everything he says, anything he’s promising,” says St. John. “We didn’t want to be another group of people manipulated by this guy, even from death row.”

Trail was sentenced to the death penalty following his trial in the summer of 2019. Boswell was convicted of first-degree murder and improper disposal of human remains in the fall of 2020 and was sentenced to life in prison the following year. “Bailey, we can say safely and without any doubt, is an incredibly complicated character. She wouldn’t talk to police, she wouldn’t talk to reporters, she wouldn’t talk to us,” says St. John. “She is a phantom at the center of this case. Since the trial, it seems she’s found it better to keep her cards close to her vest.”

As for those who escaped Trail and Boswell, “after experiencing a very harrowing issue in their lives, the girls in Aubrey’s group and even the Loofe family—everyone involved in this case—have gone on and lived happy, successful lives,” says Biegel. “And there’s an important message in that. People are more resilient than you think—they overcame a lot, and continue to do so.”

#3848. Fan favourite map Shipment arrives today in Modern Warfare 4, just four days after launch - a new record for Call of Duty

Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 is getting a small update today that includes the latest iteration of Infinity Ward's iconic Shipment map. The map arrives just four days after Modern Warfare 4's launch last Friday, 23rd October, which is unusually quickly.

Shipment is the original Call of Duty 'small map'. First introduced in Call of Duty 4: Modern Warfare in 2007, the map is designed to offer a breakneck pace of combat no other multiplayer map could. Though there have been Shipment-like small maps over the years - chiefly Treyarch's infamous Nuketown in the Black Ops sub-franchise - nothing could quite replicate Shipment's dizzying rhythm of mayhem.

Shipment's arrival in Modern Warfare 4 was expected, of course; the developer even announced the 27th October release date in a blog post prior to launch. However, joining the roster so close to the game's launch is notable.

Shipment typically arrives two or so weeks post-launch, usually in a game's first live service season. It's been part of Modern Warfare 2019, Modern Warfare 2 (2022), and Modern Warfare 3 (2023). A few other COD titles have versions inspired by it, though not entirely identical, such as COD: WW2's Shipment 1944.

It's long been assumed, perhaps cynically, that Shipment comes just as player numbers start falling off. As one of the series' most popular maps, the thinking is that bringing it back attracts a cohort of the audience who only play that map. It's often used to level up guns, and help get player levels up since the kill count is usually higher than in other, larger maps.

Indeed, Shipment typically releases alongside a dedicated, 24/7 playlist, meaning you don't have to hope for it to show up in the normal map rotation. Infinity Ward didn't say whether we can expect the same playlist this time around, though it is likely.

As with each new iteration, Modern Warfare 4 Shipment features the classic layout but in an entirely new environment inspired by locations from the game. This version has a more urban landscape look that takes after the very first version's muted colours, with Soviet-era concrete buildings in the background. The action takes place in a shipping yard by the harbour, among shipping containers, hence the name.

Incidentally, Modern Warfare 4 is also starting its first season early, with Season One kicking off 6th November, just two weeks after the game's launch, though it is curious Infinity Ward didn't wait until the season's launch to drop the map.

#3847. Why Your Smartwatch Size Really Does Matter

“Is my watch too big?” is a constant question on forums dedicated to smartwatches and fitness trackers of all kinds. (Some, but fewer, wonder if their watch is too small.) Personally, I don’t much care how a watch looks, but I’ve noticed that size can make a big difference in how accurately a watch functions. If your watch is too big, your numbers may be off.

Watches measure heart rate through an optical sensor on the back of the watch. (That’s what you see glowing green back there.) The heart rate sensor detects how that light reflects off the blood vessels in your skin. To get a proper reading, it needs to be snug against your skin, ideally in a way that blocks out ambient light.

If the sensor loses contact with your skin, or if too much light sneaks in, heart rate readings may be inaccurate. If a watch fits you well to start with, you can generally get an accurate heart rate by simply making sure the watch is snug during workouts.

But if a watch is too big, it can move around, letting light in. When you run, if a watch is bouncing around, the sensor may detect a flash of light with every footstep. This can lead to your watch reporting a heart rate that is the same as your cadence (footsteps per minute), which is sometimes called “cadence lock.” Some runners will cover their watch with a sleeve (especially in cold weather) to minimize this possibility.

No matter how you wear it, a big watch on a small wrist is likely to have a lot more difficulty than one that fits properly. Very small devices can have a similar problem: The device isn’t large enough or can’t be tightened enough to block light. This isn’t usually a problem with wristwatches, but it may explain why smart rings tend to be so poor at daytime heart rate tracking.

Now, is this a real-life concern, or just a theoretical quibble? I wasn’t sure, until I took the Forerunner 265 and 265S for a run at the same time. These are the same watch, with the same heart rate sensor, differing only in size. You can read about my results here, but the graph tells the story pretty well: The smaller 265S (42 mm) captured my heart rate more accurately than the 265 (46 mm).

If heart rate accuracy were the only issue, I would tell you to get the smallest watch you can find. It wouldn’t even look weird; before smartwatches became popular, wristwatches were often much smaller than a person’s wrist. (Watch old movies and you’ll see some tiny watches, even on people who don’t have tiny wrists.)

But watches are big and seem to be getting bigger. A bigger screen means numbers, text, and icons are easier to read. This is great news if your vision isn’t the best anymore, or if you use the watch while exercising and want to be able to see what’s on your screen with a quick glance.

Bigger watches can provide bigger screens, but also bigger batteries and more room for other internals. This is probably at least partly why more “premium” watches come in bigger sizes. That said, it’s hard for me to distinguish when a watch is huge because it needs room to pack in its hardware features, versus when the company is making the watch bigger so it will seem fancier and thus justify a higher price.

And this brings me to something I need to rant about. Smartwatch and fitness watch companies know that watch size matters: They often offer two sizes for at least some of their models. The Apple Watch Series 12 comes in 42 or 46 millimeters, while the Garmin Forerunner 570 comes in 42 or 47 millimeters, to give a couple of examples.

But when it comes to premium models, those options often disappear. The Garmin Forerunner 970, far more feature-rich than the 570, only comes in a 47-millimeter size, matching the larger size of the 570 but without a smaller option. The Apple Watch Ultra 4 only comes in a 49-millimeter size, which is even larger than the larger size of the Series 12. I’m about to start testing a Hyrox feature that only comes on Amazfit’s Balance 3 or Balance Ultra watches; those watches are 51 and 52 millimeters, respectively. This is getting ridiculous.

As a woman reporting on tech, I can’t ignore the way gender plays into this. According to ANSUR II, a U.S. Army study, women have an average wrist size of 154 millimeters (about 6.1 inches), and men have an average wrist size of 176 mm (about 6.9 inches). My wrists are right around 6 inches, making me very average for a woman.

When companies make two sizes of watch, the smaller size usually fits the average woman’s wrist fairly well, and the larger size is a better fit for men’s wrists. This isn’t a perfect match, of course, since plenty of women may fit or prefer the larger size, and vice versa. I’m glad the companies market these as “small” and “large” or just by the numerical size.

But as a person with six-inch wrists, I can’t wear a large watch without dealing with bouncing, rubbing, and potentially inaccurate data. I’m no twig; I’m perfectly average for my half of the population. But those larger watches just aren’t going to be as accurate for me as they are for the average man. I noticed a similar phenomenon when I tested the heart rate tracking in Powerbeats Pro 2 earbuds: They barely worked for me, but did fine when I tried them on a man with larger ears.

There's also an uncomfortable intersection between this gender dynamic and the way watch companies use size as a signifier of how fancy a watch is supposed to be. While Garmin only offers the 970 in a large size (47 mm), they only offer the cheaper 70 and 170 in smaller sizes (both 43 mm). Garmin’s Venu 4 comes in both 41 and 45 millimeter sizes, but the cheaper Vivoactive 6 is only available in 42. Having a small wrist can save you money, but it still shuts you off from some of the more premium options out there.

For my work reviewing and testing watches for Lifehacker, I’ve worn smartwatches ranging from around 40 millimeters (an older Apple Watch) up to 48 millimeters (a tie between the Kospet Tank and the Amazfit T-Rex 3 Pro). I love a watch in the 40-42 millimeter range; I can tolerate one around 46 millimeters if it’s lightweight enough that it doesn’t bounce too much; and I really have a hard time with anything larger.

Those bigger watches are hard to snug up sufficiently for a workout; get them tight enough not to bounce, and they’ll feel like you’re nearly cutting off your circulation. After a long run with a big, heavy watch, I’ve sometimes gotten red, irritated spots on my wrist from the inevitable friction. You may not be able to judge how badly a watch will fit when it's still in the box, but I do have a rule of thumb for when you try it on.

To judge visually, I find it helps to put a watch on your wrist, rest your arm on a table, and look directly down at it. If the lugs of the watch extend past your wrist, it’s too big. You want the watch to fit on your wrist, not overhang the sides.

And if you prefer a watch size that isn’t going to be the most accurate for heart rate, remember that you can always pair a chest strap to a watch that supports it, or to your phone if you don’t have a compatible watch. If you prefer a larger watch for more readable text or maps, or if you want a premium feature that doesn’t come in smaller sizes, a chest strap may be the way to get the best of both worlds.

#3846. Savannah Chrisley Details Surprise Proposal, Teases Wedding Plans

Savannah Chrisley is celebrating another major milestone after announcing her pregnancy.

During the Tuesday, October 27, episode of her “Unlocked” podcast, Chrisley, 29, revealed her boyfriend, Jason, proposed during a getaway to their “happy place” in the Cayman Islands.

“Jason and I started dating in July, so … five months [later],” she said. “Timing means nothing, guys, OK? But back in December, Jason and I were in the Cayman Islands, and little did I know, he had a ring in his backpack.”

Chrisley joked she “didn’t have to snoop” to find it. “Jason was getting a charger out of his backpack, I think I called him in the other room,” she recalled. “He came in there and then he left a box on the counter. I was like, ‘Oh, my God, is that what I think it is?’ But I didn’t know, to be, you know, 100 percent.”

According to the Chrisley Knows Best alum, the couple took a trip to another island, which was “extremely secluded.”

“It was the coolest thing ever,” she said. “But … there was a point in time to where I saw that he was, like, distracted. And I’m like, ‘What’s going on?'”

Jason was apparently texting a friend of Chrisley’s and telling her he thought they were in the perfect place to propose. The friend picked up the ring and flew by helicopter to the island, later telling the couple their flight that afternoon had been delayed an hour.

As the pair got ready to make their way to the airport, Jason led Chrisley to a setup with flowers and champagne.

“I just think he’s being sweet, like, I had no idea … I had been in the ocean all day. I was in a swimsuit,” she recalled. “I had hair, makeup not done, none of that. So I had no idea.”

The moment took a “hilarious” turn when Jason couldn’t find the ring, which was hidden in a tree, but he eventually proposed.

“When he gave me the ring, he was like, ‘Before I open this, I just want you to know that this is just an expression of love to you. This can be whatever you want it to be. This can be an engagement ring, a promise ring, a cocktail ring for all I care. I just want you to know how much I love you,'” Chrisley said. “And there was no pressure ever put on me to, like, ‘You have to accept this as this.'”

Chrisley acknowledged that the proposal came “really quick” into their relationship, but she didn’t care. “I feel like you could find out more about a person in three months than you did in a whole year of being with them,” she asserted.

The former reality star was “in such shock” during the special moment that she began to cry. She raved over the “insane” ring, which she said was “everything I ever wanted.”

Chrisley noted, “It was just the most magical, amazing experience [in] the entire world. It was all filmed, and so we have videos of it, photos of it, and that’s just us. And I absolutely love him for who he is and creating those special moments for us.”

Chrisley also announced her pregnancy via Instagram on Tuesday, adding on her podcast that the unexpected timing made the couple adjust their wedding plans.

“Our wedding was scheduled for January 9 of 2027,” she explained. “And let me tell you, it was going to be a phenomenal time. It still is going to be a phenomenal time. It has just been pushed a few months to May of 2027. And that is because we got the surprise of a lifetime.”

Though she had been going through a fertility journey, Chrisley said her pregnancy was a “major plot twist.”

“We both were so shocked, so thrilled,” she gushed. “God’s hand was written all over this. … We are unbelievably excited and cannot wait for this baby to get here.”

What's $3 billion to a trillion-dollar company?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This week both AMD and Nvidia have announced partnerships with Meta, securing both collabs and billions of dollars. All three companies are heavily invested in AI, and these deals are only more fuel for the fire.

Nvidia just announced its deal with Meta to "expand their partnership on Muse and the Muse family of models", and Meta, in turn, will expand Vera Rubin and Vera CPU deployment, as well as deploy Nvidia's Spectrum X Ethernet networking and BlueField-4 DPUs. Though Meta was probably buying all that stuff anyways, right?

Nvidia also said it was "considering other technological collaborations, including Nvidia OpenShell Reference Architecture to strengthen agency security and Nvidia Sentry for monitoring, structured, unstructured and graph data processing."

Importantly, Nvidia is also investing $3 billion into Meta. Money that may go elsewhere in Meta under a wholly different account book, but ultimately, Meta is spending a large amount of money on AI hardware from Nvidia. You see what's happening here? Both companies are spending big with one another.

One day after this announcement, AMD too declared its partnership with Meta, saying that it would be collaborating on CPU, GPU, networking, systems, and software for Meta's AI infrastructure. AMD also announced that its Helio rackscale solution would be powering Meta's "leading frontier models and services" and that AMD is the "compute engine of choice for Muse and AMD technologies provide Day-0 support for leading Meta models".

To top this off, AMD is investing $1 billion in Meta.

In total, that's $4 billion from both companies into Meta, with Meta announcing how it would be spending its money on each respective company.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

AI is huge for all three companies, with Nvidia becoming the world's first $5 trillion company thanks to the technology, and all three companies will want to perpetuate the growth of AI for that reason.

The AI bubble hasn't burst yet, but it did see a $1 trillion decline just a few weeks ago, and the Chinese AI market lost trillions just this week. One could read further investment as a way to keep the bubble floating for a while longer, or just one in a line of many steps from all three companies to get a foot in the door once the AI boom settles. A bubble popping could help gamers eventually get lower-priced memory, but so much of the world's economy is wrapped up in it that it's hard to say just how wide the reverberations would be, for these three companies or the rest of the world. It won't be good.

The Wall Street Journal argues AI is "becoming the biggest economic bet in US history". It shows AI spending is significantly bigger, as a percentage of annual infrastructure spending, than telecom and fiber, highways, electric, railroads, and even canals way back in the 1800s. One has to wonder what would happen if that investment suddenly lost its value.

And even now, the negative impacts of AI is hitting gamers with the memory crisis, where AI is sucking up so much memory that the average gamer has to pay more and more for less capacious memory. And it doesn't just stop at memory. Graphics cards and SSDs have only gotten worse in the pricing department over the last year, and as long as the AI buildout continues, and memory struggles to keep up, that's unlikely to change soon.

Wicker is gradually becoming one of the most talked-about titles on the 2026 movie schedule, and there are plenty of reasons for that. However, chief among those details is the fact that it sees Alexander Skarsgård play a benevolent man made of wicker. (Have we finally seen morally-gray vampires and ethically questionable antiheroes replaced as the "perfect guy"?) Skarsgård's co-stars, Olivia Colman and Elizabeth Debecki, shared their takes on why women had been fawning over his character, and I understand their viewpoints.

I'd argue that Skarsgård’s rare outing as a "good" guy might have people labeling him the "best" guy after a Wicker screening. The Fisherwoman herself, Colman, and Debicki (who plays the Tailor’s Wife), spoke with Entertainment Weekly about just what it was about a wicker man that has women ready to weave a basket, and they're giving a lot of the credit to Skarsgård:

That "canvas" is definitely something to be envious of. (However the wicker appearance didn’t make filming the intimate scenes less embarrassing for Colman who gives credit to the intimacy coordinator.) All that aside, it’s actually about what’s inside the basket that matters. The premise, based on Ursula Wills-Jones’ short story “The Wicker Husband,” sees all the wives in a village getting jealous of the ATTENTION the Fisherwoman receives from the unique man. While there is some oddly appealing charm woven into the character's physical appearance, that’s not what has the other wives paying attention.

Due to the relationship, the other wives question everything (and I’m not talking about the splinter debate). I’m talking about the fact that when the Fisherwoman has the Basket Weaver (played by Peter Dinklage) make her a perfect husband, what he gives her is a man who is tentative and listens. This is what Colman and Debicki agree makes the Wicker Husband so "hot":

They’re not wrong. Part of what makes Skarsgård so dreamy is not the fine craftsmanship of the subject of Wicker’s risqué popcorn bucket. Wicker seemingly began drumming up buzz after the Harlequin adaptation of the movie poster teased the epic romance. Directors Alex Huston Fischer and Eleanor Wilson have truly created something special, and the fresh reviews of the lusty fantasy flick have earned it a 95% critics score on Rotten Tomatoes along with a 93% audience rating, as of this writing.

Critics seem to appreciate the movie’s “fairy tale quality that's aimed at an adult audience.” Others are complimenting the movie for taking a story about a wicker man and creating something “about trust, autonomy, and finding a relationship that works for you.” Overall, the movie delivers a dreamlike romance that is as hot as it is sweet, but it should also be applauded for making Alexander Skarsgård's character not only swoonworthy but trustworthy as well.

Those who haven't seen the film may be wondering if the movie end with the other wives getting Dinklage's character to weave them new partners or if their husbands take some advice from the wicker man? You'll just have to get that answer by checking out Wicker in theaters now.

Mass Effect fans just will not go quietly into the galaxy’s good night.

The #SaveMassEffect movement started last week continued today with a new GoFundMe looking to raise funds to continue the fight with new billboards—and its goal was reached within 20 minutes.

🚨 WE'VE LAUNCHED A #SaveMassEffect GOFUNDME!Together with @kalaelizabeth, we're raising funds for more billboards near EA HQ and The Game Awards!Any extra funds will support laid-off game developers. ❤️Every dollar counts. Full transparency!🔗 https://t.co/6nbxvlO8qU pic.twitter.com/rkul2JlaXw

The fans behind the movement set a goal of $5,200 to purchase mobile billboards outside of Electronic Arts headquarters, EA Edmonton, and the location of The Game Awards taking place at the beginning of December. They’ve already reached over $5,700 and counting and posted a billboard in New York City’s Times Square to spread the message.

The organizers say that “any additional funds will be donated to the Game Worker Hardship Fund,” which is “a fund that helps laid off developers in both the US and Canada.”

“Save Mass Effect is about letting EA and everyone involved with Mass Effect Universe and Mass Effect as a franchise that we want to see the story continue,” the GoFundMe’s page reads. “We want to see the hard work of the devs who have dedicated their lives to this franchise and we want to see them finish their story. Mass Effect deserves to not die in some EA vault, dead forever.”

Last week, EA shuttered BioWare and shut down development on Mass Effect Universe, and there’s been a massive ripple effect since. A dev working on the project leaked an in-progress trailer to tease what the studio was working on, and the community has been in an uproar ever since.

I am heartened by the reaction from the Mass Effect and greater gaming community, as at the very least it shows the devs and those who worked on the franchise just how loved it was. I remain skeptical that it will make a difference for EA and its new Saudi-based owners, but if nothing else, it’s proven that the game franchise does indeed deserve better than how things have gone down of late. The #SaveMassEffect petition reaching over 250,000 signatures today just solidifies that.

#3841. Judi Dench Explains How Losing Sight Has Changed Her Life

Now aged 91, it's understandable that Judi Dench might not appear in as many films as she did at the peak of her 70-year career. But the outspoken actor, known for taking unexpected positions on topics as varied as The Crown (she's against it), Harvey Weinstein (she encourages forgiveness), and trigger warnings in the theater ("if you’re that sensitive, don’t go") says her retirement isn't consensual, and is instead a result of her deteriorating eyesight.

In 2012, Dench was diagnosed with age-related macular degeneration, a condition that progressively impairs vision. In 2023, she admitted that she could no longer learn lines for a role, and that a community of friends and family assisted her in that task. “I can’t see on a film set any more,” she said then. “And I can’t see to read. So I can’t see much. But, you know, you just deal with it. Get on."

Judy Dench as Queen Elizabeth I in Shakespeare in Love.

By 2025, her vision loss had grown so severe that she had reached a stage of “no eyesight," which required her to enlist a companion any time she ventures out. “I can’t see and I’ll walk into something or fall over,” she said then.

In an interview broadcast Thursday by BBC Radio 4, Dench provided an update on her condition. Speaking on the Today program, the Shakespeare in Love star said her career is essentially over. “I can't be on stage. I can't do a play anymore. I can't do a film anymore. I can't read anymore,” she told interviewer Emma Barnett.

Javier Bardem, Judi Dench, and Daniel Craig on October 22, 2012.

However, the actor says she doesn't let the general effects of aging hold her back. “There's nothing to say for it at all," she said when asked about getting older. "[I] don't think about it too much."

Judi Dench's long career has spanned theater, television, and film. In 1999, she won the Oscar for Best Supporting Actress for her role as Queen Elizabeth I in Shakespeare in Love, despite her relatively brief screen time. She also broke ground in t1995's GoldenEye, as the first woman to portray “M,” the James Bond world's fictional head of British intelligence. She continued in the role until 2012, when her character was killed off in the franchise's 25th film, Skyfall.

When Barnett asked if Dench had an opinion on who should play the next James Bond, she remained noncommittal. She simply replied, "no," but reminisced about a lunch she had with former MI6 chief Sir David Spedding. The meeting was “very exciting,” she said, though it was plagued with challenges.

According to Dench, she was told “we'll send a car to you, we'll come and pick you up” for the meeting, but the driver that was dispatched couldn't find her home. As a result, she was 20 minutes late to visit with the former official, Dench told Barnett. “I said, 'I'm sorry for being so late because your driver couldn't find my house.' I loved it."

#3840. Chrishell Stause's Ex Jason Oppenheim Reacts to Her & G Flip's Breakup

After Chrishell Stause and G Flip ended their three-year marriage, the Selling Sunset alum's ex-boyfriend Jason Oppenheim shared his support for the exes: "Sometimes things just don't work out."

The sun will never set on Jason Oppenheim's love for Chrishell Stause.

After the Selling Sunset alum shared that she and G Flip broke up following three years of marriage, her ex-boyfriend expressed support for both of them during this difficult chapter.

"I'm really sorry to hear this," Oppenheim wrote in an Oct. 26 comment under her Instagram post. "I'm thinking of you and sending you my love. You're both beautiful people and sometimes things just don't work out, but it certainly doesn't mean anyone failed."

The Selling Sunset star—who dated Stause for five months before splitting in 2021—also emphasized that healing will simply take time.

"There's probably nothing anyone can say to make you feel better right now, it might just take some time to process," he added. "But please know that you're very loved and I'm always there for you no matter what."

Stause confirmed on Oct. 27 that she and G Flip (real name Georgia Claire Flipo) closed the deal on their breakup after tying the knot in 2023, describing the update as "pretty humbling."

"We have agreed to treat this public separation with love and respect for each other," she said in the Instagram video. "I don't have all the information and reasons, and I'm sure, just like always, with time you get the lessons you're meant to get. I know that will happen, and I'm going to heal and everything is going to be fine."

The 45-year-old—who was married to Justin Hartley from 2017 to 2019 before she dated Oppenheim—decided to share the update in a video because she felt fans deserved more than a written statement, had she and the Australian musician worked out in another life.

"You have been so supportive and so loving and we really appreciate it," she continued. "I don't know what else to say, you guys. I feel like a little bit of a failure. There is nothing you can say about me that would be worse than what I say to myself, so I just have to heal and figure this out. It's hard. It's painful."

Stause—who had multiple wedding ceremonies with G Flip over the years, though no official marriage license was ever filed—has always put her heart on her sleeve. And looking back, she has no regrets about doing so in their relationship.

"It's really hard and stressful to share this information because I'm kind of going through it," she said. "I will always love G, and I don't ever really see that changing, and this sucks, but everyone deserves to be happy and I wish that for both of us."

As Stause and G Flip navigate their new normal, keep reading for a look back at their romance.

The duo posed for a selfie while walking hand-in-hand.

The couple shared a laugh while taking over a swing set.

"Chrishell invited me as her plus 1 and I invited my nipples as a plus 2," G Flip captioned a March Instagram post. "The shirt was flying open all night."
