#3839. Phantom Blade Zero is asking players to free up 300GB of space for pre-loading, double its actual 145GB file size

Pre-loads are live for Phantom Blade Zero, the debut action game from Chinese developer S-Game. Alongside news of the pre-load, the studio revealed the final set of PC requirements, including how much space the game is going to take up on your drive.

Once installed, Phantom Blade Zero will occupy a chonky 145GB of your precious storage space. However, to pre-load the game, you're going to need over double that - 300GB, seemingly for the decryption and decompression process. The developer explained in a Steam blog post that "temporary files and the complete game files must coexist during the unlock process". You'll get back the extra space once the game is installed, of course.

While a game requiring extra space for these operations is not uncommon, the fact Phantom Blade Zero's install size is already so large makes needing to free up double that ludicrous. Phantom Blade Zero also requires an SSD drive, and seeing as the global gen-AI-driven memory and storage shortages have made them unattainable to many, you're not likely to buy a new drive just to accommodate a single game.

High as they may be, those storage space requirements aren't likely to deter players from buying a copy. Indeed, the game is already predicted to be among the year's biggest hits, having already sold over 500,000 copies on Steam alone, according to some estimates.

Eurogamer reviewed Phantom Blade Zero. In her review, Emma Kent was critical of the game's underwhelming boss flights and its general lack of polish, which she said mar Phantom Blade Zero's otherwise fluid and cinematic kung fu-style action.

Phantom Blade Zero arrives 29th October on PC and PS5.

#3838. Kim Kardashian Reveals When She Knew Kris Humphries Marriage Was Over

Kim Kardashian revealed exactly when she knew her whirlwind marriage to Kris Humphries was over.

“I knew it before we were married in my soul,” Kardashian, 46, told journalist Jonathan Van Meter in the new book Citizen Kim: The Woman Who Created the Future, available now. “I just thought, ‘Well, it’s cold feet.’ But your gut, your soul, is always right. And then I thought, ‘Well, they’re filming for a show, and if I bail now, they’re going to call me “the runaway bride” and it will be an even bigger thing.’ But I should have just not gotten married.”

According to Van Meter, who spoke with Kardashian and her family in wide-ranging interviews conducted from 2018 to 2026, the reality star referred to her 72-day marriage as one of “the biggest mistakes (and regrets) of her life,” along with her infamous sex tape.

Kardashian began dating Humphries, now 41, in October 2010. The pair got engaged the following May and tied the knot in August 2011. A two-part wedding special aired that October — the same month Kardashian filed for divorce.

“I knew she wasn’t happy,” Kardashian’s mom, Kris Jenner, told Van Meter in the book. “I went to her during the rehearsal, before the wedding. We were in this beautiful house in Montecito, [California], and the windows were open and Robin Thicke was rehearsing some songs on the dance floor. And I remember listening to the song, and I said to her, ‘Listen … I can just … I feel like … you’re not making the right decision.’ And I said, ‘If you want to cancel the wedding, just get out of here and I’ll figure it out.'”

Keeping Up With the Kardashians producer Farnaz Farjam shared her own perspective, claiming, “Kim didn’t have the confidence to back out.”

Farjam said navigating the public conversation surrounding Kardashian’s quickie divorce helped give her a thicker skin.

“In early seasons, Kim was this hopeless romantic. She would get shy; she could get vulnerable; she wore her emotions on her sleeve. She liked being sexy and having all that attention, but she wasn’t confident,” Farjam explained. “I think she had to go through some hardships — recognizing that it’s not the end of the world — to build that up.”

In December 2011, Humphries filed a response to Kardashian’s divorce petition and requested an annulment, alleging the marriage was a fraud for publicity. Kardashian denied the accusations, and Humphries eventually dropped his case. Their divorce was officially finalized in June 2013.

Farjam claimed Kardashian “thought her life was over” amid the legal fallout, but the Skims cofounder relied on some advice from her late grandmother Mary Jo “MJ” Shannon.

“When I filed for divorce from Kris Humphries, my grandmom was like, ‘Listen. My parents saved up all their money so I could have a big wedding because I thought I was in love. About a month or two in, I realized I’d made a terrible decision and I was so embarrassed. It was in the town paper! I just wasted everyone’s time and money and I was mortified. I knew I had to get a divorce, and I just did it. I thought my parents would never speak to me again,'” Kardashian recalled in the book. “And she said to me, ‘You have to be happy. You have to be.'”

Before Humphries, Kardashian was married to Damon Thomas from 2000 to 2004. She tied the knot with Kanye West in 2014, and the pair welcomed four children before splitting in 2021. They reached a settlement in 2022.

Citizen Kim: The Woman Who Created the Future is out now.

#3837. 10 Hacks Ever Siri AI User Should Know

Khamosh Pathak is a freelance tech journalist with over 13 years of experience writing online. An accounting graduate, he turned his interest in writing and technology into a career. He holds a specialization in user experience design from ISDI Parsons in Mumbai, where he sought to deepen his understanding of UX, consumer devices, and interplay between software and technology.

Khamosh has written thousands of how-tos and guides on every major platform (iOS, macOS, Android, and Windows). He got started writing how-tos and guides for Guiding Tech, where he also published several e-books. His work has also been seen at MakeUseOf, How-To Geek, and PCMag. He is truly passionate about brewing good coffee.

If you're like me, you've spent years ignoring most of Siri's functionality. Sure, it's great for setting timers, making calls, and sending a text. But that's kind of it—Apple's promise of an "always-on" digital assistant never truly delivered.

But that's finally changed with the recent launch of Siri AI. Fifteen years after debuting the app on the iPhone 4S and four years after the release of ChatGPT, Apple has taken the time to build out its own private Apple Foundation Models (with little help from Google) that work both on-device and on Apple's servers to give quick responses to your queries. Siri AI's answers are short, to the point, and actually useful. However, the conversational side isn't the only new thing about Siri AI. In fact, once you scratch the surface, you'll find Siri AI features embedded across the entire iOS and macOS ecosystem. Here's how to get the most out of Apple's new, enhanced digital assistant.

Siri AI is already tuned for brevity. Compared to Gemini or Claude, its responses are short and to the point. But still, if you ask something with your voice, it responds using voice as well. I personally prefer to quickly read through Siri responses. If you’re like me, you can enable a hidden setting that makes sure that Siri always answers silently, no matter if you’re writing to it or using your voice. Go to Settings > Siri > Responses and switch to the Silent Response option.

On the other hand, if you prefer to listen to Siri's responses, it's worth it to spend some time fine-tuning them. If you have an iPhone that’s under two years old, you have access to a more expressive Siri voice. Siri AI has an Expressive Voice feature that works on iPhone 17 Pro and higher, iPhone Air, and Macs with an M3 processor or higher (at least 12GB RAM).

To find this feature, go to Settings > Siri > Voice. Here, you can change the Pace to make Siri faster or slower. The big feature is the Expressivity slider; you can use it to make Siri sound brighter and more enthusiastic, rather than flat. You also have several voice options at the top (color-coded).

Visual Intelligence is deeply integrated into iOS 27 and macOS 27, and it turns anything on your screen into a source that Siri AI can act upon. It can search anything on your screen, and even take action on things it finds out. You can highlight any text on an iPhone, tap and hold, and choose the Ask Siri option. On the Mac, Ask Siri is the first option in the right-click menu. Choose a file, select text, and get started talking to Siri.

But it goes further. Just pull up Siri AI on your iPhone or Mac and start asking questions about what you’re seeing on screen. If you’re seeing a photo of a cafe on Instagram, ask where it is, or ask Siri to open it in Maps. If you’re watching a scene from a movie or a TV show, ask Siri where it’s from.

Clean Up was part of Apple Intelligence features in iOS 18, but it wasn’t great. It used on-device models to remove objects, and the results were lacking. Now that Apple runs its own Apple Foundation Models in Private Cloud Compute, it can securely process images on its servers, and the Clean Up process is much better. It now removes objects from photos very reliably, and even accounts for shadows. But to use this, you have to make sure to use the High Quality feature (instead of the default Auto mode).

Tap the Edit button in a photo, go to Tools > Clean Up, and from the top, switch to the High Quality option. Now brush over or tap an object you want to remove. Then, tap Clean Up and wait for a while until Apple’s servers complete the process. Tap Done to save the changes.

One of the most tedious parts of using the Calendar app is manually entering multiple events from a complicated schedule—say, for a season's worth of your kid’s soccer practices, or your college course's weekly lecture schedule. With Visual Intelligence in iOS 27, this is now table stakes: You can point your iPhone camera towards a schedule or take a screenshot of one, then tap the Add to Calendar button. Siri will recognize all the events, and you’ll get an option to add them to the calendar individually or all at once.

Siri AI's Mail integration is a hidden gem. Once your iPhone or Mac is done indexing your local data from Mail, you can use Siri AI to triage and manage your Mail without even opening the Mail app. In my testing, it works really well, especially because Siri AI understands the context of an email. This means you don't need to remember the exact details about the sender or the subject. You can just ask something like, "Find all emails about my flights from last week and send them to my accountant." You can also ask it to do things for you. For example, you can say, "Archive all the emails from Walmart from last month," and it will give you a one-button option to do just that.

When you start using the new Siri AI more often, you'll realize that it asks for confirmation way too often. For things like deleting mail, that's useful. But for sending messages, less so. There is a hidden setting that removes the extra step when it comes to sending messages via Siri. Go to Settings > Siri > Responses and enable Automatically Send Messages.

After the Siri AI upgrade in iOS 27, I find myself using the Camera Control often, and you should try it too. Just press and hold the Camera Control button to launch the camera's Siri mode. Point towards anything you want to find out more about and tap the big Siri icon. In a second or two, you will get the Siri response at the top, along with relevant actions based on what you're looking at. This feature is genuinely useful, and if you don't like using the Camera Control (or don't have access to it on your iPhone), you can also add the Siri AI shortcut to the lock screen, Control Center, or the Action button.

While Shortcuts has been one of Apple's most powerful features, it's also one of the hardest to use. Making an automation requires setting up steps, selecting variables, and thinking about chronology, and more. With Siri AI, Apple is trying to reduce that burden. And when it comes to simpler stuff that an everyday iPhone user needs, it works.

For example, you can open Shortcuts, create a new shortcut, and use the Describe a shortcut feature to talk about a Shortcut you want to build in natural language. You can ask Siri to create a daily morning Shortcut that shows you the day's weather, upcoming calendar events, and the to-do list for the day. You can also ask it to create a Shortcut that converts images in batches and creates new notes with a pre-made template.

While Apple is now using its own Apple Foundation Models for the new Siri AI, ChatGPT isn’t technically gone. If you have a complex question, or if Siri AI’s answer isn’t doing the trick, you can still summon ChatGPT from the Siri AI app (no need to go to the ChatGPT app for this). Tap on the text field in the Siri app, choose the Ask option and then switch to the ChatGPT service. Siri AI is quite intelligent at routing, so if ChatGPT can’t answer, it will route you back to Siri AI’s own models.

#3836. Xbox Workers Are Eligible For Buyouts As Part Of Huge Shakeup At Microsoft

Microsoft has announced a new program called the Voluntary Exit Offer that it's extending to thousands of workers at the company, including people on the Xbox team.

Microsoft is doing this to shed employees, but the messaging is that a program like this could be appealing to someone who was already thinking about leaving and can now do so with a pay package.

Microsoft shared the high-level details of the Voluntary Exit Offer on its blog, and Bloomberg had more insight. The report said 60,000 workers are eligible for the Voluntary Exit Offer, and this works out to more than 25% of Microsoft's entire workforce.

Bloomberg reported that Xbox is one of the divisions of Microsoft whose employees can leave the company via the Voluntary Exit Offer, along with Windows, Copilot, and finance teams. The report said people working on Microsoft's efforts in data centers, sales, marketing, cyber security, and quantum computing are not eligible.

Microsoft made this announcement just two days before it releases its next earnings report on October 28. The technology giant is spending hundreds of billions of dollars in its massive AI push, and many in the investment community have questioned if that level of capital expenditure makes sense.

In April this year, Microsoft offered a Voluntary Retirement Program, and it offered severance pay, ongoing stock vesting, and extended health benefits. About 33% of eligible workers took that deal. Mass layoffs came in July, affecting nearly 5,000 people, including major cuts at Xbox. Terms of the new Voluntary Exit Offer are not available, but a spokesperson told Bloomberg that those who take the offer will receive benefits similar to a severance package.

For Xbox specifically, it was reported that further layoffs could be announced in October, with another round set for November, and then another in January 2027. However, The Verge's Tom Warren said the October layoffs at Xbox are not happening this month, but will occur later. Xbox has been undergoing a "reset" under the leadership of CEO Asha Sharma, and this has included mass layoffs, studio closures, and game cancellations, among other dramatic moves.

These changes are happening at Microsoft amid huge surges in revenue and profitability for the company. For the latest period, Microsoft reported $90 billion in revenue (up 18%) and a profit of $35.8 billion (up 22%). As mentioned, the newest data will be revealed on October 28, so keep checking back for more.

CEO Satya Nadella addressed the seemingly discordant nature of cutting staff so massively at a time when the company is making so much money. In a memo, he said, "This is the enigma of success."

Auroch Digital just released Boltun 2 two weeks ago, and it's got Warhammer Survivors lined up for launch in November.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GamesIndustryWarhammer 40,000: Boltgun developer Auroch Digital appears to be headed toward significant layoffs. In a statement posted to LinkedIn, since deleted but reported by GamesIndustry and others, the studio said that despite its "creative success, the same market conditions that have challenged so many of our fellow developers have now also affected us." As a result, Auroch Digital is restructuring, and has begun a redundancy consultation process that will impact "around a quarter" of its employees.

"Over the past year our colleagues have delivered amazing games like Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 and Starship Troopers: Ultimate Bug War! to wonderful reception from our players," the Auroch Digital statement said.

"Despite that creative success, the same market conditions that have challenged so many of our fellow developers have now also affected us. Consequently, we have made the decision to restructure the studio to work through a turbulent and uncertain time in our industry."

As noted, that message has since been deleted, and there's no trace of it elsewhere, aside from gaming sites that have previously reported on it. However, at least two Auroch Digital employees have also reported the redundancy notification.

Assuming there's not been some massive miscommunication here, the cuts come despite a good run for Auroch Digital over the past few years. Released in 2023, Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun was "the first truly exceptional Warhammer 40,000 game since Dawn of War," we declared in our 85% review; the follow-up, Boltgun 2, is "sick," in the words of PC Gamer shooter expert Morgan Park, and currently holds an "overwhelmingly positive" rating on Steam.

Starship Troopers: Ultimate Bug War didn't meet quite the same enthusiastic response—I enjoyed the preview build I played but our reviewer Nova Wright did not care for the full release—but the studio also has Warhammer Survivors in the pipe, set to launch on November 10, and it looks very promising.

The seeming bad news also comes less than a year after Auroch Digital was acquired by Roundtable Interactive in a deal backed by EMK Capital.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Anyway, it's all a bit strange. Why Auroch Digital would announce the pending layoff of a quarter of its staff—which clearly happened—and then erase all trace of that message shortly after is anyone's guess, but I strongly suspect there's no good answer. I've reached out to the studio for comment and will update if I receive a reply.

#3832. Jill Lepore: AI Is Eroding Civil Society

They dress like gurus, all alike in black, with similar sneakers and hoodies. They read the same books, many of them science fiction. They speak in a similar language, with a messianic tone. They send messages warning of a coming apocalypse and present themselves as the only ones capable of saving humanity from the potential disasters caused by the AI that they created.

According to Jill Lepore, a Harvard historian and 2026 Pulitzer Prize winner in history for her book We the People: A History of the U.S. Constitution, Silicon Valley leaders behave like a religious cult, with artificial intelligence as God and they as its intermediaries. In her latest book, The Rise and Fall of the Artificial State, published this past August, she argues that today Big Tech controls the United States—and, in turn, the entire planet. They wield their power authoritatively, favoring presidents who best serve their interests, and helping to erode the democratic fabric of nations, one fake news story at a time.

Vanity Fair: In your book, you define the Artificial State as “a future in which humans are ruled, and many replaced, by machines owned by corporations.” How close is that future?

Jill Lepore: The place where people can most easily see what I’m talking about is with what used to be called the public sphere. A liberal democracy depends on a free and open public discussion of the state of the world where people can get reliable information and exchange ideas and deliberate. The Artificial State doesn’t make citizens, it unmakes citizens; it makes trolls.

What this communications infrastructure asks of you is not to participate in civil society; it is to remove yourself from participating in civil society, to remove yourself from social relations, to alienate yourself from your fellow citizens, and instead to engage in the machinery of outrage and condemnation and grievance that is destructive to civil society.

Certainly in the US, there’s democratic pushback. Our municipal and state elections since last spring have been influenced by this debate in a way that I think surprised candidates, campaigns, and elected officials. The public has found that objecting to data centers is a way to say no to the entire apparatus.

What are your predictions for the midterm elections?

The polling seems to indicate Democrats will pick up a lot of seats in Congress, but the polls have been less and less reliable over the last quarter century. I don’t think anybody’s really counting on those predictions. Trump’s power, however, is very much on the decline.

Elon Musk and Donald Trump on May 30, 2025.

And that’s despite the staunch support of Big Tech, both in terms of funding and political influence across various platforms.

Silicon Valley leaders had always been libertarians. They’re really just supporting whatever political party is going to advance their business interests. During the Clinton and Gore era, they were aligned with the Democrats who were very much committed to the future of the knowledge worker.

The switch that came about in the summer of 2024, when Elon Musk announced that he would be supporting Trump, was a consequence largely, I think, of the Biden administration’s determination to regulate the cryptocurrency industry. That pissed off a lot of Silicon Valley people. The Biden administration also had a commitment to AI safety that annoyed AI companies.

As these companies began to turn their attention and investments toward accelerating the development of AI, the Biden administration was saying, “Well, no, wait, hold on. What’s this going to look like?” That’s what turned Silicon Valley toward Trump.

You discuss in your book how Trump quickly took action to eliminate AI safeguards.

His embrace of AI has really fractured the MAGA movement because MAGA is not pro-AI; it’s just Trump. It’s been one of the more splintering forces.

Between the two forces—the political power of the White House and the technological power of Silicon Valley—which has more influence?

The power of the Silicon Valley elite is not national, it’s global. Yes, Trump’s power as the leader of a superpower is significantly global as well, but I think ultimately Sam Altman is a more powerful figure than Donald Trump at this moment.

Can Leo XIV play a role in curbing the excessive power of AI and its adherents?

The Pope has made it a priority from the very beginning, and he has made it clear that this was going to be an issue of his papacy. I think the statements that he made through his encyclical [Magnifica Humanitas: On Safeguarding the Human Person in the Time of Artificial Intelligence] were really important. You can see the degree of that importance in the response of some Silicon Valley figures, with Peter Thiel wondering whether the Pope might be in league with the Antichrist and Marc Andreessen trolling the Pope online.

A lot of that world has operated using the tool of mystification in the way the medieval church did. They present themselves as the guardians of Godlike machinery that only they can explain to the world and that they are engaged in a mission that involves the possible salvation of human civilization.

If AI truly poses a threat, how can they expect us to place our hopes in its creators?

From their long-termer point of view, the prospect for the future lives of billions and billions of as-yet-unborn humans involves escaping the earth, founding colonies on other planets. We need artificial intelligence to attain escape velocity from the planet, to build the spaceships, to found the colonies. So, sure, the eight billion of us alive today, it's possible we might all die in the course of building that, but if it’s not built, the infinite billions of the future will never be born.

In your book, you make it quite clear that technological progress began with the ability to count and measure, was reinforced by its ability to make forecasts into the future, and it ended up controlling everything, including thought. What do you think we can now do as citizens to further democracy?

It’s important to find ways to participate in civil society and democratic governance that are not mediated by the machinery of the artificial state.

I think that being deliberate and purposeful about when and where you participate is important. There are a lot of organizations that are springing up that are demanding that elected representatives respond more to people’s preferences in this regard instead of nodding along and saying, “Oh, well, in the US case, AI is really propping up our entire economy.” Congress is unwilling to do anything to stand in its way for that reason, but more than 70% of Americans want Congress to do something.

Originally published by Vanity Fair Italy

#3830. Everything You Should See Along With ‘You Can See Everything’

Jeff Somers is a freelancer who has been writing about writing, books, personal finance, and home maintenance since 2012.

Jeff has published nine novels and more than 70 short stories; his story Ringing the Changes was selected for inclusion in The Best American Mystery Stories 2006. In 2018, Jeff published the book Writing Without Rules: How to Write & Sell a Novel Without Guidelines, Experts, or (Occasionally) Pants. He holds a bachelor’s degree in English and previously worked in medical publishing as a production editor.

When not writing, Jeff spends his free time fixing up his old house. He has tackled a wide range of DIY projects, including remodeling a bathroom entirely on his own in two weeks, and building a roof deck that hasn't fallen down yet. You can find him on Bluesky.

This is Streamalikes, where you can find a curated list of recommendations to stream based on what you already like.

You Can See Everything, the new documentary about Theranos founder (and accomplished con artist) Elizabeth Holmes from filmmakers Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, is breaking records (and brains). Not only is it raking it in at the box office in a way that few documentaries can manage, it’s also dominating the pop culture chat right now thanks to genre-defying techniques; a huge, rule-breaking twist (that I won't spoil); and its unrepentant and inexplicably mesmerizing subject.

While some might argue that you should head into this incredible film experience fresh, if you’re unfamiliar with any of the three major figures involved, you will benefit from a little prep work before you head in—and if you've already seen it, you're no doubt eager to dive even deeper into this truly remarkable (in every sense of the word) story. Here’s what you should watch, read, and listen to before or after You Can See Everything.

To put it simply: There’s a lot going on here. To ensure you can appreciate the film, you should make sure you know at least the basics about three things: Elizabeth Holmes (and why she’s infamous), and the work of both Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim. Here’s the crash course.

The 2019 podcast The Dropout is one of the most efficient ways to get the background on Holmes and Theranos. In the style of a true crime podcast, this show from ABC and journalist Rebecca Jarvis offers a deep dive into Holmes and her crimes that will give you all the background you’ll need.

John Carreyrou is the journalist who broke the Holmes story wide open, and his award-winning book Bad Blood offers a more journalistic and less sensational investigation than other accounts, providing a close-up look at Holmes in her CEO persona—an alternately terrifying and charismatic person who comes across as a “character” Holmes is playing. The book will inform your viewing of the documentary in important ways.

While Nathan Fielder is getting most of the attention for You Can See Everything, co-director Lance Oppenheim brings his signature technique to the film—it wouldn’t be nearly as interesting without his visual sensibility and framing.

Oppenheim has helmed several notable films, as he’s made a name for himself as a brilliant documentarian over the last few years, but to get ready for You Can See Everything, check out Spermworld, his 2024 exploration of unregulated sperm donations (you read that right). The doc deals with people who are as reality-challenged, manipulative, and possibly delusional as Holmes, and it will prep you for his visual style of extreme close-ups and excessive coverage that seems almost impossible in a normal documentary setting. You can stream Spermworld on Hulu.

If you’re unfamiliar with Nathan Fielder’s work, you need to strap in, because he’s unlike any other comedian or documentarian you’ve ever encountered.

To get a sense of his style, start with Nathan for You, a comedy-documentary-reality TV hybrid in which Fielder—as a version of himself—tries to use his business experience to help struggling people and small businesses. Presented as totally sincere, Fielder’s ideas are nevertheless increasingly elaborate and, in a word, unhinged. This show will familiarize you with Fielder’s persona and approach, but if you only have time to cram one episode, check out the series finale, “Finding Frances.” It follows Fielder’s attempts to reunite one of his earlier clients with his long-lost love, and it is a nearly perfect encapsulation of his style and approach. You can stream Nathan for You on HBO Max, Hoopla or Paramount Plus, or rent it episode-by-episode on Prime Video.

You should also watch at least some of Fielder’s other series, The Rehearsal. Here, Fielder (again, portraying a version of himself) attempts to help people prepare for difficult conversations utilizing increasingly outlandish techniques and scenarios that spiral into comedic insanity, including building sets, hiring actors, and immersing himself and his subjects in an alternate reality of sorts—an approach that will prepare you for some of You Can See Everything’s swerves. If you don’t have time to watch the whole thing, just check out the first episode, “Orange Juice, No Pulp.” It’s a self-contained chapter and will give you the basics. You can stream The Rehearsal on Apple TV or HBO Max, or rent episodes on Prime Video.

Have more bandwidth before watching You Can See Everything, or want a deeper dive after the documentary has melted your brain? Here’s what else you can check out.

The companion podcast to Carreyrou’s book, Bad Blood: The Final Chapter, is well worth a listen. Hearing the journalist’s perspective about his investigation and work during and after Holmes’ trial offers additional insight into her personality and methods that will really resonate after watching her in Fielder and Oppenheim’s doc.

Once you’ve noodled on You Can See Everything for a moment, there are two more movies you should check out.

For more on Holmes’s specific grift and how she pulled it off, Alex Gibney’s excellent The Inventor: Out for Blood in Silicon Valley is the way to go, especially if you didn’t follow the Theranos story closely at the time and your main visual guide to Holmes is the new documentary. Gibney’s film is more traditional, but offers a wealth of insight into Holmes and her bubble. You can stream The Inventor on HBO Max or rent it on Prime VIdeo.

If you more from co-director Lance Oppenheim, watch Some Kind of Heaven, his 2020 breakout documentary about The Villages retirement community in Florida. It’s the film that put Oppenheim on the map, and offers hints of the same detached observational style that makes You Can See Everything so arresting. You can stream Some Kind of Heaven on Kanopy or Hoopla, or rent it on Prime Video.

Speaking of which, if you loved the visual style and “enhanced” reality of You Can See Everything and want to see more of that, catch the Robert Pattinson-starring Primetime, still in theaters. It’s Oppenheim's narrative film directorial debut, and combines his hyperstyled visual flair with thriller pacing and a narrative based on a true story.

The Curse, Fielder’s 2023 TV series, is his first work of fiction, and it offers some fascinating insights. It follows a newly married couple (played by Fielder and Emma Stone) who struggle to get an HGTV-style show off the ground as things get excruciatingly awkward (and, eventually, kind of surreal). After watching You Can See Everything, it will be interesting to see how Fielder views the power of editing, the ethics of reality TV, and working with a name actor to get the results that he wants. Plus, similarly to the film, The Curse swerves into a final act that will melt your brain. You can stream The Curse on Apple TV or rent it on Prime Video.

You should also definitely watch The Dropout, the 2022 Hulu series starring Amanda Seyfried. It’s a well-made film, and Seyfried’s performance was rightly praised for capturing the weirdness of Holmes' public persona. You can stream The Dropout on Hulu.

Jeff Somers is a freelancer who has been writing about writing, books, personal finance, and home maintenance since 2012.

#3829. Who Is Pregnant Savannah Chrisley's Fiancé? Everything She's Shared

Savannah Chrisley announced she's pregnant—and engaged! So, who is the Chrisley Knows Best alum's fiancé? While she's kept much of their romance private, here's everything she's shared so far.

Savannah Chrisley knows to keep the best part of her life to herself.

The Chrisley Knows Best alum shared that she is pregnant and expecting her first baby with her now-fiancé Jason, who she has kept notoriously private.

“You know I share just about everything with you guys,” Chrisley explained on the Oct. 27 episode of her Unlocked podcast. “But I have learned that in order to protect my own peace that I had to keep things closer to the chest for a little while. That’s just for my own mental health.”

However, upon sharing her pregnancy and engagement news, Chrisley was willing to share some more details about her mystery man.

“My protector,” Chrisley wrote in an Oct. 27 Instagram post announcing her pregnancy. “My fiercest fighter. My most loyal person. My lover. My safe place. A man with the patience of Job (and Lord knows he needs it). The one who makes me laugh even on my hardest days.”

On her podcast, Chrisley admitted her relationship with Jason is “a dream relationship that anyone could ever hope to be in.”

And the 29-year-old said her romance with Jason—who she has confirmed is a “lot older” than her—began in July 2025 after they met “in the world of politics” as she was fighting for pardons for parents Todd Chrisley and Julie Chrisley.

Following her parents’ pardons, Chrisley noted she received a text from Jason congratulating her, noting, “When we truly started digging deep into things, it was so much stronger of a connection than either of us ever thought was there.”

Since then, Chrisley—sister to Chase Chrisley, 30, and Grayson Chrisley, 20—confirmed she and Jason have traveled around the world, noting, “We have had so many amazing experiences and I will hold those so close to my heart.”

Of course, Chrisley admitted her whirlwind romance hasn’t been without its roadblocks, especially considering her split from Robert Shiver in April 2025 after two years together.

“I went through a really sad breakup,” Chrisley acknowledged. “I wasn’t expecting to meet someone and then I met someone right in the midst of my heartache and heartbreak and confusion. So, I didn’t know what to do. I just know I felt this insane attraction and emotional pull to this person.”

That said, Chrisley noted it felt “pretty awesome” to keep her relationship under wraps until she was ready to share it.

In fact, the podcaster did not confirm she was even dating someone until May.

“I am dating someone and I am extremely happy and just fulfilled in life,” Chrisley revealed during a May episode of Unlocked. “I feel like where I’m at in life is the moment I’ve always dreamt of but never thought was possible. I am with someone who loves me, respects me, he is my biggest cheerleader.”

The Chrisleys: Back to Reality star mused, “He is just the epitome of the perfect partner.”

For a deeper look into the Chrisley family tree before their latest little addition, keep reading…

Todd Chrisley is the patriarch of the Chrisley family.He was born in Georgia in April 1969 and raised in South Carolina. He and first wife Teresa Terry welcomed daughter Lindsie Chrisley in 1989 and son Kyle Chrisley in 1991 before splitting a few years later. After their breakup, Todd wed Julie Chrisley (born in South Carolina in January 1973) in 1996.Together, they added to their family with the births of kids Chase Chrisley, Savannah Chrisley and Grayson Chrisley and the adoption of Kyle's daughter Chloe Chrisley. They also went through challenges, including Julie's 2012 breast cancer battle (she's now cancer-free).As Todd said on the premiere of Chrisley Knows Best—a show that followed the family's ups and downs as well as their rich lifestyle in Atlanta and later Nashville—he made "95 percent" of their money through his real estate business before entering reality TV. As for Julie, she was working at a bank when she started dating Todd. Chrisley Knows Best ran from 2014 to 2023 and led to spin-offs like Growing Up Chrisley and What's Cooking With Julie? as well as other media ventures like the family members' podcasts.In 2022, Todd and Julie were convicted of fraud and tax crimes, and they were originally sentenced to 12 and seven years in prison, respectively (with him being held in Florida and her being behind bars in Kentucky). The couple pleaded not guilty.In May 2025, President Donald Trump pardoned Todd and Julie and they were released from prison.

Born in September 1989, Lindsie is Todd's eldest daughter from his marriage to Teresa.She attended Georgia State University, where she met now-ex husband Will Campbell. They wed in 2012, and she graduated and gave birth to their son Jackson that December. Two years later, Chrisley Knows Best premiered.Lindsie appeared on the series for five seasons, where viewers saw how Todd and Will didn't get along. She and Will filed for divorce in 2016, and she left the show in 2017.In the years following, there was public fallout between her and her family. It wasn't until 2022 that Lindsie, Todd, Julie and Savannah reunited publicly on crossover episodes of their podcasts.However, their reunion was short-lived. In a 2025 episode of her podcast The Southern Tea, Lindsie said she hasn't seen her dad "in quite some time." The following month, she confirmed she's also estranged from her siblings."We can live in the same space doing the same things and not necessarily agree or align and it’s no hate, it’s no ill will," Lindsie said on Catelynn Lowell and Tyler Baltierra’s podcast, Cate & Ty Break It Down in 2025. "We just don’t serve each other in each other’s lives."In May 2026, Lindsie was arrested on suspicion of DUI, attempting to elude police, improper passing and reckless driving and speeding."I got pulled over speeding past a car on a two lane road," she told TMZ, "because they almost hit an animal and I was trying to miss hitting them and whatever the animal was."

Kyle, born in August 1991, is Todd’s eldest son from his former marriage to Teresa. In November 2012, he became father to daughter Chloe, whose mother is his ex Angela Johnson. But in 2016, Todd and Julie adopted Chloe.While Kyle appeared on only the first season of Chrisley Knows Best (aside from a brief cameo in 2021), his past arrests and rocky relationship with his family (which involved them being estranged and reuniting at various points in time) continued to make headlines. He’s also spoken candidly about his mental health journey and battle with addiction, sharing in a 2019 Facebook post that he's sober.In 2021, Kyle wed Ashley Nelson and became a father to her kids Haiden and Mackenzie.As for where Kyle stands with Todd and Julie, he expressed his hope to reconnect with them after their return from prison."I know they can't wait to get home and just be with family," he told E! News in May 2025, "and I'm going to remain hopeful that another reconciliation is possible."

Chase is the first child Todd welcomed with wife Julie, arriving in June 1996.In addition to appearing on Chrisley Knows Best with his parents and siblings, the entrepreneur co-starred on Growing Up Chrisley with his sister Savannah from 2019 to 2022. Chase was previously engaged to Emmy Medders. But after breaking up and getting back together, they officially parted ways in 2023.After Chase's parents entered prison, he spoke about how he and his siblings came together."We've definitely gotten a lot closer," he told Extra in 2023. "When you get hit with something like this, I feel like a lot of people either separate and go their own ways or they cling together, which is how we were raised. We've clung together and we're still fighting."Chase had his own run-in with the law in January 2025, when he was arrested in Atlanta on a simple battery charge for allegedly becoming "really drunk" and slapping a bar manager after being asked to leave, according to police records obtained by E! News. His lawyers told People that "this incident has been entirely blown out of proportion.""He did not assault anyone," they added. "Chase was trying to close out his tab and leave. We will be independently investigating the situation."

Todd and Julie welcomed daughter Savannah in August 1997.Early in her career, she competed in the pageant world—winning Miss Tennessee Teen USA in 2016 and vying for the Miss Teen USA crown later that year. However, her time in the limelight didn't end there as she went on to appear on several reality and competition shows, including Chrisley Knows Best, Growing Up Chrisley, The Masked Singer and Special Forces: World's Toughest Test. Savannah is also a real estate agent in Tennessee, the founder of Good Girl RX and host of the podcast Unlocked.In her personal life, Savannah got engaged to hockey player Nic Kerdiles in 2019. However, they broke up the following year, and he died after a motorcycle crash in 2023. She later dated Robert Shiver; however, she confirmed their split in April 2025 after about two years together. Savannah obtained custody of her brother Grayson and sister Chloe while Julie and Todd were in prison and advocated for her parents' release.After Trump pardoned Julie and Todd and they were released from prison, Savannah posted footage from their return home, including one that read "Reunited and it feels so good."In October 2026, Savannah announced she's engaged to a man named Jason and that they're expecting a baby.

Grayson is Todd and Julie's youngest son.Born in May 2006, Grayson entered the spotlight when Chrisley Knows Best premiered eight years later. He's now a student at the University of Alabama—and yes, he gets recognized on campus."It's been insane," he said on a February 2025 episode of Savannah's podcast Unlocked. "It's more than it's ever happened here."After Julie and Todd went to prison, Savannah gained custody of Grayson and Chloe."I’m not their mom. I’m not their dad. I’m their sister and I get to love and guide them through life," the podcaster wrote on Instagram in July 2023. "Being a bonus parent is TOUGH…no one could ever add up to their parents. But these kiddos have shown me so much love, patience, grace, understanding, and appreciation! Helping to raise these two kiddos is the hardest but most rewarding job I will ever have."And Savannah's bond with Grayson grew even stronger."Many people look at our situation and think, 'She saved him,'" she wrote on Instagram for his 18th birthday in May 2024, "but little do they know… YOU saved ME."

Todd and Julie started out as Chloe's grandparents after his son Kyle fathered the child with ex Angela Johnson in November 2012. However, they adopted her four years later.“My heart!” Julie wrote on Instagram in February 2018. “This child brings so much happiness to our family. What did we do without you?”Fans watched Todd and Julie raise Chloe—or CoCo as her family calls her—on Chrisley Knows Best. But after the couple entered prison, Savannah took over as her guardian.“Watching her grow has been the biggest blessing of my life… she has QUITE the personality!” Savannah wrote on Instagram for Chloe’s 11th birthday in November 2023. "But I'm excited to be going to get my dad and bringing him home to her."

"I love you more than you will ever know and you fill my heart with love, hope, faith and confidence."Born in September 1944, Nanny grew up in South Carolina. In addition to Todd and Julie's convictions, Nanny was diagnosed with bladder cancer in 2021. "I’ve been out moving around, doing the best I can."Doing the best she can has been Nanny's approach to life in general."The world don’t always give you a good deck of cards to play with, so you play with them the best you can," she said on Savannah's podcast Unlocked in 2023. "You get up, brush yourself off and keep going."

#3827. Steam Next Fest Was Flooded With Slop. These 50 Demos Cut Through The Noise

The October Edition of Steam Next Fest was, unsurprisingly, a complete mess. What was once a fun week-long festival of early game demos has become a dumping ground for vibe-coded AI slop, making it increasingly hard to find and wishlist good games. And if you weren't bothered by playing a game demo that was hastily cobbled together, the sheer volume--around 7,100 game demos were available this time--of what was on offer made Next Fest a nightmare to navigate. So what's a person to do when Valve's own storefront lacked the tools to separate the wheat from the chaff? Be patient, and look at the general consensus; that's what.

While Next Fest is over, many of those games' demos are still readily available to download and play. The Top 50 Next Fest demos for October 2026 span a wide variety of genres and themes, and the list shows a few trends. Social, co-op, and friendslop games rose to the top, as people couldn't get enough of multiplayer chaos in party games like Worst Class Ever, while Fart Together offered physics-driven goofiness and intestinal turbulence.

Niche management and shopkeeper sims also attracted players, with Questing Goose Studio's Probably Stolen and Victorian-gothic gem-cutting sim Abyssal Lapidary proving popular. Anyone looking for low-effort experiences will likely enjoy desktop companion games like Character House, and anticipated sequels like ArcheAge Chronicles drew big player numbers. One thing to note is that some of these games--like Teach My Little Sister How To Drive--do have AI disclosures attached, which you can filter for.

The list below should be a solid starting point for checking out game demos that drew massive buzz, but waiting a week to see what's worth playing based on popularity alone isn't exactly ideal. Valve hasn't said anything about how the third and final Next Fest of the year was a mess, despite the public outcry, even though the number of games for this instance of the event almost doubled compared with the June 2026 edition. If that trend continues, next year's Next Fest in February could be even more difficult to explore.

Beyond Next Fest, Steam has also confirmed the dates for several upcoming sales and themed promotions for the rest of 2026 and the first half of 2027. The Steam Winter Sale 2026 will run from December 17 – January 4, 2027, and right now, you can stock up on horror games through Steam Scream V Fest.

#3826. Lori Loughlin Just Starred In Her First Hallmark Movie In Eight Years. It Took Years To Get There

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

Get the latest movie, TV and streaming news delivered to your inbox.

Ariel Winter Just Admitted To A Modern Family Romance I Didn't See Coming

Sydney Sweeney Just Fantasy Cast Herself As Cindy Lou Who For Unexpected Holiday Lingerie Ad

I Just Realized Leighton Meester And Jared Padalecki's New Rom-Com Is Based On A Book I Loved

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It’s that time of year again, and no, I’m not talking about the spooky season. I’m referring to Merry Hallmark-mas, which counts down to everyone’s favorite holiday with an assortment of cheery romances. Though, you know what’s even better than hanging lights? It’s having a familiar face back helping count down to Christmas: Lori Loughlin just starred in her first Hallmark movie in eight years.

In fact, her new Christmas movie almost became a ‘what if.’ Her new movie, What If Christmas, can be watched online with a Hallmark+ subscription. In it, Loughlin plays a supportive wife who is in the midst of feeling lost and regretting giving up her dreams. So naturally, a heart-shaped ornament allows her to live out her dreams in an alternate reality. Though this sounds like a perfect Hallmark premise, it took years to land on the network. The actor gives the details to TV Insider about the long road it took to make What If into a reality:

I read the initial draft of this script probably 3-4 years ago, and I was trying to get it made somewhere else before I went back to Hallmark, and it just didn’t come to fruition.

It’s been nice seeing her back on the upcoming Hallmark movies list. Her last holiday-themed movie was Homegrown Christmas (2018), and after her exit from Hallmark, I thought that’d be the last time we’d get to see her on the network. That is, until What If Christmas became the perfect holiday movie to get her back into the countdown tradition, and what’s not to love? Obviously, the network thought so too, according to Loughlin:

And then when I went back to Hallmark, they wanted to do a Christmas movie, and I remembered the script. So I had Brad Krevoy, a friend of mine and producer that I work with all the time. He had purchased the script when I initially read it and found it, and he still had the rights to it. So I called him up and I said, ‘Hey, let’s send that script to Hallmark and see what they think.’

Everything about it is classic Hallmark. Loughlin plays Erin, who has traded a potential life as a fashion designer to help her heart surgeon husband (played by Tom Cavanagh) organize hospital galas. When he switches to a new hospital, he suddenly doesn’t need her help anymore, so enter a magical ornament that lets her live out her dreams. The premise is definitely up Hallmark’s alley. Loughlin reveals that it didn't take long for the network to get on board:

So they did, and Hallmark liked it. And then they brought in another writer to do another pass, Julie, and she did a great job. They liked the concept and the idea, and so did I. And that’s how it all came about.

The network made the right choice. Not only did we get the charming movie added to our holiday watchlist, but it paired Loughlin with Cavanagh, which is a perfect match made for Christmas. I loved hearing the actress gushing about working with him, and that “We got along right out of the gate.” We know that Hallmark loves a good pairing, almost as much as they love showing off their Christmas movies, so maybe we’ll get to see them do another movie together.

However, while I love seeing Loughlin be merry, I was even more excited to see her back solving murders. Her Garage Sales Mysteries has been dormant since her departure but finally came back with a 17th installment, Murder Takes the Stage. The actor also surprised everyone by showing up in the When Calls the Heart Season 13 finale, reprising her role as Abigail Stanton, and is expected to have a major part in Season 14.

Her first Hallmark movie back is certainly not the last, and I’m excited to see her back catching killers, decking the halls, and even falling in love.

#3825. After Onimusha's focused swordplay, Phantom Blade Zero delivers a sprawling, maximalist action epic

At just about 15 hours into Phantom Blade Zero, I've changed out my primary weapons more times than I do in an entire Souls campaign. I quickly dropped my starter sword for a pair of dual blades and a jagged weapon that chunks damage off my enemies in showers of blood. But I didn't stick with either for long. Soon I was running around with a two-handed blade taller than my martial arts hero Soul, which sprouts a trail of magical, energy-sapping flowers with every swing. It shares a moveset with another two-handed giant that absorbs life with each hit. But I don't have time to use either one: I'm already smitten with my new twin blades, one of which I can send spinning in a circle around me like a protective Beyblade.

Somehow I haven't even touched the weapon that I took off a crazed monk that turns Soul into a drunken boxer. But I'm sure I'll have time: apparently I'm less than a third(!) of the way through a game that's far, far bigger than I anticipated when I played a chunk of it last year.

For the most part, Phantom Blade Zero's epic ambition is exciting—PC Gamer senior guides writer Sean Martin promises me that I've got about a hundred thrilling boss fights left to come. Its first dozen hours also illustrate the dangers of a long-in-development game ballooning in scope.

While playing Phantom Blade Zero I've been thinking a lot about this season's other big action game, which also spent more than half a decade in development: Capcom's revival of Onimusha. That game wastes little time in introducing its protagonist Miyamoto Musashi or the forces he'll be up again. He's brash and headstrong, taunting a group of samurai after whipping up on their master—then leading them on a Looney Tunes-style chase when there are too many to fight at once. The game punctures this silly tone shortly after introducing Musashi's rival, Ganryu, when both are murdered by demonic Genma.

Musashi's saved when he's given an oni gauntlet in the afterlife and sent back to the land of the living to harness souls with it; the rest of the game is about him deciding who and what he fights for. It's about that personal journey first and foremost, with the machinations of the game's villain spurring him along—and fantastic acting and facial motion capture do a ton of the work to make the story compelling.

In Phantom Blade Zero, by contrast, I've already lost track of the tangled web of characters, relationships, betrayals and motivations with dozens of hours to go. I've seen (and played) flashbacks to Soul's childhood, teenage years, and recent past; I've watched cutscenes about his enemy doing some Machiavellian scheming with another guy I can't name; I've read dozens of cryptic messages scattered throughout each zone that are meant to explain more about why I'm fighting puppets, dudes whose bodies have "transcended" into pure energy, monks who coat their bodies in hardened gold, and mean dogs. (Actually, nothing has explained the dogs yet).

I appreciate PBZ's attempt to start in media res and give Soul a sense of urgency—we flash back to him being stabbed through the heart, then shortly after flash forward to him getting a temporary fake heart that will fail on him in 66 days. But the flashbacks and bits of incidental storytelling often feel like infodumps from someone who's spent years writing a massive story bible and has lived with the whole thing in their head for so long that they don't realize how hard it is to follow when you're coming in with zero context.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Take these flowers, Phantom Blade Zero's way of doling out health upgrades. You'll find them scattered around each area, and finding all of them (usually around 3-6) will net you about 10% more health. Each plays 30 seconds or so of dialogue, and the writing's often vague references to characters and events I've never witnessed mostly makes me want to mash the skip button. We're far from the evocative audio vignettes of BioShock, here.

Onimusha: Way of the Sword has some plenty forgettable sidequests, too, but it also features a number of quests that reinterpret Japanese fables written by Lady Murasaki, the author of The Tale of Genji. These sidequests are great, embellished with unique art that inspired me to read more Japanese mythology. Like Onimusha's main story, they're simple and straightforward, but with just enough flair to make them compelling.

So far Phantom Blade Zero is a lot more convoluted than my favorite wuxia films, with small casts of characters and straightforward motivations—a love story, a "save the small town from a corrupt official" story, a ghost story. But when its epic sweep means I get to play with an absolutely luxurious variety of weapons, I'm not exactly heartbroken that the story is less than riveting.

In Onimusha: Way of the Sword, I started the game with sword, and I ended the game with sword. At no point in between did I wield anything but sword.

This is not a complaint. I'm not sure another videogame has done sword better than Onimusha frankly ever—the physicality of it, the dance between blocks, deflects and parries, the lightning strike of an issen—the animations and weighty physics in Onimusha combine to create a game rich in sensory feedback even with none of the complex combos of, say, Devil May Cry.

What makes Phantom Blade Zero special for me are all the ways it isn't a soulslike—it's even less of one than Black Myth Wukong was. It abandons many of those rigid structures in favour of an action experience that focuses more on feeling good. Enemies don't respawn, you don't lose souls on death, if you die on a boss's second phase, it'll still be in its second phase when you return.

Most bosses also don't take more than one or two attempts, but each is long enough that you get to see everything they have and learn their attacks. Most of all, Phantom Blade Zero feels like a kung-fu epic, dripping in martial mastery as your boss parries turn into veritable cutscenes of you styling on them, and Soul is immediately adept in every weapon or Phantom Edge he picks up.

The old Capcom playbook in that vein of character action game is to give you a handful of weapons, each with a lengthy list of button inputs that create slightly different attacks; stringing them together is visually satisfying and keeps your combo counter ticking up and up. Modern Soulslikes offer way more weapons, but at the expense of that rich variety—each weapon type has a couple unique animations to its name, and that's it.

Phantom Blade Zero blends those two approaches, with the weapon variety of a modern action RPG but the feel and combo mad flourishes of 2000s character action games. Each of Soul's weapons has a fairly limited basic combo string, but also wholly unique mechanic that impacts how you use it in a fight. And each weapon has an upgrade path that adds one or two new actions to its repertoire.

The Outlaw Warblade, one of those massive two-handed swords I mentioned at the start, initially drains enemy health with basic attacks, and lets you convert your own health into recoverable damage to perform enhanced guard-breaking attacks. After five upgrades, it gains a new attack that lets you tank damage while you charge it up and instantly restores a chunk of HP if you land the hit. After 15 upgrades, it grants you an "emergency shield" for continuously sapping your own health, rewarding living on the edge.

Like Onimusha, Phantom Blade Zero's boss fights are definitely its highlights, with the arena designs and one-off mocapped stunts making it clear why this game took so long to make. The big difference between the two, though, is how much world PBZ has in and around those fights. So far I've found progression a bit more linear than I hoped, with levels that are Dark Souls-esque but with fewer surprising paths linking back to previous areas. Still, they're peppered with diversions that are closed off until you acquire certain weapons, and lots of overlooks and out-of-reach treasures that taunt you to double-check for routes you might have missed.

It's a way, way more engaging way to spend time between fights than what Onimusha offered in its hub world of Kyoto, and I appreciate that Phantom Blade Zero keeps its map very vague—there's no compass waypoint telling me where to go for a quest. So far the storytelling around these quests has been barebones, but at least I'm not turning my brain off and following an arrow to each and every one.

Instead of designing Phantom Blade's world around paths you'll tread and retread and enemy placements you'll learn to carve your way through, S-Game seems to have opted to craft a 50-hour game by buckling down and making so much stuff. How is it this big without a timesuck of an open world or infinite respawning fodder or roguelike gimmicks? Because it may be the most maximalist action game of its type we've ever seen, from its torrent of text notes to its luxurious animations. Even when it doesn't all work, it's hard not to be won over by a game that goes this all-out.

Wes has been covering games and hardware for more than 10 years, first at tech sites like The Wirecutter and Tested before joining the PC Gamer team in 2014. Wes plays a little bit of everything, but he'll always jump at the chance to cover emulation and Japanese games.

When he's not obsessively optimizing and re-optimizing a tangle of conveyor belts in Satisfactory (it's really becoming a problem), he's probably playing a 20-year-old Final Fantasy or some opaque ASCII roguelike. With a focus on writing and editing features, he seeks out personal stories and in-depth histories from the corners of PC gaming and its niche communities. 50% pizza by volume (deep dish, to be specific).

#3823. Taylor Swift Celebrates 20 Years Of 'Taylor Swift'

Forget for a moment the Taylor Swift we know now: The sold-out stadiums, the Hollywood friendships, and the Easter eggs scattered with extreme precision. Before she became a pop star capable of turning every album into a global event and every ex-boyfriend into a potential hit song, there was simply Taylor Swift the sixteen-year-old, with a cascade of blonde curls, an acoustic guitar, and a remarkable ability to recount her teenage dramas in a compelling, best-selling way.

Taylor Swift sings the National Anthem as the Detroit Lions host the Miami Dolphins on Thanksgiving Day, November 23, 2006.

On October 24, 2006, Swift dropped her first, eponymous album, Taylor Swift. Eleven country-pop songs behind a cover with a distinctly 2000s aesthetic, from a Pennsylvania girl with her sights set on conquering Nashville. Little did she know that, twenty years later, she would have conquered practically everything else.

People has retraced the album’s origins, unearthing anecdotes that seem straight out of a teen movie script. It all began in Wyomissing, Pennsylvania, where the future star grew up on a Christmas tree farm. Swift's childhood was tailor-made for a Hallmark movie—but the future pop star already had other plans. In 2004, with Swift was 14, the family moved to Tennessee to support her musical ambitions. Soon thereafter, she became the youngest songwriter to sign a contract with Sony/ATV Music Publishing.

Taylor Swift sings the National Anthem on opening day at Dodger Stadium on April 9, 2007.

There’s one detail that, looking back on it today, reveals a lot about the future queen of pop. Before releasing her album, the singer had secured a development deal with RCA Records, but when she realized that the path proposed by the label might mean more waiting and less creative freedom, she decided not to proceed. A bold choice for a young girl who hadn’t yet released an album. But even then, Swift knew exactly what she wanted: to sing her own songs, tell her own stories, and make her own decisions on what her voice should sound like.

A meeting with Scott Borchetta changed the course of her career. The music executive was preparing to launch Big Machine Records, and he immediately recognized Swift's potential. Swift was his new label's first signed artist, and the rest, as they say, is music history. But it’s also a great lesson in stubbornness applied to business.

Scott Borchetta and Taylor Swift on September 19, 2011.

Among the tracks on her debut album is “The Outside,” which Swift wrote when she was just 12 years old. The theme? Loneliness, feeling different, the sensation of watching the world from the outside while everyone else seems to know exactly where they’re going. This is where we catch a glimpse of one of the secrets to her success: Taylor Swift has never tried to recount extraordinary experiences, but rather ordinary ones—often painful and embarrassing—which she transforms into songs that millions of people find relatable. A formula that, twenty years later, continues to work. Loves, cities, and musical genres may change, but that girl who felt left out has never really stopped writing.

At first, the sales figures pointed to a promising artist, not a global phenomenon. Taylor Swift sold about 40,000 copies in the first week, but sales grew steadily thanks to country radio and word of mouth. The album remained at number one on Billboard’s Top Country Albums chart for 24 weeks. Five singles were certified platinum in the United States.

Taylor Swift accepts the award for Breakthrough Video of the Year for the song "Tim McGraw" at the 2007 CMT Music Awards on April 16, 2007.

Twenty years have passed since October 24, 2006. Since then, she's garnered fourteen Grammys, shifted her musical genre, gone on record-breaking tours, and fought a long battle for control of her own recordings. In 2026, Swift also became the youngest woman to be inducted into the Songwriters Hall of Fame. An honor that seems to bring things full circle for that stubborn little girl who dreamed of performing on stages around the world from her farm.

A tribute to the 20th anniversary has appeared on the singer’s official website, featuring confetti and photographs from her various eras. So while fans the world over celebrate her first album, they're also scrutinizing every detail for clues on the release of her next album. The singer has already revealed that she rerecorded her debut, and the date would certainly be auspicious for a big reveal.

As of publication time, however, no new release has been announced, leaving fans to quote Swift's 2010 song, “Sparks Fly.” “And I could wait patiently,” Swift sang on that track from Speak Now, “but I really wish you would drop everything now.”

Originally published by Vanity Fair Italy

#3822. Roblox users have made a playable version of Counter-Strike and full, working PlayStation games that you can play right now

Multiple remakes of beloved games including Counter Strike, The Simpsons: Hit and Run, and Super Hot, are available to play on Roblox right now. These have not been uploaded by the original game's creators.

One game - which is listed as an 'experience' in Roblox parlance, has been released by Roblox user Takeoff Interactive and listed as 'just a passion project', is a game "inspired by Counter-Strike" and has no monetisation according to its description. Eurogamer played this game today, and it looks and plays exactly like Counter-Strike.

The game includes the standard bomb diffusion mode, where two teams fight over two bomb sites. There are missing sound effects, the smoke doesn't look as good, and a friendly bot stabbed me in the back while diffusing a bomb, but otherwise it looks to be a largely identical recreation of Valve's game, albeit without Valve's involvement.

As for the PlayStation Experience, it's a full working PlayStation 4 dashboard with a media gallery, web browser, and trophies. There's even a server-based chat feature, in which Roblox players can talk to each other. The dashboard includes seven playable games - most of which are indie games. These include: The Simpsons Hit and Run, Cluster Truck, Superhot, Gang Beasts, Five Nights at Freddy's, Undertale, and PaRappaTheRapper.

Eurogamer played The Simpsons Hit and Run, and likewise found it to be a largely identical recreation of Activision's game. Players can drive around Springfield and cause mayhem much like the original game, albeit with a slightly dodgy objective guidance arrow.

Eurogamer also played Superhot, which once again looks and plays much like the original game, though it's not quite as visually impressive. This version of Superhot, Simpsons Hit and Run, and the PlayStation Experience as a whole was uploaded to Roblox by user Redwing Works, not Activision, not the Superhot team, and not Sony.

It is worth noting these are not Roblox games that are merely inspired by popular releases outside its ecosystem. Previously, a few popular Roblox games inspired by the game but not openly copying it exist. Counter Blox, for example, is a tactical FPS which uses Roblox assets and models, while Arsenal is another FPS inspired by Counter-Strike's Gun Game mode in which each kill progresses you to different weapons.

This is not the first time a popular game has been recreated on Roblox without the permission of its creators. The Peak developers urged people to pirate their game rather than play a "microtransaction-riddled ripoff slop" on Roblox.

Eurogamer has contacted Sony and developers for comment.

#3821. Check Out 46 Seconds Of New Final Fantasy 7 Revelation Gameplay (And A Pink Chocobo)

Final Fantasy 7 Revelation aims to be the biggest chapter in the remake trilogy, not only in the number of regions players can explore, but also in verticality. Square Enix had previously shown off how the game's cast will be able to skydive into new locations on the world map, but for a taste of just how much verticality the game will have in certain areas, you can check out this new slice of gameplay set in the Midgar region, which features Cloud exploring a massive chasm underneath the city--and atop a pink chocobo, too.

While that's a lot of empty space to fly through, it's still impressive just how much of it there is. Of course, this is only one part of the game, and there's no guarantee every region will have caverns this massive. But considering how Final Fantasy 7 Rebirth greatly expanded on the environments of Final Fantasy 7 Remake with its open-world design, Final Fantasy 7 Revelation is expected to raise the bar by increasing the size of the world around Cloud and the rest of the gang. Revelation will also introduce Cid's iconic airship, the Highwind, which will serve as the gang's base of operations, and unfortunately, Chadley is there too.

All that "planetary-scale" content will take up a lot of space on your PC or console, with game director Naoki Hamaguchi estimating the game will be "around" 200GB.

One of the big games to look forward to next year, Final Fantasy 7 Revelation will launch on April 8, 2027. It's estimated that a complete playthrough could take as long as 100 hours--something Hamaguchi had done 46 times already when last we spoke to him--and two post-launch expansions starring Vincent Valentine and Sephiroth will follow in the game's wake.

"My time with the final chapter of the Final Fantasy 7 remake trilogy clearly shows that the team at Square Enix has plenty of story left to tell. We’ll just have to wait until April for the rest of the Revelation(s)," Jason Fanelli wrote after going hands-on with Final Fantasy 7 Revelation earlier this month.
